m(R_{k}\cap B_{k,l})\leq(2^{-k-1}r)^{1-n}\mu(B(z_{k,l},2^{-k+3}r))\,|B_{k,l}|\leq C2^{-k}r\mu(B(z_{k,l},2^{-k+3}r)).

For each
k
, the
B_{k,l}
have bounded overlap, so

m(R_{k})\leq\sum_{l}m(R_{k}\cap B_{k,l})\leq C2^{-k}r\,\sum\mu(B(z_{k,l},2^{-k+3}r))\leq C2^{-k}r\,\mu(\Gamma\cap B(x,12r)).

We sum over
k\geq 0
and get the upper bound in (
3.14
).
For the lower bound, we use (
H1
) to select a corkscrew ball

B\subset\Omega\cap B(x,r)
. Observe that
w(X)\geq C^{-1}r^{1-n}\mu(B(x,r))

for
X\in B
(because
\mu
is doubling), so
m(B(x,r))\geq m(B)\geq C^{-1}r\mu(B(x,r))
,
which completes the proof (
3.14
).

It follows from (
3.14
) that
m
is locally finite. The intertwining property (
H5
)
follows at once from (
3.14
), which says that
\rho(x,r)\approx 1
, and (
H6
)
holds because of (
H6’
), by (
3.13
) and because
\mu
is doubling. We are left with the
doubling property (
H4
) for
m
.

So let
X\in\Omega
be given, and choose
x\in\Gamma
such that
|X-x|=\delta(X)
.
For
R<\delta(X)/2
,
m(B(X,R))\approx\delta(X)\mu(B(X,2\delta(X)))\approx\delta(X)\mu(B(x,\delta(X))
because
\mu
is doubling.
For
R
larger than
2\delta(X)
,
B(x,R/2)\subset B(X,R)\subset B(x,3R)
, so

m(B(X,R))\approx m(B(x,R)\approx R\mu(B(x,R))
by (
3.14
).
The doubling property follows easily.

So in this setting too, our assumptions hold and the rest of the paper will show that
there is a well behaved elliptic measure associated to each operator
L=\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A\nabla

such that
w(x)^{-1}A
satisfies the usual boundedness and ellipticity properties.

4.
The definition of the space
W

We want to define the space
W
as the space of functions
u
such that
\nabla u
is in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
, and we wish to prove that this space is complete;
more precisely that the quotient of
W
by constants,
equipped with the quotient of the semi-norm
\|\nabla\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega,m)}
,
is complete.

However,

W
is not entirely defined by the fact that
\nabla u
is in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
, because we don’t explain where the functions
u
are taken from. The first natural space where we could
take
u
from is
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
,
but in this case
recall that we do not assume enough regularity on
w
to make sure that

u\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,dx)
, and then maybe
u
does not define a distribution and
we do not know the meaning of
\nabla u
.
The second choice would be to take
u
is the space of distributions, or in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,dx)
, but nothing guarantees that the quotient of the constructed space by constants will be complete.

To solve this problem, we use the strategy from
[
HKM
]
, which consists in completing the smooth functions with respect to an appropriate norm. Our spaces shall be homogeneous,
while the ones in
[
HKM
]
are inhomogeneous.
Homogeneous spaces are slightly
trickier, because we need to
quotient by constant functions
to get a Hilbert space.

Definition 4.1
.

A function
u
belongs to
W
if
u\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
and there exists a vector valued function
v\in L^{2}(\Omega,m)
such that for some sequence
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
, one has

(4.2)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,dm<+\infty\qquad\text{ for any $i\in\mathbb{N}$,}

(4.3)

\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|\,dm=0\qquad\text{ for any ball $B$ satisfying $2B\subset\Omega$}

and

(4.4)

\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi_{i}-v|^{2}\,dm=0.

Observe that if
u\in W
, then, assuming the first part of (
H6
), the vector
v

from the definition is unique. In the rest of the article, if
u\in W
, we use the notation

\nabla u
(or
\nabla_{W}u
when the notion of derivative we are talking is not obvious) for the
unique vector valued function
v
given by Definition
4.1
. In particular, we can equip
W
with the semi-norm

\|u\|_{W}:=\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega,m)}\qquad\text{ for $u\in W$}.

We want to highlight that
\nabla\cdot
is a linear operator, but is not (necessarily) the gradient in the sense of distribution. An example where the two notions of derivative are different is given page 13 of
[
HKM
]
.

Let us recall now some cases where the two notions of derivative actually coincide. First, if
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,dx)=L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,dm)
- which is the case for instance under the assumption (
H6’
) - then (
4.3
) implies the convergence of the
\varphi_{i}
to
u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,dx)
, which in turn implies the convergence
\varphi_{i}\to u
in the sense of distribution. So the only possible limit of
\nabla\varphi_{i}
is
\nabla u
, where
\nabla u
is the derivative taken in the sense of distributions.

Let us present another case, given on
page 14 of
[
HKM
]
. Let the measure
m
be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, so that there exists a weight
w
satisfying
dm(x)=w(x)dx
.
Assume in addition that

w
belongs to the Muckenhoupt class

\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Then
the measure
m
satisfies (
H4
) and
\nabla_{W}u
is
the distribution gradient of
u
in
\Omega
.
See Subsection
3.6
for a bit more information.

In general,
Lemma 1.11 in
[
HKM
]
shows that

(4.5)

if

u
is compactly supported and Lipschitz, then

u\in W
and
\nabla_{W}u
is the usual gradient.

The proof of (
4.5
) uses the fact that
m
is absolutely continuous
with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

We now show
that the Poincaré inequality given as (
H6
) extends to all functions in
W
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(\Omega,m)
satisfy (
H6
). Then for any ball
B
such that

2B\subset\Omega
and any
u\in W
, one has

\fint_{B}|u-u_{B}|\,dm\leq C_{6}r\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
u_{B}
stands for
\fint_{B}u\,dm
and
r
is the radius of
B
.

Proof
.  By definition of
W
, we can find a sequence of functions
\varphi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}\fint_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|\,dm=0

and

\lim_{i\to\infty}\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla\varphi_{i}-\nabla u|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=0.

Let
\varphi_{i,B}
stand for
\fint_{B}\varphi_{i}\,dm
; then for
i\geq 0

\begin{split}\fint_{B}|u-u_{B}|\,dm&\leq\fint_{B}|\varphi_{i}-\varphi_{i,B}|\,dm+\fint_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|\,dm+|\varphi_{i,B}-u_{B}|\\
&\leq C_{6}r\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+2\fint_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|\,dm\\
&\leq C_{6}r\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{6}r\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla\varphi_{i}-\nabla u|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+2\fint_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|\,dm,\end{split}

where we use (
H6
) in the second inequality. Taking the limit as
i\to\infty
gives the desired result.

\Box

We have the following nice improvement of Lemma
4.6
by
Keith and Zhong
[
KZ
]
, where it is enough to control
|\nabla u|
in some
L^{p}
norm,
p<2
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(\Omega,m)
satisfy (
H4
) and (
H6
).
There exists
p_{0}\in[1,2)
such that for any
p\in[p_{0},2]
, any ball
B
satisfying

2B\subset\Omega
, and any
u\in W
,

(4.8)

\fint_{B}|u-u_{B}|\,dm\leq Cr\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
u_{B}
stands for
\fint_{B}u\,dm
and

r
is the radius of
B
. The parameter
p_{0}
and the constant
C
depends only on
C_{4}
and
C_{6}
.

Remark 4.9
.

If (
4.8
) is true for some
p_{0}<2
, then it holds
for all
p\in[p_{0},2]
,
by Jensen’s inequality.

Proof
.  The Poincaré inequality (
2.9
) holds for all locally Lipschitz functions according to Lemma
4.6
, (
4.5
), and the fact that (
2.9
) is a local property.
We deduce that our metric measured spaces
(B,|.|,m)
are doubling spaces that admit a
(1,2)
-Poincaré inequality in the sense of
[
KZ
]
, and the doubling constant and the Poincaré constant are uniform on the balls
B
.
Theorem 1.0.1 in
[
KZ
]
applies, so we have the existence of
p\in(1,2)
and
C>0
independent of
B
such that our spaces
(B,|.|,m)
admit a
(1,p)
-Poincaré inequality with constant
C
, which means in the terminology of
[
KZ
]
that (
4.8
) holds for any locally Lipschitz function.
The proof of (
4.8
) for all
W
then follows
from the same density argument as
in the proof of Lemma
4.6
.

\Box

We end the section with a simple but useful lemma.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(\Omega,m)
satisfy (
H1
), (
H2
), and (
H6
).
Take
u\in W
. Then

\|u\|_{W}=0

if and only if
u
is
m
-almost everywhere equal to a constant function.

Proof
.  First, constant functions are in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
. So if
u
matches a constant function
c
except maybe on a set of
m
-measure
0
, we can take
v=0
and
\varphi_{i}=c
in the Definition
4.1
. By the uniqueness of
v=\nabla u
, we deduce that

\nabla u=0
.

Conversely, let
u\in W
be such that
\|u\|_{W}=0
.
By Lemma
4.6
, for any ball
B
such that

2B\subset\Omega
, we have that
\int_{B}|u-u_{B}|\,dm=0
, which implies immediately that
u\equiv u_{B}

m
-a.e. on
B
. Yet,
\Omega
is connected (and can even be connected by a chain of balls
\{B_{i}\}_{i}
satisfying
2B_{i}\subset\Omega
,
thanks to Proposition
2.18
), so
u
is
m
-a.e. equal to a constant function.

\Box

5.
The access
cones and their properties

In all this section, we assume that
\Omega
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H2
) and that the measures
\mu
and
m
satisfy
(
H3
) and (
H4
).
We also choose to take
\Gamma
(and thus
\Omega
) to be infinite. This assumption is not part of (
H1
)–(
H4
), and is not even necessary for our proofs to work. The proofs of the bounded and unbounded cases only differ slightly, but will require us to separate cases. We will present the infinite case - which we plan to use in future articles and which we believe is less common - and we shall discuss the differences in Section
13
.
We first describe the dyadic decomposition of
(\Gamma,\mu)
of M.
Christ (see
[
Ch
, Theorem 11]
).

Proposition 5.1
.

There exists a collection of measurable
subsets - we call them cubes by comparison with the Euclidean case -
\{Q_{j}^{k},\,k\in\mathbb{Z},\,j\in\mathcal{J}_{k}\}
, and some constants
\eta
,
a_{0}
,
C
- all of them depending only on
C_{3}
- such that

(i)

\Gamma=\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{k}}Q^{k}_{j}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

If
\ell\geq k
, then either
Q_{i}^{\ell}\subset Q^{k}_{j}
or
Q_{i}^{\ell}\cap Q^{k}_{j}=\emptyset
.

(iii)

For each pair
(k,j)
and each
\ell<k
, there exists a unique
i
such that
Q^{k}_{j}\subset Q^{\ell}_{i}
.

(iv)

\diam\,Q^{k}_{j}\leq 2^{-k}
.

(v)

Q^{k}_{j}
contains some surface ball
B(z^{k}_{j},a_{0}2^{-k})\cap\Gamma
.

(vi)

\mu(\{x\in Q^{k}_{j},\,\,\mathrm{dist}(x,\Gamma\setminus Q^{k}_{j})\leq\rho 2^{-k}\})\leq C\rho^{\eta}\mu(Q^{k}_{j})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

j\in\mathcal{J}_{k}
, and some constant
\rho>0
.

We shall denote by
\mathbb{D}_{k}
the collection

\mathbb{D}_{k}:=\{Q^{k}_{j},\,j\in\mathcal{J}_{k}\}

and by
\mathbb{D}
the collection

\mathbb{D}:=\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{D}_{k}.

Remark 5.2
.

An element of
\mathbb{D}
is given by a subset
Q
of
\Gamma
and a generation
k
. Indeed, if we only know the set
Q
, contrary to dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we cannot be sure of the generation.

Despite the above comment,
we shall abuse notation and use
the term
Q
for both an element of
\mathbb{D}
and the corresponding subset of
\Gamma
. We write
k(Q)
when we want to refer to the “dyadic generation” of the cube
Q\in\mathbb{D}
, that is the
only
integer
k
such that
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}
. The length of a dyadic cube is
\ell(Q)=2^{-k(Q)}
.

The conclusion
(vi)
will not be used in this article, but we wanted to state the complete result of Christ nevertheless. Moreover, properties
(iv)
and
(v)
of the decomposition implies the existence of
z_{Q}\in\Gamma
such that

(5.3)

B(z_{Q},r_{Q})\cap\Gamma\subset Q\subset B(z_{Q},R_{Q}),\quad\text{with $r_{Q}=a_{0}\ell(Q)$ and $R_{Q}=\ell(Q)$.}

When
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and
\lambda\geq 1
, we also use the notation
\lambda Q
for the set
\{x\in\Gamma:\,\,\mathrm{dist}(x,Q)\leq(\lambda-1)\ell(Q)\}
.
As a consequence, if
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are from the same generation, i.e.,

k(Q)=k(Q^{\prime})
, and
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are adjacent, i.e.,

\partial Q\cap\partial Q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then
Q^{\prime}\subset 2Q
.

Also, as in the first pages of
[
Ste
]
, we can define a Whitney decomposition of
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
made by (true) dyadic cubes. To do this, take a dyadic decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by cubes
I
, ordered by inclusion, and we define
\mathcal{W}
as a the set of dyadic cubes
I\subset\Omega
for which
4\,\diam\,I\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(4I,\Gamma)
but the parent
I^{\prime}
of
I
- that is the only dyadic cube
I^{\prime}\supset I
satisfying
\ell(I^{\prime})=2\ell(I)
- doesn’t satisfy
4\,\diam\,I^{\prime}\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(4I^{\prime},\Gamma)
. It is easy to check that
\mathcal{W}
is a non-overlapping covering of
\Omega
, that for
I\in\mathcal{W}

(5.4)

4\,\diam\,I\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(4I,\Gamma)\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(I,\Gamma)\leq 12\diam(I)

and if
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{W}
are two adjacent cubes

(5.5)

\frac{\diam\,I_{1}}{\diam\,I_{2}}\in\Big\{\frac{1}{2},1,2\Big\}.

Let us write
X_{I}
for the center of
I\in\mathcal{W}
,
\ell(I)
for its side length
(thus
\ell(I)\approx\diam\,I
), and
k(I)
for the integer
k
that satisfies
\ell(I)=2^{-k}
.

Now, let us match the dyadic decomposition
\mathbb{D}
of
\Gamma
with
the Whitney decomposition
\mathcal{W}
of
\Omega
.
For each
Q\in\mathbb{D}
, we define
\mathcal{W}_{Q}
as

(5.6)

\mathcal{W}_{Q}:=\{I\in\mathcal{W},\,C_{a}^{-1}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(I)\text{ and }\,\mathrm{dist}(I,Q)\leq 2\ell(Q)\},

where
C_{a}^{-1}=C(C_{1},n)>1
is chosen
in the following next lines.
Set
X_{Q}
as a Corkscrew point associated to a point
x_{Q}\in 2Q
and a distance
\ell(Q)
,
that is
X_{Q}\in B(x_{Q},\ell(Q))
and
B(X_{Q},\ell(Q)/C_{1})\subset\Omega
.
The point
X_{Q}
belongs to some
I_{Q}\in\mathcal{W}
. Observe that

\,\mathrm{dist}(I_{Q},Q)\leq|X_{Q}-x_{Q}|+\,\mathrm{dist}(x_{Q},Q)\leq 2\ell(Q)

and

\ell(I_{Q})\geq\frac{1}{16\sqrt{n}}\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{Q},\Gamma)\geq\frac{1}{16C_{1}\sqrt{n}}\ell(Q);

we can pick
the constant
C_{a}
in (
5.6
) as for instance
1000C_{1}\sqrt{n}
, so that
I_{Q}\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}
. But the choice of

C_{a}
doesn’t really matter (as long as it is big enough);
we can choose it as an additional parameter and make the future results depend on
C_{a}
too.
Now define
the associated Whitney region

(5.7)

U_{Q}:=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}}I,

which contains by construction of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Q}
all the Corkscrew points associated
to a point
x\in 2Q
and the
distance
\ell(Q)
. We also define, for each
x\in\Gamma
, the
“dyadic access” cone

(5.8)

\gamma(x):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathbb{D}:\,Q\ni x}U_{Q}.

We also need cones with a “larger aperture”. We consider
the collection
\mathcal{W}_{Q}^{0}
of dyadic
cubes that meet
B(X,\delta(X)/2)
for some
X\in U_{Q}\cup U_{Q^{\prime}}
, where
Q^{\prime}
is the parent of
Q
.
Thus, when
I\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}^{0}
,

\delta(X_{I})\approx\ell(Q)
with constants that depends only on
C_{a}
(i.e.,
n
and
C_{1}
), so each couple of centers
X_{I},X_{I^{\prime}}
,
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}^{0}
, can be linked by a Harnack chain (see Proposition
2.18
). We define

\mathcal{W}_{Q}^{*}
as the collection of cubes in
{\mathcal{W}}
that meet at least one of those Harnack chains
from
(
2.14
), and finally define

(5.9)

U_{Q}^{*}:=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{W}_{Q}^{*}}I

and, for
x\in\Gamma
, the cone

(5.10)

\gamma^{*}(x):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathbb{D}:\,Q\ni x}U^{*}_{Q}.

We shall also need the truncated cone

(5.11)

\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x):=\bigcup_{Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}:\,x\in Q^{\prime}\atop\ell(Q^{\prime})\leq\ell(Q)}U^{*}_{Q^{\prime}},

and the “tent sets”

(5.12)

T_{Q}:=\bigcup_{x\in Q}\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)\quad\text{ and }\quad T_{2Q}:=\bigcup_{x\in 2Q}\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x).

The following standard properties of the sets above are easy to check.
The cones
\gamma(x),\gamma^{*}(x)
are such that
\gamma(x)\subset\gamma^{*}(x)
and

(5.13)

\delta(X)>c|X-x|\quad\text{ for }X\in\gamma^{*}(x).

The Whitney regions
U_{Q}
and
U^{*}_{Q}
are such that
U_{Q}\subset U^{*}_{Q}
and

(5.14)

\ell(Q)\lesssim\,\mathrm{dist}(U^{*}_{Q},Q)\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(U_{Q},Q)\lesssim\diam\,U_{Q}\leq\diam\,U^{*}_{Q}\lesssim\ell(Q),

where the constants depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
.
The tent sets
T_{Q}
and
T_{2Q}
satisfy

(5.15)

B(z_{Q},r^{\prime}_{Q})\cap\Omega\subset T_{Q}\subset T_{2Q}\subset B(z_{Q},R^{\prime}_{Q}),

where
z_{Q}
is as
in (
5.3
), and
r^{\prime}_{Q},R^{\prime}_{Q}\approx\ell(Q)
.
Indeed, the second inclusion is easy;
for the first one, observe that if
Z\in B(z_{Q},r^{\prime}_{Q})
with
r^{\prime}_{Q}
small enough,
then any point in
\Gamma
such that

|z-Z|=\delta(Z)
lies in
B(z_{Q},r_{Q})
,
where
r_{Q}=a_{0}\ell(Q)
as
in (
5.3
).
The point
Z
is a Corkscrew point for
z
, so
Z\in\gamma(z)
, and as long as
r^{\prime}_{Q}
is small enough, it is also in
\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)\subset T_{Q}
. The measure of the various sets that we just introduced
are given by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and
x\in Q
.
Then

(i)

\mu(Q)\approx\mu(B(x,\ell(Q)))
,

(ii)

m(U_{Q})\approx m(U^{*}_{Q})\approx m(B(x,\ell(Q))\cap\Omega)
,

(iii)

\rho(x,\ell(Q))\approx\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q})}{\mu(Q)\ell(Q)}
.

In (i), the constants depends only on
C_{3}
, and in (ii) and (iii), the constants depend also
on
n
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{4}
.

In particular, we can define
\rho(Q)
as

(5.17)

\rho(Q):=\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q})}{\mu(Q)\ell(Q)},

and if (
H5
) if satisfied, we have

(5.18)

\frac{\rho(Q^{*})}{\rho(Q)}\leq C\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{1-\epsilon},

where
C>0
depends on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{5}
.

Proof
.  Let us prove (i). By (
5.3
) and (
H3
),

\mu(Q)\leq\mu(B(z_{Q},R_{Q}))\leq\mu(B(x,2R_{Q}))\lesssim\mu(B(x,\ell(Q)))

and

\mu(B(x,\ell(Q))\leq\mu(B(z_{Q},2R_{Q}))\lesssim\mu(B(z_{Q},r_{Q}))\leq\mu(Q).

The assertion (i) follows. As for (ii), since
U_{Q},U_{Q}^{*}
are Whitney regions associated to
Q
, (
5.14
) shows that we can find
K>1
and
X\in U_{Q}
such that

B(X,K^{-1}\ell(Q))\subset U_{Q}\subset U^{*}_{Q}\subset B(x,K\ell(Q))\cap\Omega\subset B(X,K^{2}\ell(Q)).

The assertion (ii) is now an immediate consequence of (
H4
), the doubling measure property for
m
. The conclusion (iii) is no difficulty from (i) and (ii).

\Box

One can also easily check that the number of dyadic cubes in
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{Q}
is uniformly bounded. Indeed,
the cubes in
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{Q}
are pairwise disjoint, and their diameters
are all equivalent to the diameter of
U^{*}_{Q}
- which is their union. One can also easily check that
U^{*}_{Q}
is connected (by construction, we linked the points in
U_{Q}\cup U_{Q^{\prime}}
by Harnack chains). So since
W^{*}_{Q}
is only constituted of dyadic cubes, for any couple
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
, we can find a sequence of cubes in
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
linking
I
to
I^{\prime}
, where two consecutive cubes are adjacent; the sequence has uniformly bounded length because there is a bounded number
of cubes in
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
. We summarize these conclusions in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.19
.

There exists
N_{0}:=N_{0}(n,C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and

I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
, we can find a collection
\{I_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq N_{0}}
of cubes in
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
such that

(i)

I_{0}=I
,
I_{N_{0}}=I^{\prime}
,

(ii)

for any
i\in\{1,\dots,N_{0}\}
,
I_{i-1}
and
I_{i}
are adjacent or equal.

As a corollary, we get the following result with balls instead of cubes.

Lemma 5.20
.

There exists
N_{0}:=N_{0}(n,C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and for
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
, we can find a collection
\{B_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq N_{0}}
of balls such that

(i)

2B_{i}\subset\Omega
and
B_{i}\subset U^{*}_{Q}
,

(ii)

B_{0}
is
B(X_{I},\ell(I)/2)
and
B_{N_{0}}
is
B(X_{I^{\prime}},\ell(I^{\prime})/2)
,

(iii)

for any
i\in\{1,\dots,N_{0}\}
, we have
r_{i}\approx\ell(I)
, where
r_{i}
is the radius of
B_{i}
,

(iv)

for any
i\in\{1,\dots,N_{0}-1\}
, one has
|X_{i+1}-X_{i}|\leq r_{i}
, where
X_{i}
is the center of
B_{i}
.

Proof
.  We construct the sequence of balls
\{B_{i}\}
from the sequence of dyadic cubes
\{I_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq N_{0}}
as follows. We replace each cube
I_{i}
,
i<N_{0}
, by
n+2
balls
\{B^{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq n+1}
, according to the following procedure:

•

If
I_{i+1}
is smaller than
I_{i}
, then since
I_{i}
and
I_{i+1}
are adjacent,
hence
\ell(I_{i})=2\ell(I_{i+1})
by (
5.5
).
So up to translation, rotation, and dilatation,
I_{i}
is the cube
[0,4]^{n}
and
I_{i+1}
is the cube
[-2,0]\times[0,2]^{n-1}
. In this case, we take
B^{0}
as the ball with center at
(2,\dots,2)
- the center of
I_{i}
- and radius
2
, the balls
B^{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, are centered on

(\underbrace{2,\dots,2}_{n-j},\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{j})

and are of radius 1, the ball
B^{n+1}
is centered on
(0,1,\dots,1)
and again of radius 1.

•

If
I_{i+1}
has the same size of
I_{i}
, yet is different from
I_{i}
, then up to rotation,
translation and dilatation,
I_{i}=[0,4]^{n}

and
I_{i+1}=[-4,0]\times[0,4]^{n-1}
. The

B^{j}
have the same
radius
2
,
B^{0}
is the ball centered on
X_{I_{i}}=(2,\dots,2)
, and all
the other balls
B^{j}
are equal and centered on
(0,2,\dots,2)
.

•

If
I_{i+1}
is bigger than
I_{i}
, then as
before we necessary have
2\ell(I_{i})=\ell(I_{i+1})
. So up to translation, rotation, and dilatation,

I_{i}=[-2,0]\times[0,2]^{n-1}
and
I_{i+1}=[0,4]^{n}
.
All the balls have but the last one have radius
1
and
B^{n+1}
has radius 2;
B^{0}
is centered on
(-1,1\dots,1)
,
B^{1}
is centered on
(0,1,\dots,1)
, and for
2\leq j\leq n+1
,

B_{j}
is centered on

(\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{n+2-j},\underbrace{2,\dots,2}_{j-1}).

•

If
I_{i+1}=I_{i}
, then
B^{j}
is
always the same ball
B(X_{I_{i}},\ell(I_{i})/2)
.

We replace
I_{N_{0}}
by the ball
B(X_{I_{N_{0}}},\ell(I_{N_{0}})/2)
.

The balls that we constructed satisfy (i), because first

B^{j}\subset I_{i}\cup I_{i+1}

and second, the Whitney cubes
I_{i}
satisfy (
5.4
), which ensures that

2B^{j}\subset\Omega
;
(ii) and (iv) are not hard to check by construction, (iii) comes from the fact that all
I_{i}
have similar radius (equivalent to the diameter of
U^{*}_{Q}
). The lemma follows.

\Box

We shall
use the last lemma to prove quantitative connectedness on the sets
U^{*}_{Q}
,
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
,
and
T_{Q}
. We start with a definition.

Definition 5.21
.

We say that a (bounded)
set
D\subset\Omega
satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
, where
\kappa\in[1/2,1)
, if there exists a distinguished ball
B_{0}\subset D
such that for every
x\in D
, there exists an infinite sequence of balls
B_{0},B_{1},\dots
(called chain) with the following properties:

(i)

for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
B_{i}\subset D
and
2B_{i}\subset\Omega
;

(ii)

for
i\geq 0
,
x\in MB_{i}
;

(iii)

for
i\geq 0
, one has

M^{-1}(\diam\,D)\kappa^{i}\leq r_{i}\leq M(\diam\,D)\kappa^{i},

where
r_{i}
is the radius of
B_{i}
;

(iv)

for
i\geq 0
, if
X_{j}
denotes the center of
B_{j}
, we have
|X_{i+1}-X_{i}|\leq r_{i}

Remark 5.22
.

The definition above is shamelessly inspired by the
C(\lambda,M)
condition in
[
HaK2
]
.
Notice
that
\kappa
in our condition doesn’t correspond to
\lambda
in the chain condition of
[
HaK2
]
. Indeed,
\kappa
is fixed equal to 1/2 in
[
HaK2
]
, while the
\lambda
in
[
HaK2
]

doesn’t really have an equivalent in our condition. However, these technicalities don’t really
change the core the proofs.

Lemma 5.23
.

For every

\kappa\in[1-n^{-1/2},1)
, there exists
M:=M(\kappa,n,C_{1},C_{2})
such that each Whitney cube
I\in{\mathcal{W}}
, and each set
U^{*}_{Q}
,
Q\in\mathbb{D}
, satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
.

There exists
\kappa\in[1/2,1)
and
M\geq 1
- both depending only on
n
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{4}
- such that for any
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and any
x\in 2Q
, the sets
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
,

T_{Q}
, and
T_{2Q}
, satisfy the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
.

Proof
.  We start with an (open) Whitney cube
I\in{\mathcal{W}}
. Take
\kappa\in[1-n^{-1/2},1)
. We choose the distinguish ball associated to
I
as
B_{0}:=B(X_{I},\ell(I)/2)
. Then we take
X\in I
and we construct the chain of balls
\{B_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
as follows. For
i\geq 1
, the ball
B_{i}
has radius
r_{i}=\kappa^{i}\ell(I)/2
and its center
X_{i}
is the closest point to
X
on the segment
[X_{I},X]
which satisfies
|X_{i}-X_{i-1}|\leq r_{i-1}
and
\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\partial I)\leq r_{i}
. If
M=\sqrt{n}
, the points (iii) and (iv) of Definition
5.21
are true by construction, as well as the fact that
B_{i}\subset I
. The condition
2B_{i}\subset\Omega
is true because we have
B_{i}\subset I
and (
5.4
). The condition (ii) of Definition
5.21
holds because we chose
\kappa
large enough to ensure that we can get (at least infinitely close) to
X
at some point.

Now let
\kappa\in[1-n^{-1/2},1)
and
Q\in\mathbb{D}
be given.
We want to prove that the sets
U^{*}_{Q}

satisfy the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
for some
M
. We choose
I_{0}
as any dyadic cube in

\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q}
(the choice is not important here), and then we choose the distinguished
ball
B_{0}
as
B(X_{I_{0}},\ell(I_{0})/2)
.
Take then
X\in U^{*}_{Q}
. There exists
I\in{\mathcal{W}}^{*}_{Q}
such that
X\in I
. The balls
B_{i}
are constructed as follows:
\{B_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq N_{0}}
is the collection of balls linking the center of
I_{0}
to the center of
I
given by Lemma
5.20
, and the balls
\{B_{i}\}_{i>N_{0}}
are the chain associated to the cube
I
and the point
x
that we constructed above. We can
check that the chain satisfies all the conditions
of Definition
5.21
when
M
is large enough.

We turn to the proof of the chain condition for the sets
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
.
For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
Q_{j}
as the dyadic cube in
\mathbb{D}_{j+k(Q)}
that contains
x
.
Choose for
X_{j}

a Corkscrew point associated to
x
and
\ell(Q_{j})=2^{-j}\ell(Q)
.
By construction of
\mathcal{W}_{Q_{j}}
, we can find a dyadic cube
I^{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{Q_{j}}
that contains
X_{j}
. We construct the chain
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}
as follows:
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{jN_{0}\leq i\leq(j+1)N_{0}}
is the collection linking the center of
I^{j}
to the center of
I^{j+1}
given by Lemma
5.20
(recall that both
I^{j}
and
I^{j+1}
are in
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q_{j}}
).

Now let us take
X\in\gamma^{*}_{Q}
. By construction,
X
lies
in
U^{*}_{Q_{j(X)}}
for some
j(X)\in\mathbb{N}
. We construct the chain
\{B_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
as follows:
if
i\leq jN_{0}
, then
B_{i}=\mathcal{B}_{i}
; and then
the chain
\{B_{i}\}_{i\geq jN_{0}}
is the one used to prove that
U_{Q_{j}}^{*}
satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
with
\kappa=2^{-1/N_{0}}
.

At last, we shall prove that
T_{Q}
and
T_{2Q}
satisfy the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
for
\kappa:=2^{-1/N_{0}}
and for some
M
independent of
Q
. We only prove it for

T_{2Q}
, since
T_{Q}
is very similar. It is actually an easy consequence of the chain condition
of
\gamma_{Q}^{*}(x)
and of
U_{Q}^{*}
. Indeed, we chose the distinguish ball
B_{0}^{x}

of
\gamma_{Q}^{*}(x)
as a ball centered on a dyadic cube
I_{0}^{x}
containing a Corkscrew point
associated to
(x,\ell(Q))
. However, by construction of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Q}
, all the

B_{0}^{x}\subset I_{0}^{x}
are subsets of the same
U_{Q}\subset U^{*}_{Q}
.
So we take any
I_{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{Q}^{*}
, we chose
B_{0}:=B(X_{I_{0}},\ell(I_{0})/2)
as the distinguish ball. Take then
X\in T_{2Q}
, and pick
x\in 2Q
so that
X\in\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
. We construct the chain between the distinguish cube
B_{0}
and
X
as the concatenation of the chain (of finite length) linking
B_{0}
to
B_{0}^{x}
given by Lemma
5.19
and the one linking
B_{0}^{x}
to
X
given by the fact that
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
satisfies the
C(\kappa,M)
chain condition.
The lemma follows.

\Box

We may now
extend the Poincaré inequality given in (
H6
) to domains that are not balls.

Theorem 5.24
.

Assume that
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H4
) and (
H6
).
Let
p_{0}\in(1,2)
be as in Lemma
4.7
, and take
p\in[p_{0},2]
.

Let
M>1
and
\kappa\in(1/2,1)
. Assume that
D\subset\Omega
satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
. Then there exists
k>1
,
that depends only on
C_{4}
,
such that, for any
u\in W
,

(5.25)

\left(\fint_{D}|u-\bar{u}|^{pk}\,dm\right)^{1/pk}\leq C\diam(D)\left(\fint_{D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{p},

where
\bar{u}
is the average of
u
on any set
E\subset D
satisfying
m(E)\geq cm(D)
, and where
C>0
depends only on
\kappa
,
M
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
, and
c
.

In particular, for any cube
Q\in\mathbb{D}
and any
x\in 2Q
,
(
5.25
) holds for
D=U^{*}_{Q}
,

\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
,
T_{Q}
, or
T_{2Q}
, and the constant
C
depends now (only) on
n
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
, and
c
.

Remark 5.26
.

The theorem gives in particular that any function
u\in W
lies
in
L^{1}(D)
, where
D
is any
domain that satisfies the
C(\kappa,M)
condition for some
\kappa
and
M
. In particular
D
can stand for
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(x)
,
T_{Q}
, or
T_{2Q}
, despite the fact that none of these domains are relatively compact in
\Omega
.

Remark 5.27
.

We can apply the theorem when
D=2B
, where
B
is a ball such that
2B\subset\Omega
,
and
u\in W
vanishes a.e. on
2B\setminus B
; then we can take
E=2B\setminus B
and (
5.25
) becomes

(5.28)

\left(\fint_{B}|u|^{pk}\,dm\right)^{1/pk}\leq C\diam(B)\left(\fint_{B}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{p},

because
u=|\nabla u|=0
a.e. on
D\setminus B=2B\setminus B
anyway.

Proof
.  Let us not lie, our proof is the one of
[
HaK2
]
with very small
modifications. But we write it for completeness (and since it is quite short and fun). Also, in all the proof, if
S\subset D
, then
u_{S}
denotes
\fint_{S}u\,dm
.

Let
B_{0}\subset D
be the distinguished ball given by the
C(\kappa,M)
condition.
Also
write
r
for the diameter of
D
.
From (ii) of Definition
5.21
, the radius
r_{0}
of
B_{0}
is equivalent to
r
, so we deduce from
(
H4
) that
m(I_{0})\approx m(D)
. As a consequence,

\begin{split}\left(\fint_{D}|u-\bar{u}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}&\leq\left(\fint_{D}|u-u_{B_{0}}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}+|\bar{u}-u_{B_{0}}|\\
&\leq\left(\fint_{D}|u-u_{B_{0}}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}+\fint_{E}|u-u_{B_{0}}|\,dm\\
&\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{m(D)}\int_{D}|u-u_{B_{0}}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\end{split}

by the Hölder inequality and the fact that
m(E)\approx m(D)
.

So it is enough to prove the theorem when
\bar{u}=u_{B_{0}}
. Besides, without loss of generality, we can assume that
u_{B_{0}}=0
. Our goal is to establish a weak-type
L^{q}-L^{p}
estimate for
q>p
that will be improved into a strong
L^{q^{\prime}}-L^{p}
estimate for
q^{\prime}\in(p,q)
by a standard argument.

Let
Z\in A_{t}:=\{|u|>t\}
be a Lebesgue point for

u
, i.e., a point
Z
such that

\lim_{r\to 0}\sup_{B_{r}\text{ ball of radius }r\atop\text{and }x\in MB_{r}}\fint_{B_{r}}|u(X)-u(Z)|\,dm(X)=0.

It is well known
that the Lebesgue points have full measure, i.e.
m(A_{t})=m(\{Z\in A_{t},\,Z\text{ is a Lebesgue point}\})
.

Let
B_{0},B_{1},\dots
be the chain assigned to
Z
and given by Definition
5.21
, and write
r_{i}
for the radius of
B_{i}
. Pick a ball
B^{\prime}_{i}\subset B_{i}\cap B_{i+1}
with radius comparable to
r_{i}

(and
r_{i+1}
); it is indeed possible since
r_{i}\approx r_{i+1}
and, thanks to (iv) of
Definition
5.21
, the center
X_{i+1}
of
B_{i+1}
belongs to
\overline{B_{i}}
.
Since

Z
is a Lebesgue point of
u
and since the chain
\{B_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
satisfies (ii) and (iii) of Definition
5.21
,

\begin{split}t&<|u(Z)-u_{B_{0}}|\leq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}|u_{B_{i}}-u_{B_{i+1}}|\leq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(|u_{B_{i}}-u_{B^{\prime}_{i}}|+|u_{B_{i+1}}-u_{B^{\prime}_{i}}|\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\fint_{B^{\prime}_{i}}[|u-u_{B_{i}}|+|u-u_{B_{i+1}}|]\,dm\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\fint_{B_{i}}|u-u_{B_{i}}|\,dm+\fint_{B_{i+1}}|u-u_{B_{i+1}}|\,dm\right).\end{split}

Poincaré’s inequality (
H6
) implies that

\begin{split}t&\lesssim r\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa^{i}\left[\left(\fint_{B_{i}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\fint_{B_{i+1}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right]\\
&\lesssim r\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa^{i}\left(\fint_{B_{i}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\end{split}

which can be written, when
\epsilon>0
, as

(5.29)

r\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa^{i}\left(\fint_{B_{i}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\gtrsim t\gtrsim t\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\kappa^{i\epsilon}.

The estimate above proves that there exists
i_{Z}
such that

r\kappa^{i_{Z}}\left(\fint_{B_{i_{Z}}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\gtrsim t\kappa^{i_{Z}\epsilon}

hence, taking the power
p
and writing the average explicitly,

(5.30)

\kappa^{i_{Z}p(\epsilon-1)}m(B_{i_{Z}})\lesssim\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{p}\int_{B_{i_{Z}}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm.

Condition (ii) of Definition
5.21
gives that
Z\in MB_{i_{Z}}
.
Another way to say this
is that
B_{i_{Z}}\subset B_{Z}:=B(Z,r_{Z})
for some
r_{Z}\approx r_{i_{Z}}\approx\kappa^{i_{Z}}r
.
Moreover, due to (
H4
),

m(B_{i_{Z}})\approx m(B_{Z}\cap\Omega)
and

\kappa^{-i_{Z}d}\gtrsim\left(\frac{r}{r_{Z}}\right)^{d}\gtrsim\frac{m(B(Z,r)\cap\Omega)}{m(B_{Z}\cap\Omega)}\gtrsim\frac{m(D)}{m(B_{Z}\cap\Omega)},

where
d
is the exponent
d_{m}
given in (
2.5
), and where we recall that
r:=\diam D
.
We can freely assume that
\epsilon<1
,
and (
5.30
) becomes

(5.31)

m(B_{Z}\cap\Omega)^{1+(\epsilon-1)p/d}m(D)^{(1-\epsilon)p/d}\lesssim\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{p}\int_{B_{Z}\cap D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm.

The balls
B_{Z}
, where
Z\in A_{t}
is a Lebesgue point,
cover almost all of
A_{t}
.
Hence
the Vitali covering lemma entails that there exists a collection of pairwise disjoints balls

B_{Z_{j}}
,
j\in J
,
such that
A_{t}\subset\Omega\cap\big(\bigcup_{j\in J}5B_{Z_{j}}\big)

modulo a negligible set.
We fix
\epsilon
such that
1+(\epsilon-1)p/d=1-p/(d+p)=d/(d+p)
, that is

(\epsilon-1)p/d=-p/(d+p)
. Then

\begin{split}m(A_{t})^{d/(d+p)}&\leq\Big[\sum_{j\in J}m(5B_{Z_{j}}\cap\Omega)\Big]^{d/(d+p)}\leq\sum_{j\in J}m(B_{Z_{j}}\cap\Omega)^{d/(d+p)}\\
&\lesssim m(D)^{p/(d+p)}\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{p}\sum_{j\in J}\int_{B_{Z_{j}}\cap D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\leq m(D)^{p/(d+p)}\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{p}\int_{D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\end{split}

by the covering property, because
d/(d+p)<1
, then by (
5.31
),
our choice of
\varepsilon
,
because the exponent for
m(D)
is
(1-\epsilon)p/d=-1+[1+(1-\epsilon)p/d]=1-d/(d+p)=p/(d+p)
, and finally because the
B_{Z_{j}}
are disjoint.
Written differently, we proved that

\frac{m(A_{t})}{m(D)}\leq C\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{p(d+p)/d}\Big\{\int_{D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\Big\}^{p(d+p)/d},

or in other words
u
lies in the weak Lebesgue space
L^{p(d+p)/d}_{w}(D)
.
We can use this and the Cavalieri formula to estimate
||u||_{L^{q}(D)}
for any
q<p(d+p)/d
,
and get that

\left(\fint_{D}|u-u_{B_{0}}|^{q}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left(\fint_{D}|u|^{q}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C_{q}r\left(\fint_{D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}};

Theorem
5.24
follows.

\Box

Remark 5.32
.

A careful inspection on the proof would show that we can prove

\left(\frac{1}{m(D)}\int_{D}|u-\bar{u}|^{q}\,dm\right)^{1/q}\leq C_{q}\diam(D)\left(\frac{1}{m(D)}\int_{D}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

for every
q<+\infty
if
p\geq d
and every
q<\frac{pd}{d-p}
if
p<d
.
So in Theorem
5.24
, if
2\geq d
, we can take for
k
every positive value, and if
2<d
,
k
can take every value smaller than
d/(d-2)
.

6.
The Trace Theorem

As in the previous section, we assume that
\Gamma
and
\Omega
are infinite, but
the results of this section, in particular Theorem
6.6
, and Lemma
6.21
,
still hold when
\Gamma
and/or
\Omega
are finite.
We shall discuss this again
in Section
13
.

Let us first play
a bit with the dyadic decomposition
\mathbb{D}
,
Hölder, and Fubini.

Lemma 6.1
.

Assume that
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H3
) and let

q>1
. For
g\in L^{q}(\Omega,m)
,

\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-q}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}g\,dm\right)^{q}\leq C\int_{\Omega}|g|^{q}\,dm,

where
C
depends only on constants
C_{1}
to
C_{5}
,
n
and
q
.

Proof
.  The Hölder inequality implies that
for every
Q\in\mathbb{D}
,

\begin{split}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}g\,dm\right)^{q}&\leq\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}|g(Z)|^{q}dm(Z)\right)m(U^{*}_{Q})^{q-1}.\end{split}

We sum over
the dyadic cubes
Q
to get that

\begin{split}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-q}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}g\,dm\right)^{q}&\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}|g(Z)|^{q}\,dm(Z)\\
&\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|g(Z)|^{q}h(Z)\,dm(Z)\\
\end{split}

by Fubini’s lemma, and where

h(Z)=\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}{\mathds{1}}_{U^{*}_{Q}}(Z).

The sets
U^{*}_{Q}
are Whitney regions
associated to the cubes
Q
, so

Z\in U^{*}_{Q}
implies that
\delta(Z)\approx\ell(Q)\approx\,\mathrm{dist}(Z,Q)
, and
for each
Z

there can be only a bounded
number of such dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{D}
(the number depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
).
Hence

h(Z)\lesssim 1
and

\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-q}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}g\,dm\right)^{q}\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|g(Z)|^{q}\,dm(Z).

The lemma follows.

\Box

We also need the following Hardy inequality.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
q>1
. Assume that
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a weight on
\mathbb{Z}
that satisfies

(6.3)

\frac{s_{i}}{s_{j}}\leq C_{s}2^{(j-i)\epsilon}\quad\text{ for }i>j,

for some positive constants
C_{s}
and
\epsilon
.
Then, for
[g_{i}]_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z},s_{i})
,

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{k}^{1-q}\left(\sum_{i>k}s_{i}g_{i}\right)^{q}\leq C\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{i}|g_{i}|^{q},

where
C
depends only on
q
,
\epsilon
and
C_{s}
.

Remark 6.4
.

If
g_{i}=0
for
i>i_{0}
,
then we only need to require
(
6.3
) for
i\leq i_{0}
.

Proof
.  Let
\alpha=\epsilon/2>0
. Then by Hölder’s inequality

\begin{split}\left(\sum_{i>k}s_{i}g_{i}\right)^{q}&=\left(\sum_{i>k}2^{-i\alpha}s_{i}2^{i\alpha}g_{i}\right)^{q}\\
&\leq\left(\sum_{i>k}2^{-i\alpha}|s_{i}2^{i\alpha}g_{i}|^{q}\right)\left(\sum_{i>k}2^{-i\alpha}\right)^{q-1}\\
&\lesssim 2^{-k\alpha(q-1)}\sum_{i>k}2^{-i\alpha}|s_{i}2^{i\alpha}g_{i}|^{q}\end{split}

because
\alpha>0
. We sum in
k\in\mathbb{Z}

and then apply Fubini’s lemma
to get

\begin{split}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{k}^{1-q}\left(\sum_{i>k}s_{i}g_{i}\right)^{q}&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{k\alpha}s_{k})^{1-q}\sum_{i>k}2^{-i\alpha}|s_{i}2^{i\alpha}g_{i}|^{q}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-i\alpha}|s_{i}2^{i\alpha}g_{i}|^{q}\sum_{k<i}(2^{k\alpha}s_{k})^{1-q}.\end{split}

By (
6.3
),
2^{i\alpha}s_{i}=2^{i(\alpha-\varepsilon)}2^{i\varepsilon}s_{i}\lesssim 2^{i(\alpha-\varepsilon)}2^{k\varepsilon}s_{k}=2^{(i-k)(\alpha-\varepsilon)}2^{k\alpha}s_{k}
for
k<i
; then

\sum_{k<i}(2^{k\alpha}s_{k})^{1-q}\lesssim\sum_{k<i}(2^{i\alpha}s_{i})^{1-q}2^{(k-i)(\epsilon-\alpha)(q-1)}\lesssim(2^{i\alpha}s_{i})^{1-q},

because
q>1
and
\alpha<\epsilon
. This yields

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{k}^{1-q}\left(\sum_{i>k}s_{i}g_{i}\right)^{q}\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}s_{i}|g_{i}|^{q}

and the lemma follows.

\Box

The aim of the section is to show that the functions in
W
have a trace,
and that the traces lie in the space
H
defined as

(6.5)

H:=\Big\{g:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}\,;\,\text{$g$ is $\mu$-measurable and}\,\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(y)d\mu(x)<+\infty\Big\},

where
\rho
is as in (
2.6
).
The space
H
is equipped with the semi-norm

\|g\|_{H}:=\left(\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)d\mu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(adding a constant to
g
keeps
g
in
H
and does not change
\|g\|_{H}
).

The existence of traces is given by the following result.
Recall the nontangential cones
\gamma(x)
,
x\in\Gamma
, from (
5.8
).

Theorem 6.6
.

Assume that
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H6
). There exists a bounded linear operator
\Tr:\,W\to H
(a trace operator) with the following properties. The trace of
u\in W
is such that, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\Gamma
,

(6.7)

\Tr u(x)=\lim_{X\in\gamma(x)\atop{\delta(X)\to 0}}\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}u\,dm

and even,
analogously to the Lebesgue density property,

(6.8)

\lim_{X\in\gamma(x)\atop{\delta(X)\to 0}}\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|u(Z)-\Tr u(x)|\,dm(Z)=0.

Proof
.  For
x\in\Gamma
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\Tr_{k}u(x)
for any quantity

\Tr_{k}u(x):=\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}u(Z)\,dm(Z),

where

X
is picked in
U_{Q^{k}(x)}

and
Q^{k}(x)
is the only set in
\mathbb{D}_{k}
containing
x
. Keep in mind that

\Tr_{k}u(x)
is not uniquely defined, but the estimates on
\Tr_{k}u
that will be proven here hold with a constant independent of the choice of
X\in U_{Q^{k}(x)}
. For the rest of the proof, we also write
B^{k}_{x}
for
B(X,\delta(X)/2)
when
X\in U_{Q^{k}(x)}
. For any couple of integers
k<j
, one has

\left|\Tr_{j}u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(x)\right|\leq\sum_{k<i\leq j}\left|\Tr_{i-1}u(x)-\Tr_{i}u(x)\right|

and then for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, since both
B^{i-1}_{x}
and
B^{i}_{x}
belong to

U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}
by construction,

\begin{split}\left|\Tr_{i-1}u(x)-\Tr_{i}u(x)\right|&\leq\left|\fint_{B^{i-1}_{x}}u\,dm-\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}u\,dm\right|+\left|\fint_{B^{i}_{x}}u\,dm-\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}u\,dm\right|\\
&\leq\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}\left(\left|u(Z)-\fint_{B^{i-1}_{x}}u\,dm\right|+\left|u(Z)-\fint_{B^{i}_{x}}u\,dm\right|\right)dm(Z)\\
&\lesssim 2^{-i}\left(\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{p}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\end{split}

where the last inequality and the parameter
p\in(1,2)
are given by the Poincaré inequality
(Theorem
5.24
). The combination of the two
proves that

(6.9)

\left|\Tr_{j}u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(x)\right|\lesssim\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}2^{-i}\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Take
Q^{*}\in\mathbb{D}

and write
k^{*}
for
k(Q^{*})
. Let us prove that
(\Tr_{k}u)_{k\geq k^{*}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(Q^{*},\mu)
. We integrate in
x
to get

(6.10)

\begin{split}\int_{Q^{*}}\left|\Tr_{j}u-\Tr_{k}u\right|^{2}d\mu&\lesssim\int_{Q^{*}}\left(\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}2^{-i}\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{2}dx\\
&\hskip-56.9055pt\lesssim\int_{Q^{*}}\left[\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\frac{2^{-i}\rho(x,2^{-i})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})^{2/p}}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{p}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\right]\left[\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\frac{2^{-i}}{\rho(x,2^{-i})}\right]\,d\mu(x),\end{split}

where we use Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality for the last line, and where
\rho
is the function defined in (
2.6
).

From now on, let
\varepsilon=C_{5}^{-1}
denote the same small constant as in (
H5
). Then
(
2.7
) says that
\displaystyle\frac{2^{-i(1-\varepsilon)}}{\rho(x,2^{-i})}\lesssim\frac{2^{-k^{*}(1-\varepsilon)}}{\rho(x,2^{-k^{*}})}
, and hence

\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\frac{2^{-i}}{\rho(x,2^{-i})}\lesssim\frac{2^{-k^{*}}}{\rho(x,2^{-k^{*}})}\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\left(\frac{2^{-k^{*}}}{2^{-i}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\lesssim\frac{2^{-k^{*}}}{\rho(x,2^{-k^{*}})}\,2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}.

Moreover, thanks to (
2.6
) and
Lemma
5.16
,

(6.11)

\rho(x,2^{-i})=\frac{m(B(x,2^{-i})\cap\Omega)}{2^{-i}\mu(B(x,2^{-i}))}\approx\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}{2^{-i}\mu(Q^{i}(x))}.

We deduce from (
6.10
) and the two last estimates
(including (
6.11
) for
k^{\ast}
for the second line) that

\begin{split}\int_{Q^{*}}|\Tr_{j}u&-\Tr_{k}u|^{2}d\mu\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{-k^{*}}}{\rho(x,2^{-k^{*}})}\,2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{Q^{*}}\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})^{1-2/p}}{\mu(Q^{i}(x))}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{p}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\,d\mu(x)\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}\,2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{Q^{*}}\sum_{i=k+1}^{j}\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})^{1-2/p}}{\mu(Q^{i}(x))}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{p}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\,d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\sum_{i>k}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{i}\atop Q\subset Q^{*}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-2/p}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{2}{p}},\end{split}

where for the last line we decomposed
Q^{\ast}
into cubes
Q=Q^{i}(x)
for each
i
.
We write
\Omega_{k}
for

\Omega_{Q^{*},k}:=\bigcup_{x\in Q^{*}}\gamma^{*}_{Q^{k}(x)}=\bigcup_{Q\subset Q^{*}\atop k(Q)\leq k}U^{*}_{Q},

(see (
5.11
)), so that the difference of traces is bounded by

\int_{Q^{*}}\left|\Tr_{j}u-\Tr_{k}u\right|^{2}d\mu\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-2/p}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}{\mathds{1}}_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}.

Now we use lemma
6.1
with
q=2/p
and
g={\mathds{1}}_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{p}
to obtain that for
j>k>k^{\ast}

(6.12)

\int_{Q^{*}}\left|\Tr_{j}u-\Tr_{k}u\right|^{2}d\mu\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{2}dm.

The last result is pretty nice, and
just keeping the information that for each
Q^{\ast}
,

||\Tr_{j}u-\Tr_{k}u||^{2}_{L^{2}(Q^{\ast},d\mu)}\leq C(u,Q^{\ast})2^{-k\varepsilon}

for
j>k>k^{\ast}
, we get that the series
\sum_{k}\Tr_{k+1}u-\Tr_{k}u
converges
normally in every
L^{2}(Q^{\ast},d\mu)
, hence in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
and

\mu
-almost everywhere. That is,

\Tr u(x)=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\Tr_{k}u(x)=\lim_{X\in\gamma(x)\atop{\delta(X)\to 0}}\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}u(Z)\,dm(Z)

exists for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\Gamma
and by (
6.12
)

(6.13)

\int_{Q^{*}}\left|\Tr_{k}u-\Tr u\right|^{2}d\mu\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{2}dm.

The estimate (
6.13
) is not strong enough to imply the Lebesgue density property (
6.8
). However, observe that for
k<j
and
X\in U_{Q_{k}(x)}
,

\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr_{j}u(x)|\,dm\leq\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr_{k}u(x)|\,dm+|\Tr_{j}u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(x)|

and, thanks to Lemma
4.7
(improved Poincaré inequality) and the fact that
m(U^{*}_{Q^{k}_{x}})\approx m(B_{x}^{k})
(by (
H4
) and Lemma
5.16
),

\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr_{k}u(x)|\,dm\lesssim 2^{-k}\left(\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{k}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Together with (
6.9
), this implies that

\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr_{j}u(x)|\,dm\lesssim\sum_{i=k}^{j}2^{-i}\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We integrate on
x\in\Gamma
and
invoke Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality and Lemma
6.1
to get,
analogously to (
6.12
),

\int_{x\in Q^{*}}\left(\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr_{j}u(x)|\,dm\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{2}dm.

The right-hand side does not depend on
j
; we take
the limit as
j
approaches
+\infty
and
obtain

(6.14)

\int_{x\in Q^{*}}\left(\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr u(x)|\,dm\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k^{*}}\mu(Q^{*})}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{*}})}2^{-(k-k^{*})\epsilon}\int_{\Omega_{Q^{*},k}}|\nabla u|^{2}dm.

It follows that
x\to\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr u(x)|\,dm
converges to 0 in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
as
k\to+\infty
, and this
implies the Lebesgue density property (
6.8
).

It remains to prove that
\Tr
is a bounded operator from
W
to
H
.
If
x,y\in\Gamma
, we write
k(x,y)
for the only integer
k
that satisfies

2^{-k-1}\leq|x-y|<2^{-k}
. We use
(
6.5
) and decompose the integral as

\begin{split}\|\Tr u\|_{H}&:=\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\dfrac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|\Tr u(x)-\Tr u(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)\\
&\hskip 0.0pt\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\dfrac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|\Tr u(x)-\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(x)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\dfrac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|\Tr u(y)-\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\dfrac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(x)-\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt:=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.\end{split}

Let us first treat the term
I_{3}
.
More precisely,
we
start with the difference
|\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(x)-\Tr_{k(x,y)}u(y)|
. To lighten the notation,
write
k
for
k(x,y)
. As before, denote by

B_{x}^{k}
and
B_{y}^{k}

the balls used to define

\Tr_{k}u(x)
and
\Tr_{k}u(y)
.
That is,

B_{x}^{k}
and
B_{y}^{k}
are such that

\Tr_{k}u(x)=\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}u\quad\text{ and }\quad\Tr_{k}u(y)=\fint_{B_{y}^{k}}u.

Since the balls
B_{x}^{k},B_{y}^{k}

lie at distances at least
c2^{-k}
from the boundary
\Gamma
and at most

C2^{-k}
from each other,
the Harnack chain condition (Proposition
2.18
) says that we can find a chain of balls joining
B_{x}^{k}
to
B_{y}^{k}
, with
uniformly bounded
length, staying at a distance at least
c2^{-k}
from the boundary and
at distance at most
C2^{-k}
from both
x
and
y
. We define
P_{x,y}^{k}
as the union of the cubes in
\mathcal{W}
that meet one of the balls of the chain. From what we just said,
P_{x,y}^{k}
is a Whitney region associated to both
(x,2^{-k})
and
(y,2^{-k})
, and so it
is the union of a bounded
number of adjacent cubes in
{\mathcal{W}}
. Therefore, similarly to the sets
U^{*}_{Q}
,
P_{x,y}^{k}
satisfies the
C(\kappa,M)
chain condition for some uniform
\kappa,M
and is thus fitted for the Poincaré inequality.
These observations allow us to use Theorem
5.24
and write

\begin{split}|\Tr_{k}u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(y)|&:=\left|\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}u\,dm-\fint_{B_{y}^{k}}u\,dm\right|\lesssim\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}\Big|u-\fint_{B_{y}^{k}}u\,dm\Big|\,dm\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{m(B_{x}^{k})}\int_{P_{x,y}^{k}}\Big|u-\fint_{B_{y}^{r}}u\,dm\Big|dm\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{-k}m(P_{x,y}^{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{m(B_{x}^{k})}\left(\int_{P_{x,y}^{k}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

Since both
P^{k}_{x,y}
and
B_{x}^{k}
are Whitney region associated to
y
and
2^{-k}
(i.e. there exists a large constant
C
such that both sets are contained in
B(y,C2^{-k})
, contain a ball
B
of radius
C^{-1}2^{-k}
, and are at distance at least
C^{-1}2^{-k}
of
\Gamma
), the doubling measure condition (
H4
) implies that
m(B_{x}^{k})\approx m(P_{x,y}^{k})\approx m(B(y,2^{-k-1})\cap\Omega)
. Therefore,

(6.15)

|\Tr_{k}u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(y)|^{2}\lesssim\frac{2^{-2k}}{m(B(y,2^{-k-1})\cap\Omega)}\int_{P_{x,y}^{k}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}dm.

We inject (
6.15
) in
I_{3}
and
observe that
m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)\approx m(B(x,2^{-k(x,y)})\cap\Omega)
and
\rho(x,|x-y|)\approx\rho(x,2^{-k(x,y)})\approx\rho(y,2^{-k(x,y)})
by (
H3
)–(
H4
).
Therefore,

(6.16)

\begin{split}I_{3}&\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{2^{-k(x,y)}\rho(x,2^{-k(x,y)})}{m(B(x,2^{-k(x,y)})\cap\Omega)}\frac{2^{-k(x,y)}\rho(y,2^{-k(x,y)})}{m(B(y,2^{-k(x,y)})\cap\Omega)}\int_{P_{x,y}^{k(x,y)}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}dm\,d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\\
&\quad=\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,2^{-k(x,y)}))}\frac{1}{\mu(B(y,2^{-k(x,y)}))}\int_{P_{x,y}^{k(x,y)}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}dm\,d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y),\end{split}

by the definition (
2.6
) of
\rho
.
Since
P_{x,y}^{k(x,y)}
is a ‘Whitney region’ for both
x
and
y
, we have that

x,y\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))
for
Z\in P^{k(x,y)}_{x,y}

where the constant
C\geq 2
depends only on
n,C_{1},C_{2}
,
and moreover

2^{-k(x,y)}\approx\delta(Z)
.
Then by Fubini’s lemma

(6.17)

\begin{split}I_{3}&\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(Z)|^{2}dm(Z)\,\int_{x\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))}\frac{d\mu(x)}{\mu(B(x,c\delta(Z)))}\int_{y\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))}\frac{d\mu(y)}{\mu(B(y,c\delta(Z)))}.\end{split}

Yet
the doubling property (
H3
) implies that for
z\in\Gamma\cap B(Z,C\delta(Z))
,

\mu(B(z,c\delta(Z)))\gtrsim\mu(B(z,C^{2}\delta(Z)))\geq\mu(B(Z,C\delta(Z))),

hence we can simply bound
I_{3}
by

(6.18)

I_{3}\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(Z)|^{2}\,dm(Z)

as desired.

We turn now to the bound on
I_{1}
.
Notice that the estimate for
I_{2}
is the same as for
I_{1}
, either by symmetry or
since by (
H3
)–(
H4
),
m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)\approx\mu(B(y,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)
and
\rho(x,|x-y|)\approx\rho(y,|x-y|)
.

Notice that
I_{1}
depends on
y
only via
|x-y|
,
so, by the doubling property (
H3
) again and then (
2.6
)

(6.19)

\begin{split}I_{1}&\lesssim\int_{x\in\Gamma}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\rho(x,2^{-k})^{2}|\Tr u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(x)|^{2}}{m(B(x,2^{-k})\cap\Omega)}\int_{y\in B(x,2^{-k})\setminus B(x,2^{-k-1})}d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)\\
&\lesssim\int_{x\in\Gamma}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{k}\rho(x,2^{-k})|\Tr u(x)-\Tr_{k}u(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x).\end{split}

The trace operator is defined for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\Gamma
by (
6.7
). For such
x
, one get by letting
j
tend to
+\infty

in (
6.9
) that

(6.20)

\left|\Tr_{k}u(x)-\Tr u(x)\right|\lesssim\sum_{i>k}2^{-i}\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We use the above estimate in (
6.19
) to obtain that

\begin{split}I_{1}&\lesssim\int_{x\in\Gamma}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{k}\rho(x,2^{-k})\left(\sum_{i>k}2^{-i}\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)^{2}\,d\mu(x)\\
&\qquad=\int_{x\in\Gamma}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{k}\rho(x,2^{-k})\left(\sum_{i>k}\frac{1}{2^{i}\rho(x,2^{-i})}\,g_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\,d\mu(x),\end{split}

where

g_{i}(x)=\rho(x,2^{-i})\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Thanks to (
H5
), the sequence
\{s_{i}\}_{i\geq k_{0}}
defined as

s_{i}:=[2^{i}\rho(x,2^{-i})]^{-1}
satisfies (
6.3
).
As a consequence, Lemma
6.2

(with
q=2
) gives that for each
x\in\Gamma
,

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{k}\rho(x,2^{-k})\left(\sum_{i>k}\frac{1}{2^{i}\rho(x,2^{-i})}\,g_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{2^{i}\rho(x,2^{-i})}\,|g_{i}(x)|^{2}.

Thus the bound on
I_{1}
becomes

\begin{split}I_{1}&\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{2^{i}\rho(x,2^{-i})}|g_{i}(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x)\\
&\qquad=\int_{\Gamma}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-i}\rho(x,2^{-i})\left(\frac{1}{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})}\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}d\mu(x).\end{split}

We use (
6.11
) to get rid of the function
\rho
, and then write the bound we obtained
as a sum over

\mathbb{D}
, which gives

\begin{split}I_{1}&\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{m(U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)})^{1-2/p}}{\mu(Q^{i}(x))}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q^{i}(x)}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}d\mu(x)=\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}m(U^{*}_{Q})^{1-2/p}\left(\int_{U^{*}_{Q}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}.\end{split}

We can now apply Lemma
6.1

with

q=2/p
and
g=|\nabla u|^{p}
. Recall recall that
q>1
because
p
comes from
Theorem
5.24
and was chosen above (
6.9
), with
1<p<2
.
We get that

I_{1}\lesssim\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm;

Theorem
6.6
follows.

\Box

We end this section with a useful result concerning the trace of a product.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfy (
H1
)–(
H6
).
Suppose
u\in W
and
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Then
u\varphi\in W
, with the product rule

(6.22)

\nabla(u\varphi)=\varphi\nabla u+u\nabla\varphi

for the gradient, and

(6.23)

\Tr(u\varphi)(x)=\varphi(x)\Tr u(x)\quad\hbox{for every point $x\in\Gamma$ satisfying \eqref{defLebesgue}.}

Proof
.  This result is the analogue of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 5.4]
.
The proof is similar, so we only sketch it.

We start with the simplest case is when we can see
u
as a distribution on
\Omega
;
this is the case when the stronger form (
H6’
) of our assumption (
H6
) holds.
Then
\nabla(u\varphi)=\varphi\nabla u+u\nabla\varphi
in the sense of distributions,
and we are about to check that
\nabla(u\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Omega,dm)
.

Choose
Q\in\mathbb{D}
so large that
\supp\,\varphi\cap\Omega\subset T_{2Q}
.
Then, setting
\bar{u}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q}}u\,dm
,

(6.24)

\|\nabla(u\varphi)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega,dm)}\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\|u\|_{W}+\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\left(\int_{T_{2Q}}|u-\bar{u}|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}m(T_{2Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}|\bar{u}|.

All three terms in the right hand side are finite, since
\varphi
is smooth,
u\in W\subset L^{1}(U^{*}_{Q},m)
, and by Theorem
5.24
(Poincaré’s inequality). Consequently,
u\varphi\in W
as desired.

As for the trace, observe that if
x
satisfies (
6.8
), and if
B_{x}^{k}
denotes
B(X,\delta(X)/2)
for some
X\in U_{Q_{k}(x)}
, where
as usual
Q_{k}(x)
is the only cube of
\mathbb{D}_{k}
that contains
x
, then

\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|\varphi u-\varphi(x)\Tr u(x)|\,dm\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|u-\Tr u(x)|\,dm+|Tu(x)|\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|\varphi-\varphi(x)|\,dm.

The first term converges to 0 as
k\to+\infty
since
x
is a Lebesgue point for
u
, and the second term also tends to 0 since
|Tu(x)|<+\infty
(
x
is a Lebesgue point) and
\varphi
is continuous. Therefore

\lim_{\delta(X)\to 0,\atop X\in\gamma(x)}\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}|\varphi u-\varphi(x)\Tr u(x)|\,dm=0,

which easily implies
\Tr(u\varphi)(x)=\varphi(x)\Tr u(x)
by the definition of
\Tr
.

This takes care of the lemma when
u
and
\nabla u
are taken as distributions.
In general, we used Definition
4.1
to define the space
W
and the gradient
\nabla u
.
Recall that we wrote
u
as a limit in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
of smooth functions

\varphi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
, as in (
4.3
), and required that (
4.2
)
and (
4.4
) hold for a suitable
v\in L^{2}(\Omega,dm)
which is unique by (
H6
)
and which we also called
\nabla u
.

Now we want to show that
u\varphi\in W
, so we approximate it by the
smooth functions
\varphi_{i}\varphi
. It is easy to see that the
\varphi_{i}\varphi

converge to
u\varphi
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
as in (
4.3
),
and that
\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\varphi\varphi_{i})|^{2}<+\infty

for every
i
, as in (
4.2
) (recall that
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
).
We try the gradient
w=\varphi v+u\nabla\varphi=\varphi\nabla u+u\nabla\varphi

in the definition (
4.4
). First observe that
w\in L^{2}(\Omega,dm)
by the proof
of (
6.24
) (and where Theorem
5.24
is applied in the general context of
W
).
We claim that

\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\varphi\varphi_{i})-w|^{2}dm=0,

as needed for (
4.4
). The first part of
\nabla(\varphi\varphi_{i})

is
\varphi\nabla\varphi_{i}
, which converges to
\varphi v
in
L^{2}(\Omega,dm)
,
by (
4.4
). Thus we are left with showing that
\varphi_{i}\nabla\varphi

converges to
u\nabla\varphi
in
L^{2}(\Omega,dm)
. Or, since
\varphi
is bounded
and
\supp\,\varphi\cap\Omega\subset T_{2Q}
, that

(6.25)

\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{T_{2Q}}|\varphi_{i}-u|^{2}dm=0.

Denote by
c_{i}
the average of
\varphi_{i}-u
on
2Q
. Then by Poincaré’ inequality
(Theorem
5.24
) and (
4.4
),
\int_{T_{2Q}}|\varphi_{i}-u-c_{i}|^{2}dm
tends to
0
.
But also (
4.3
) says that
\int_{B}|\varphi_{i}-u|dm
tends to
0
for some small
ball
B\subset T_{2Q}
, so in fact
c_{i}
tends to
0
, (
6.25
) holds, and
u\varphi\in W

with a derivative equal to
w
. The remaining estimates are as in the easier case, and
Lemma
6.21
follows.

\Box

7.
Poincaré inequalities on the boundary

We are interested in
a version of the Poincaré inequality for functions that
have a vanishing trace at the boundary.
The proofs shall use the tent sets
T_{2Q}
that were constructed in Section
5
,
where we assumed
\Gamma
and
\Omega
unbounded. But as explained in Section
13
,
the same construction works for bounded
\Gamma
and/or
\Omega
(with maybe a restriction on the size of possible
Q
), and the proofs in the section are directly adaptable to this case. The Poincaré inequalities that we prove here are a local results, so it makes sense anyway that they don’t depend on the boundedness of
\Omega
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfy (
H1
)–(
H6
).
There exists
p_{1}\in[1,2)
and
k:=k(C_{4})>1
such that for
p\in[p_{1},2]
,

Q\in\mathbb{D}
,
and
u\in W
such that

\Tr u=0
on a set

E\subset 2Q
such that

\mu(E)\geq c\mu(2Q)
, we have

(7.2)

\left(\frac{1}{m(T_{2Q})}\int_{T_{2Q}}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C\ell(Q)\left(\frac{1}{m(T_{2Q})}\int_{T_{2Q}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

where
T_{2Q}
is the same tent set over
2Q
as in (
5.12
), and

C>0
depends only on
n
, the constants
C_{1}
-
C_{6}
, and
c
.

Proof
.  Take
z\in 2Q
.
We start as in
the proof of Lemma
5.23
, and
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}

we define
Q_{j}^{z}
as the dyadic cube in
\mathbb{D}_{j+k(Q)}
that contains
z
.
Let
X_{j}^{z}
be
a Corkscrew point associated to
z
and the scale

\ell(Q^{z}_{j})=2^{-j}\ell(Q)
; by construction of
\mathcal{W}_{Q_{j}^{z}}
, we can find a cube
I^{j,z}\in\mathcal{W}_{Q_{j}^{z}}
containing
X_{j}^{z}
, and we
denote by
Y_{j}^{z}

the center of
I^{j,z}
. By construction of
\mathcal{W}_{Q_{j}^{z}}^{*}
,

B_{j}^{z}:=B(Y_{j}^{z},\delta(Y_{j}^{z})/2)\subset U^{*}_{Q}.

By Proposition
2.18
, we can find a uniform integer
N=N(n,C_{1},C_{2})
such that we can link
B_{j}^{z}
to
B^{z}_{j+1}
by a Harnack chain of length
N
. We construct
a chain
of balls
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z}\}
as follows: for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z}\}_{jN\leq i\leq(j+1)N}
is the chain linking
B_{j}^{z}
to
B^{z}_{j+1}
given by Proposition
2.18
and used to built
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{Q^{z}_{j+1}}
.
The collection of balls
(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z})_{i\geq 0}

that we just constructed has bounded overlap, is
included in
\gamma^{*}_{Q}(z)
, and is such that

\diam\,\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z}\approx 2^{-i/N}\ell(Q)
; observe also that by construction of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Q}
,
we can choose
I^{0,z}
(and thus

B_{0}^{z}=\mathcal{B}_{0}^{z}
)
independent of
z
, hence we write
B_{0}
for
B_{0}^{z}
.

For any subset
S\subset T_{2Q}
, we write as before
u_{S}
for
\fint_{S}u\,dm
.
Theorem
7.1
will follow from Theorem
5.24
as soon as we prove
that for some
p_{1}\in[1,2)
,

(7.3)

|u_{B_{0}}|\lesssim\ell(Q)\left(\frac{1}{m(T_{2Q})}\int_{T_{2Q}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p}

holds for all
p\in[p_{1},2]
.

Let
q\in[p_{0},2]
, where
p_{0}
is the value provided by Lemma
4.7
.
Thanks to Theorem
6.6
, for
\mu
-almost every
z\in E
, we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{jN}}|=0.

In particular,

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&\leq\lim_{j\to+\infty}\big\{|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{jN}}|+|u_{B_{0}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{jN}}|\big\}\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{jN}}|+\sum_{0\leq i<jN}|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|\leq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|.\end{split}

Since
\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}\cap\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}
,
\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}

have comparable sizes, the Poincaré inequality (Lemma
4.7
) gives that

\begin{split}|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|&\leq|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}\cap\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|+|u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}\cap\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\\
&\leq\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}\cap\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}\left(|u-u_{B^{z}_{i}}|+|u-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|\right)\,dm\\
&\lesssim\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|u-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\,dm+\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|u-u_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|\,dm\\
&\lesssim 2^{-i/N}\ell(Q)\left(\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\nabla u|^{q}\,dm+\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i+1}}|\nabla u|^{q}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\\
\end{split}

So the last two computations yield that, for
\mu
-almost every
z\in E

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-i/N}\ell(Q)\left(\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\nabla u|^{q}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\ell(Q)\left(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-iq\alpha/N}\fint_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\nabla u|^{q}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\end{split}

where we applied Hölder’s inequality for the last part, and the price to pay is that
we need to introduce
\alpha\in(0,1)
, close to 1, that will be fixed later on. Now
observe that, for

Z\in\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}
,
we have
\delta(Z)\approx 2^{-i/N}\ell(Q)
and
hence
2^{-iq\alpha/N}\approx\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}\ell(Q)^{-q\alpha}
.
So, by (
H4
),

m(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z})\approx m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))\quad\text{ for }Z\in\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z}.

Hence

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathcal{B}^{z}_{i}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}}{m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))}\,dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\int_{\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}}{m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))}\,dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
\end{split}

since the balls
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{z}
have bounded overlap and are contained in
\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)

(see the beginning of the proof, slightly above (
7.3
))
We
average over
z\in E
this estimate for
|u_{B_{0}}|^{q}
to obtain

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&=\left(\fint_{z\in E}|u_{B_{0}}|^{q}d\mu(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\fint_{E}\int_{\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}}{m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))}\,dm(Z)\,d\mu(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\fint_{z\in 2Q}\int_{\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}}{m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))}\,dm(Z)\,d\mu(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\end{split}

because we assume
\mu(E)\geq c\mu(2Q)
.
Notice that
Z\in\gamma_{Q}^{*}(z)
implies that

z\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))
.
Therefore, by Fubini’s lemma and (
5.12
),

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{\mu(2Q)}\int_{Z\in T_{2Q}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha}}{m(B(Z,C\delta(Z)))}\,dm(Z)\int_{z\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))}d\mu(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{split}

If
Z\in\Omega
,
we pick
z_{0}\in\Gamma
such that
|Z-z_{0}|=\,\mathrm{dist}(Z,\Gamma)
, and
define
\rho(Z)
by
\rho(z_{0},\delta(Z))
. The point
z_{0}
, and then
\rho(Z)
, are not
uniquely defined,
but it is easy to check that two choice of
z_{0}
will be equivalent (up to constants independent of
Z
),
or if the reader prefers, the estimates below do not depend on our choice of
z_{0}
.
The doubling conditions (
H3
) and (
H4
) and the definition (
2.6
) imply that

\int_{z\in B(Z,C\delta(Z))}d\mu(z)\approx\mu(B(z_{0},\delta(Z)))\approx\frac{m(B(z_{0},\delta(Z))\cap\Omega)}{\delta(Z)\rho(z_{0},\delta(Z))}\approx\frac{m(B(Z,2\delta(Z)))}{\delta(Z)\rho(Z)},

and the estimate on
u_{B_{0}}
becomes

|u_{B_{0}}|\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{\mu(2Q)}\int_{Z\in T_{2Q}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\,\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha-1}}{\rho(Z)}\,dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

By (
5.3
), (
5.15
),
(
2.6
),
and (
H4
), one can show that
m(T_{2Q})\approx\ell(Q)\rho(z_{Q},\ell(Q))\mu(2Q)
, where
z_{Q}
is a fixed point in
Q
. Therefore, for
p\in(q,2]
,

\begin{split}|u_{B_{0}}|&\lesssim\ell(Q)\left(\ell(Q)^{1-q\alpha}\rho(z_{Q},\ell(Q))\fint_{Z\in T_{2Q}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{q}\frac{\delta(Z)^{q\alpha-1}}{\rho(Z)}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\lesssim\ell(Q)\Big(\fint_{T_{2Q}}|\nabla u|^{p}dm\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\big[\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha q}\rho(z_{Q},\ell(Q))\big]^{p/(p-q)}\fint_{Z\in T_{2Q}}\left(\frac{\delta(Z)^{[q\alpha-1]}}{\rho(Z)}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-q}}dm(Z)\right)^{\frac{p-q}{qp}}\end{split}

by the Hölder inequality. The claim (
7.3
) will thus be proven if we can establish that, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
close to
1
,

(7.4)

I_{0}:=\fint_{Z\in T_{2Q}}\left(\frac{\delta(Z)^{[q\alpha-1]}}{\rho(Z)}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-q}}dm(Z)\lesssim[\ell(Q)^{1-\alpha q}\rho(z_{Q},\ell(Q))]^{-p/(p-q)}

when
p<2
is close enough to
2
.

By construction of
T_{2Q}
(see (
5.12
) and (
5.11
)),

T_{2Q}\subset\bigcup_{R\subset 100Q}U_{R}^{*}

and the covering has a uniformly finite overlap.
Notice also that

\rho(Z)\approx\rho(R)\qquad\text{ for $Z\in U^{*}_{R}$ and $R\in\mathbb{D}$},

where
\rho(R)
is defined in (
5.17
) and where the constants are independent of
Z
and
R
. We call
\rho(R)
the value of
\rho(Z)
for a
Z\in U^{*}_{R}
. The two last observation allow us to write

(7.5)

I_{0}\lesssim\frac{1}{m(T_{2Q})}\sum_{R\in\mathbb{D}\atop R\subset 100Q}m(U^{*}_{R})\left(\frac{\ell(R)^{[q\alpha-1]}}{\rho(R)}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-q}}.

We let the reader check that by definition of
\rho
, and by arguments similar to the ones used to prove Lemma
5.16
,

m(T_{2Q})\approx\ell(Q)\rho(Q)\mu(Q)

and

m(U^{*}_{R})\approx\ell(R)\rho(R)\mu(R).

The bound (
7.5
) becomes

(7.6)

I_{0}\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(Q)\rho(Q)\mu(Q)}\sum_{R\in\mathbb{D}\atop R\subset 100Q}\ell(R)^{[q\alpha-1]\frac{p}{p-q}+1}\rho(R)^{1-\frac{p}{p-q}}\mu(R).

By (
H5
),

\rho(R)\gtrsim\rho(Q)\left(\frac{\delta(R)}{\delta(Q)}\right)^{1-\epsilon}\approx\rho(Q)\left(\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{1-\epsilon},

where
\epsilon=C_{5}^{-1}
is the one given in (
H5
).
We use this to replace
\rho(R)
in (
7.6
) by
\rho(Q)
; notice that the
inequality goes in the right direction because the exponent
1-\frac{p}{p-q}=\frac{-q}{p-q}

is negative (recall that
p\in(q,2]
). We get that

(7.7)

I_{0}\lesssim\rho(Q)^{-p/(p-q)}\ell(Q)^{a}\mu(Q)^{-1}\sum_{R\in\mathbb{D}\atop R\subset 100Q}\ell(R)^{b}\mu(R),

with the exponents
a=-1+(\varepsilon-1)\big(1-\frac{p}{p-q}\big)

and
b=[q\alpha-1]\frac{p}{p-q}+1+(1-\varepsilon)\big(1-\frac{p}{p-q}\big)=[q\alpha-2+\epsilon]\frac{p}{p-q}+2-\epsilon
.

If
2-p\leq\min\{\epsilon/2,(2-p_{0})/2\}
, we can pick
\alpha\in(0,1)
(small) and
q\in[p_{0},2)
such that
q\alpha-2+\epsilon\geq 0
. With these values,
we can still pick
p\in(q,2]
as above, and since the power for
\ell(R)
is
b>0
,
we can bound
\ell(R)
brutally by
\ell(Q)
, which gives

(7.8)

I_{0}\lesssim\rho(Q)^{-p/(p-q)}\ell(Q)^{a+b}\mu(Q)^{-1}\sum_{R\in\mathbb{D}\atop R\subset 100Q}\mu(R)\lesssim\rho(Q)^{-p/(p-q)}\ell(Q)^{a+b}.

Notice that
a+b=[q\alpha-1]\frac{p}{p-q}
; the
claim (
7.4
) follows from the observation that
\rho(Q)\approx\rho(z_{Q},\ell(Q))
, and
we have seen before that (
7.3
) and Theorem
7.1
follow.

\Box

In the following corollary of Theorem
7.1
we replace the tents
T_{2Q}
by balls.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfy (
H1
)–(
H6
).
There exists
p_{1}\in[1,2]
and
k:=k(C_{4})>1
such that the following happens for
p\in[p_{1},2]
.
Let
\lambda>1
be given, and let
x\in\Gamma
,
r>0
, and
u\in W
be such that
\Tr u=0
on
B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Gamma
; then

(7.10)

\left(\fint_{B(x,r)\cap\Omega}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C_{\lambda}r\left(\fint_{B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
\lambda
.

Proof
.  Let
x^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
r^{\prime}>0
be given. Let
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
be
the only dyadic cube such that
x^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}\leq k(Q^{\prime})<2r^{\prime}
. Then

B(x^{\prime},r^{\prime})\subset 2Q^{\prime}
, and by Theorem
7.1
and (
5.15
),
there exists
K>1
that depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
such that

(7.11)

\left(\fint_{B(x^{\prime},r^{\prime})\cap\Omega}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq Cr^{\prime}\left(\fint_{B(x^{\prime},Kr^{\prime})\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

provided that
\Tr u\equiv 0
on
Q^{\prime}\subset B(x^{\prime},Kr^{\prime})\cap\Gamma
.

This looks like the desired estimate, but the constant
K
is too large; we will fix this with
a covering argument. Set
\tau=(\lambda-1)r/100K
, with
\lambda
as in the statement
and
K
as above. Then let
x\in\Gamma
and
r>0
be given. Denote by

(x_{i})_{i\in I}
a maximal collection of points of
\Gamma\cap B(x,(1+2\tau)r)
such that

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq\tau r
for
i\neq j
. Thus the balls
B_{i}=B(x_{i},2\tau r)
,
cover
\Gamma\cap B(x,(1+2\tau)r)
, and the sets
D_{i}=\Omega\cap B(x_{i},4\tau r)

cover

H:=\big\{X\in\Omega:\,\,\mathrm{dist}(X,\Gamma\cap B(x,(1+2\tau)r)\leq 2\tau r\big\}

Notice that
I
has at most
C
elements, with a constant that depends also on
\lambda

and
K
through
\tau
, but this is all right. We can apply (
7.11
) to each

B(x_{i},4\tau r)
, and we get that

(7.12)

\left(\fint_{D_{i}}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C\tau r\left(\fint_{B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

because
B(x_{i},4K\tau r)\subset B(x,\lambda r)
by choice of
\tau
.
We may sum over
i
and get that

(7.13)

\left(\fint_{H}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C\tau r\left(\fint_{B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

and now we just need to take care of
H_{1}=\Omega\cap B(x,r)\setminus H
.
Let
(y_{j})_{j\in J}
be a maximal collection of points of
H_{1}
, with

|y_{i}-y_{j}|\geq\tau r
for
i\neq j
. Thus
J
has at most
C=C(\tau)

points, and the set
B_{j}=B(y_{j},2\tau r)
,
j\in J
, cover
H
. We want to control
each
\fint_{B_{j}}|u|^{kp}\,dm
, and then we’ll sum.

Fix
j\in J
, and call
z_{j}
the first point of
[y_{j},x]
(starting from
y_{j}
)
that lies within
\tau r
from
\Gamma
. Obviously
z_{j}\in B(x,r)
, and

B(z_{j},\tau r)\subset H
because
\Gamma\setminus B(x,(1+2\tau)r)
is too far.
Now denote by
W_{j}
the convex hull of
B(y_{j},\tau r)
and
B(z_{j},\tau r)

(a nice tube contained in
\Omega
) and set
\widetilde{W}_{j}=W_{j}\cup B_{j}

(with a larger head around
y_{j}
, and still contained in
\Omega
). It is easy to see
that
\widetilde{W}_{j}
satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
of Definition
5.21
,
with any small
\kappa
chosen in advance, and with an
M
that depends only on
\kappa

and
\tau
; we can take
B(z_{j},\tau r/2)
as the distinguished ball. This allows us to apply
Theorem
5.24
, and prove that

(7.14)

\left(\fint_{B_{j}}|u-\bar{u}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C\left(\fint_{\widetilde{W}_{j}}|u-\bar{u}|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq Cr\left(\fint_{\widetilde{W}_{j}}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p},

where
\bar{u}
denotes the average of
u
on
B(z_{j},\tau r/2)
.
Now
|\bar{u}|\leq C\left(\fint_{B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p}
,
by (
7.13
) and because
B(z_{j},\tau r)\subset H
, and
\widetilde{W}_{j}\subset B(x,(1+2\tau)r)\subset B(x,\lambda r)
by definition of
\tau
, we we may sum (
7.14
) over
j

and get that

(7.15)

\left(\fint_{H_{1}}|u|^{kp}\,dm\right)^{1/kp}\leq C\tau r\left(\fint_{B(x,\lambda r)\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\,dm\right)^{1/p}.

We combine this with (
7.13
) and get (
7.11
), as needed for
Corollary
7.9
.

\Box

8.
The Extension Theorem

The aim of this section is the construction of an extension operator
\Ext:\,H\to W
such that the composition
\Tr\circ\Ext
is the identity on
H
.
The section can be seen as the dual of Section
6
. As in Section Section
6
, the results will be only proven when assuming
\Gamma
and
\Omega
unbounded, and the proof in the bounded case is very similar and is discussed in Section
13
.

We assume that
\Gamma
and
\Omega
are unbounded.
The beginning of this section is similar to
[
DFM2
, Section 7]
,
but the proof of the density result Lemma
8.12
is different.

We shall construct
\Ext
with the help of a Whitney extension. But first, it is crucial to observe that for any
g\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
and
\mu
-almost every
x\in\Gamma
, one has

(8.1)

\lim_{r\to 0}\fint_{B(x,r)}|g(y)-g(x)|\,d\mu(y)=0.

This is a consequence
of the Lebesgue differentiation theorem in doubling spaces (see for instance
[
Fed
, Sections 2.8-2.9]
). It is easy to verify that
H\subset L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)

(see (
6.5
))
and so
(
8.1
) holds for any function
g\in H
.

Our construction will rely on
the family
{\mathcal{W}}
of dyadic Whitney cubes already used
in Section
5
. We associate to

\mathcal{W}
a partition of unity
\{\varphi_{I}\}_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}
where
the
\varphi_{I}
are smooth functions supported in
2I
that satisfy
0\leq\varphi_{I}\leq 1
,
|\nabla\varphi_{I}|\leq C/\ell(I)
and
\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}\varphi_{I}={\mathds{1}}_{\Omega}
.

We record a few properties of
{\mathcal{W}}
, that can be found in
[
Ste
, Chapter VI]
.
If two dyadic cubes
I
and
I^{\prime}
are such that
2I\cap 2I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then

\ell(I)/\ell(I^{\prime})\in\{1/2,1,2\}
, and also
I^{\prime}\subset 6I
.

Hence, for a given
I
,

(8.2)

the number of cubes
I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
2I^{\prime}\cap 2I\neq 0
is at most
2\cdot 12^{n}
,

because each such
I^{\prime}
needs to be a dyadic cube in
6I
such that
\ell(I)\geq\ell(I)/2
.

For each
I\in\mathcal{W}
, we write
\delta(I)=\,\mathrm{dist}(I,\Gamma)
, pick a point
\xi_{I}\in\Gamma

such that
\,\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{I},I)\leq 2\delta(I)
, and set
B_{I}=B(\xi_{I},\ell(I))
.

We define
the extension operator
\Ext
on functions
g\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
by

(8.3)

\Ext g(X):=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}\varphi_{I}(X)y_{I},

where

(8.4)

y_{I}:=\fint_{B_{I}}g(z)\,d\mu(z).

If we wanted to have an extension operator on - for instance - Lipschitz function, we could take
y_{I}=g(\xi_{I})
. However, since the function
g
is
not smooth (and maybe not even defined everywhere),
we need this extra average; a good way to see this
is to notice that otherwise we would only use the values of
g
on the countable set

\{\xi_{I}\}_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}
,
which does not make sense for functions in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
.

Notice that
\Ext g
lies in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
because (
8.2
) yields that the sum in (
8.3
) is locally finite. Moreover, if
g
is continuous on
\Gamma
, then
\Ext g
is continuous on
\overline{\Omega}
(see
[
Ste
, Proposition VI.2.2]
).

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H6
).
For any
g\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)

(8.6)

\Tr\circ\Ext g=g\qquad\text{ $\mu$-a.e. in $\Gamma$}.

Moreover,
\Ext
is a bounded linear operator from
H
to
W
, i.e. there exists
C:=C(C_{3},C_{4},C_{5})>0
such that for any
g\in H
,

(8.7)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\Ext g|^{2}\,dm\leq C\|g\|_{H}^{2}:=C\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|))}\,d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y).

Proof
.  Let
g\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
be given.
We write
u
for
\Ext g
and we want to show that
\Tr u=g
,
in the sense that (
6.7
) holds with
Tru(x)=g(x)
for
\mu
-almost
every
x\in\Gamma
, regardless of whether
g\in H
or
u\in W
.
We will actually prove the following stronger result, analogous to (
6.8
):
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\Gamma
, one has

(8.8)

\lim_{X\in\gamma(x)\atop{\delta(X)\to 0}}\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|u(Z)-g(x)|\,dm(Z)=0.

Since we only want to prove (
8.8
) for
\mu
-a.e. point, we can restrict to
the case when
x
is a Lebesgue point of
g
, that is, when (
8.1
) is satisfied.

Fix such an
x\in\Gamma
and
X\in\gamma(x)
.
We write
B
for
B(X,\delta(X)/2)
. Then

\fint_{B}|u(Z)-g(x)|\,dm(Z)\leq\frac{1}{m(B)}\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{W}}(B)}\int_{R}|u(Z)-g(x)|\,dm(Z),

where
{\mathcal{W}}(B)
is the set of dyadic cubes
I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
that meet
B
. It is easy to check that
{\mathcal{W}}(B)
contains a finite number of cubes
I^{\prime}
(the number is bounded uniformly in
X\in\Omega
),

for which
\ell(I^{\prime})\approx\delta(X)
, and then, by (
H4
),
m(I^{\prime})\approx m(B)
.
So (
8.8
) will be proven if we can establish that

(8.9)

\fint_{I^{\prime}}|u(Z)-g(x)|\,dm(Z)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{ as $\delta(I^{\prime})\to 0$},

where we restrict to dyadic cubes
I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
x\in KI^{\prime}
for some large enough constant
K:=K(n)
.
Recall from the definition (
8.3
) of
u=\Ext g
that
u(Z)=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}\varphi_{I}(Z)y_{I}
.
We observed earlier that
the sum is locally finite on
I^{\prime}
, and the cubes
I
for which
\varphi_{I}

does not vanish identically on
I

are such that
I^{\prime}\subset 6I
and
\ell(I)/\ell(I^{\prime})\in\{1/2,1,2\}
. We deduce that any such
I
satisfies
B_{I}\subset K^{\prime}B_{I^{\prime}}\subset B(x,K^{\prime\prime}\delta(I^{\prime}))
and
B_{I^{\prime}}\subset K^{\prime}B_{I}\subset B(x,K^{\prime\prime}\delta(I^{\prime}))
, and then by (
H3
) that
\mu(B_{I})\approx\mu(B(x,K^{\prime\prime}\delta(I^{\prime})))
. The conclusion is that

\begin{split}\fint_{I^{\prime}}|u(Z)-g(x)|\,dm(Z)&=\fint_{I^{\prime}}\bigg|\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}:\,2I\cap 2I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset}\varphi_{I}(Z)\fint_{B_{I}}[g(z)-g(x)]\,d\mu(z)\bigg|\,dm(Z)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}:\,2I\cap 2I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset}\fint_{B_{I}}|g(z)-g(x)|\,d\mu(z)\\
&\lesssim\fint_{B(x,K^{\prime\prime}\delta(I^{\prime}))}|g(z)-g(x)|\,d\mu(z)\end{split}

because the number of
I\in{\mathcal{W}}
that verify
2I\cap 2I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
is uniformly
bounded.
Thanks to (
8.1
), the right-hand side above converges to 0 as
\delta(I^{\prime})\to 0
.
The claims (
8.9
), (
8.8
), and then (
8.6
), follow.

Now, we want to show that for
g\in H
,
u\in W
and even
\|u\|_{W}\lesssim\|g\|_{H}
.
Recall that
u
is smooth on
\Omega
because the sum in (
8.3
) is locally
finite, so
u
is locally integrable in
\Omega
, and its distribution derivative is locally
integrable too, and given by

(8.10)

\nabla u(X)=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}y_{I}\nabla\varphi_{I}(X)=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}}[y_{I}-y_{I^{\prime}}]\nabla\varphi_{I}(X),

where
I^{\prime}
is any cube (that may depend on
X
but not on
I
), and the identity holds because

\sum_{I}\nabla\varphi_{I}=\nabla(\sum_{I}\varphi_{I})=\nabla 1=0
.
So we only need to show that

\|u\|_{W}\leq C\|g\|_{H}<+\infty
.
First decompose
\|u\|^{2}_{W}
as

(8.11)

\|u\|^{2}_{W}=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}}\int_{I^{\prime}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm.

For the moment, we fix
I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
and
X\in I^{\prime}
, and
get a bound on
