|\nabla u(X)|
. If
{\mathcal{W}}(I^{\prime})
denotes the sets of dyadic cubes
I\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
2I
meets
I^{\prime}
, then

|\nabla u(X)|\leq\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}(I^{\prime})}|y_{I}-y_{I^{\prime}}||\nabla\varphi_{I}(X)|\lesssim\ell(I^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{W}}(I^{\prime})}|y_{I}-y_{I^{\prime}}|

because
\nabla\varphi_{I}\lesssim\delta(I)^{-1}\approx\delta(I^{\prime})^{-1}
. We use the definition of
y_{I},y_{I^{\prime}}
, the facts that
I\subset 6I^{\prime}
and
\delta(I)\approx\delta(I^{\prime})
to obtain that

\begin{split}|y_{I}-y_{I^{\prime}}|&\leq\fint_{B_{I}}\fint_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\\
&\leq\left(\fint_{B_{I}}\fint_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{-1}\left(\int_{100B_{I^{\prime}}}\int_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

The combination of the last two computations gives

|\nabla u(X)|\lesssim\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{-1}\ell(I^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\int_{100B_{I^{\prime}}}\int_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

since
{\mathcal{W}}(I^{\prime})
contains at most
2\cdot 12^{n}
elements,
and then

\int_{I^{\prime}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm\lesssim\ell(I^{\prime})^{-2}\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{-2}m(I^{\prime})\int_{100B_{I^{\prime}}}\int_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y).

We inject the above estimate in (
8.11
) and
obtain that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{W}^{2}&\lesssim\sum_{I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}}\ell(I^{\prime})^{-2}\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{-2}m(I^{\prime})\int_{100B_{I^{\prime}}}\int_{B_{I^{\prime}}}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)\\
&\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}h(x,y)d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y),\end{split}

where

h(x,y):=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}}\ell(I^{\prime})^{-2}\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{-2}m(I^{\prime}){\mathds{1}}_{100B_{I^{\prime}}}(x){\mathds{1}}_{B_{I^{\prime}}}(y).

Fix
x,y\in\Gamma
. Observe that if
I^{\prime}
satisfies
(x,y)\in 100B_{I^{\prime}}\times B_{I^{\prime}}
,
then by (
H3
),
\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})\approx\mu(B(x,\ell(I^{\prime})))
and by (
H4
),
m(I^{\prime})\approx m(B(x,\ell(I^{\prime}))\cap\Omega)
. Hence by (
2.6
)

\frac{m(I^{\prime})}{\ell(I^{\prime})^{2}\mu(B_{I^{\prime}})^{2}}\approx\frac{\rho(x,\ell(I^{\prime}))}{\ell(I^{\prime})\mu(B(x,\ell(I^{\prime})))}\,.

Under the same assumption on
I^{\prime}
, we also have
|x-y|\leq 101\ell(I^{\prime})
, so by
(
H4
) again,

\mu(B(x,\ell(I^{\prime})))^{-1}\lesssim\mu(B(x,|x-y|))^{-1}
. In addition, (
H5
) gives that

\rho(x,\ell(I^{\prime}))\lesssim\rho(x,|x-y|)\left(\frac{\ell(I^{\prime})}{|x-y|}\right)^{1-\epsilon}

where
\epsilon:=C_{5}^{-1}>0

(notice that if
\ell(I^{\prime})\leq|x-y|\leq 101\ell(I^{\prime})
, we don’t need
to use (
H5
), just the doubling properties). All this yields

h(x,y)\lesssim\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)}{\mu(B(x,|x-y|))|x-y|^{1-\epsilon}}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}\atop\delta(I^{\prime})\geq|x-y|/101}\ell(I^{\prime})^{-\epsilon}\,{\mathds{1}}_{B_{I^{\prime}}}(y).

Since
B_{I^{\prime}}\subset\kappa I^{\prime}

for some constant
\kappa:=\kappa(n)>1
that does not depend on
I^{\prime}
,
we see that for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, the number of dyadic cubes
I^{\prime}
such that
\ell(I^{\prime})=2^{j}

and
y\in B_{I^{\prime}}
is uniformly bounded.
Together with the fact that
\delta(I^{\prime})\approx\ell(I^{\prime})
, this yields

\sum_{I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}\atop\ell(I^{\prime})\geq|x-y|/101}\ell(I^{\prime})^{-\epsilon}\,{\mathds{1}}_{B_{I^{\prime}}}(y)\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(2^{k}|x-y|)^{-\epsilon}\lesssim|x-y|^{-\epsilon}.

Altogether,

h(x,y)\lesssim\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)}{\mu(B(x,|x-y|))|x-y|}\approx\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}

by (
2.6
) and thus

\|u\|_{W}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y):=\|g\|_{H}^{2}

as desired (see the definition (
6.5
)). Theorem
8.5
follows.

\Box

Lemma 8.12
.

For every
g\in H
, we can find a sequence
(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
whose restrictions to
\Gamma
(we still call them
g_{k}
) belong to
H
and such that
(g_{k})_{k}
converges to
g
in
H
,
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
and
\mu
-a.e. pointwise.

Remark 8.13
.

The above density result (whose proof doesn’t use Theorem
8.5
) actually entails the Lebesgue density result given as (
8.1
).
The proof of this implication uses
maximal functions, is classical, and is left to the reader.

Proof
.  For the density of smooth functions, we are given
g\in H
and we want to approximate it with smooth functions. The simplest way
for us to construct functions
g_{k}
will be to use our dyadic decompositions

\mathbb{D}_{k}
of
\Gamma
, but coverings of
\Gamma
with balls of radius
2^{-k}
would work as well.
We associate to
\mathbb{D}_{k}
a collection of smooth functions
\{\varphi_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}
such that
\varphi_{Q}
is supported in
2Q
,
\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\varphi_{Q}=1
near
\Gamma
, and
\|\nabla\varphi_{Q}\|_{\infty}\leq C2^{k}
.
Finally we set

(8.14)

g_{k}(x)=\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\varphi_{Q}(x)y_{Q}

for
x\in\Gamma
, where we take
y_{Q}=\fint_{2Q}g(y)d\mu(y)
.
It is obvious that
g_{k}
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(the sum in (
8.14
) is locally finite).
We shall prove now that

(8.15)

\|g-g_{k}\|^{2}_{H}\leq CJ(k),\ \text{ where }J(k)=\iint_{x,y\in\Gamma\,;\,|x-y|\leq 2^{3-k}}{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}\over m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y).

Notice that
J_{k}
is a subintegral of
\|g\|_{H}^{2}
, where the domain of integration
decreases to the empty set when
k
tends to
+\infty
; thus

\lim_{k\to+\infty}J(k)=0
,
and as soon as we prove (
8.15
), we will get that
g_{k}\in H

and
g_{k}
tends to
g
in
H
; the density of smooth functions in
H
will follow.

We need some notation. Set
h_{k}=g-g_{k}
and for
Q\in\mathbb{D}

(8.16)

{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}=\big\{(x,y)\in Q\times 2Q\,;|x-y|\geq\ell(Q)/2\big\}.

Every
pair
of points

(x,y)\in\Gamma^{2}
lies in at least one
{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
: choose
j

such that
2^{-j-1}\leq|x-y|<2^{-j}
, let
Q=Q^{j}(x)
be the element of
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
,
and observe that
y\in B(x,2^{-j})\subset 2Q
, hence
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
.
Because of this,

(8.17)

\|g-g_{k}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}}T_{Q}^{k},

where

(8.18)

\displaystyle T_{Q}^{k}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}{\rho(x,|x-y|)^{2}|h_{k}(x)-h_{k}(y)|^{2}\over m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}{|h_{k}(x)-h_{k}(y)|^{2}\over\mu(B(x,|x-y|))}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)}{|x-y|}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(Q)}{\ell(Q)}\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}\frac{|h_{k}(x)-h_{k}(y)|^{2}}{\mu(2Q)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

because
|x-y|\approx\ell(Q)
, and by (
H3
)–(
H4
) and the definitions(
2.6
) and (
5.17
).

We start the estimate of
T_{Q}^{k}
with
the large scales,
where we shall merely estimate the size of
h_{k}
on
\Gamma
.
Let us check that for any cube
Q^{*}
such that

\ell(Q^{*})\geq 2^{-k}
,

(8.19)

\int_{2Q^{*}}|h_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)\leq C\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))}\int_{z\in B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x).

We shall first estimate the contribution of a
a given cube
Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*})
, where

(8.20)

\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*}):=\{Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k},\,2Q_{0}\cap 2Q^{*}\neq\emptyset\},

and then sum. So let
Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*})

be given.
We estimate

(8.21)

\displaystyle a(Q_{0})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}|h_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)=\int_{2Q_{0}}|g(x)-g_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}|g(x)-\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\varphi_{Q}(x)y_{Q}|^{2}d\mu(x)=\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\big|\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\varphi_{Q}(x)[g(x)-y_{Q}]\big|^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\varphi_{Q}(x)|g(x)-y_{Q}|^{2}d\mu(x)\leq\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})}|g(x)-y_{Q}|^{2}d\mu(x)

by (
8.14
), the fact that
\sum_{Q}\varphi_{Q}(x)=1
, Cauchy-Schwarz for a finite average, and the fact that
\varphi_{Q}(x)=0
outside of
2Q
.
Notice that when
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})
and
x\in 2Q_{0}
,

(8.22)

\displaystyle|g(x)-y_{Q}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\fint_{z\in 2Q}[g(x)-g(z)]d\mu(z)\right|^{2}\leq\fint_{z\in 2Q}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\fint_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)

because for
x\in 2Q_{0}
, the fact that
2Q_{0}\cap 2Q\neq\emptyset
implies that

2Q\subset B(x,2^{2-k})
, and
\mu
is doubling by (
H3
).
Hence, since the number of element in
\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})
is bounded,

(8.23)

\displaystyle a(Q_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})}\fint_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))}\int_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x).

Now, we sum on
Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*})
and get that

(8.24)

\displaystyle\int_{2Q^{*}}|h_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*})}\int_{2Q_{0}}|h_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)\leq\sum_{Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}a(Q_{0})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q^{*})}\int_{x\in 2Q_{0}}\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))}\int_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))}\int_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)

because the
2Q_{0}
cover
Q^{*}

(actually, they cover
2Q^{*}
), are contained in
8Q^{\ast}
,
and have bounded covering; the estimate (
8.19
) follows.

Recall that
since
\ell(Q^{\ast})\geq 2^{-k}
,
(
H5
) and Lemma
5.16
imply that

\frac{2^{2-k}}{\rho(x,2^{2-k})}\lesssim\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{\rho(Q^{*})}\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{2^{-k}}\right)^{-\epsilon}

(with
\epsilon=C_{5}^{-1}
as usual) and, for

z\in B(x,2^{2-k})\cap\Gamma
,

\frac{\rho(x,2^{2-k})}{2^{2-k}}\lesssim\frac{\rho(x,|x-z|)}{|x-z|}\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{|x-z|}\right)^{-\epsilon}\lesssim\frac{\rho(x,|x-z|)}{|x-z|}.

We return to (
8.19
), use the two estimates above and the fact that

\mu(B(x,|x-z|))\leq C\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))
when
B(x,2^{2-k})
, and get that

(8.25)

\begin{split}\int_{2Q^{*}}|h_{k}|^{2}d\mu&\lesssim\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\frac{\rho(x,2^{2-k})}{2^{2-k}\mu(B(x,2^{2-k}))}\frac{2^{2-k}}{\rho(x,2^{2-k})}\int_{z\in\Gamma\cap B(x,2^{2-k})}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)\\
&\lesssim\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{\rho(Q^{*})}\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{2^{-k}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\int_{z\in B(x,2^{2-k})}\frac{\rho(x,|x-z|)|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}}{\mu(B(x,|x-z|))|x-z|}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)\\
&=\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{\rho(Q^{*})}\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{2^{-k}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\int_{z\in B(x,2^{2-k})}\frac{\rho(x,|x-z|)^{2}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-z|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(z)d\mu(x)\end{split}

where the last
estimate comes from the definition of
\rho
.
The right-hand side tends to
0
(for any fixed
Q^{\ast}
) when
k
tends to
+\infty
,
so (
8.25
)
means that
(g_{k})
converges to
g
in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
.

Let us return to
T^{k}_{Q^{*}}
,
starting with the case when
\ell(Q^{*})\geq 2^{-k}
.
Observe that by (
8.18
) and (
8.25
)

(8.26)

\displaystyle T^{k}_{Q^{*}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(Q^{*})}{\ell(Q^{*})}\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q^{*}}}\frac{|h_{k}(x)-h_{k}(y)|^{2}}{\mu(2Q^{*})}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(Q^{*})}{\ell(Q^{*})}\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q^{*}}}\frac{|h_{k}(x)|^{2}+|h_{k}(y)|^{2}}{\mu(2Q^{*})}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(Q^{*})}{\ell(Q^{*})}\int_{2Q^{*}}|h_{k}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{*})}{2^{-k}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\int_{x\in 8Q^{*}}\int_{z\in B(x,2^{2-k})}\frac{\rho(x,|x-z|)^{2}|g(x)-g(z)|^{2}}{m(B(x,|x-z|)\cap\Omega)}d\mu(z)d\mu(x).

We now sum this over
Q^{\ast}
such that
\ell(Q^{*})\geq 2^{-k}
. Fix
x,z\in\Gamma
;
for each generation
j
,
j\leq k
, there are at most
C
cubes
Q^{\ast}
,
Q^{*}\in\mathbb{D}_{j}
,
such that
x\in 8Q^{*}
. Therefore,

(8.27)

\sum_{j\leq k}\sum_{Q^{*}\in\mathbb{D}_{j}}T_{Q^{*}}^{k}\lesssim\sum_{j\geq k}2^{(j-k)\epsilon}J(k)\lesssim J(k),

where
J(k)
is as in (
8.15
). This part fits with (
8.15
) (see (
8.17
)).

For the small scales we shall use the regularity of
g_{k}
. That is, for
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-k}
, we recall that

h_{k}=g-g_{k}
, hence, by the first part of (
8.18
),
T_{Q}^{k}\leq 2U_{Q}^{k}+2V_{Q}^{k}
, where

(8.28)

V_{Q}^{k}:=\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}{|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|^{2}\over\mu(B(x,|x-y|))}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)}{|x-y|}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

and

(8.29)

W_{Q}^{k}:=\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}{|g(x)-g(y)|^{2}\over\mu(B(x,|x-y|))}\frac{\rho(x,|x-y|)}{|x-y|}d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

are the analogues of
T_{Q}^{k}
for
g_{k}
and
g
.

We deduce from the definition (
8.16
) that
\ell(Q)/2\leq|x-y|\leq 2\ell(Q)
when
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
,
so a given pair
(x,y)
cannot lie in more than
C
sets
{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
,
Q\in\mathbb{D}
, so (
8.29
) yields

(8.30)

\sum_{j>k}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{j}}W_{Q}^{k}\lesssim J(k).

As for the
V_{Q}^{k}
, we decide to estimate
|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|
rather brutally. Again we localize at the scale
2^{-k}
. Let
Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}
be given,
and then pick
x\in Q_{0}
and
y\in 2Q_{0}
.
We want to estimate
|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|
in terms of

(8.31)

b(Q_{0}):=\fint_{y\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in 4Q_{0}}|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(y)d\mu(z).

By (
8.14
),

(8.32)

\displaystyle|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}[\varphi_{Q}(x)-\varphi_{Q}(y)]y_{Q}\Big|=\Big|\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}[\varphi_{Q}(x)-\varphi_{Q}(y)][y_{Q}-y_{Q_{0}}]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}|\varphi_{Q}(x)-\varphi_{Q}(y)|\,|y_{Q}-y_{Q_{0}}|

because
\sum_{Q}\varphi_{Q}(x)=\sum_{Q}\varphi_{Q}(y)=1
. Notice that if

|\varphi_{Q}(x)-\varphi_{Q}(y)|>0
,
then
x
or
y
lies in
2Q
,
hence
Q
lies in the collection
\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})
of (
8.20
). For such balls,

(8.33)

\displaystyle|y_{Q}-y_{Q_{0}}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\fint_{y\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in 2Q}[g(y)-g(z)]\,d\mu(z)\,d\mu(y)\Big|\leq\fint_{y\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in 2Q}|g(y)-g(z)|\,d\mu(z)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\{\fint_{y\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in 2Q}|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(y)d\mu(z)\Big\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big\{\fint_{y\in 2Q_{0}}\fint_{z\in 4Q_{0}}|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}d\mu(y)d\mu(z)\Big\}^{1/2}=Cb(Q_{0})^{1/2},

where
b(Q_{0})
is as in (
8.31
), and
because
2Q\subset 4Q_{0}

for
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})
, and by (
H3
).
Recall that
|\nabla\varphi_{Q}|\leq C{2^{k}}
. Since there are no more than
C
cubes
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})
,
(
8.32
) yields

(8.34)

|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|^{2}\lesssim|x-y|^{2}2^{2k}\sup_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(Q_{0})}|y_{Q}-y_{Q_{0}}|^{2}\lesssim|x-y|^{2}2^{2k}b(Q_{0}).

We shall use this soon, but for the moment let us estimate a given

V_{Q}^{k}
,
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-k}
.
Observe that by (
H3
)–(
H4
) and the definitions(
2.6
) and (
5.17
), and as in the last part of (
8.18
),

V_{Q}^{k}\lesssim\frac{\rho(Q)}{\ell(Q)}\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}\frac{|g_{k}(x)-g_{k}(y)|^{2}}{\mu(2Q)}d\mu(x)d\mu(y).

Then let
Q_{0}
denote the cube of
\mathbb{D}_{k}
that contains
x
, then

x\in Q_{0}
and
y\in 2Q_{0}
when
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
, so we can use
(
8.34
) and get that

(8.35)

\displaystyle V_{Q}^{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(Q)}{\ell(Q)}\iint_{{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}2^{2k}b(Q_{0})}{\mu(2Q)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x).

Since

|x-y|\approx\ell(Q)
when
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{Q}
,

(8.36)

\displaystyle V_{Q}^{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{2k}\ell(Q)\rho(Q)\int_{x\in Q}\int_{y\in 2Q}\frac{b(Q_{0})}{\mu(2Q)}d\mu(y)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{2k}\ell(Q)\rho(Q)\mu(Q)b(Q_{0})

(8.37)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{2k}m(U^{*}_{Q})b(Q_{0})\approx m(U^{*}_{Q})\ell(Q_{0})^{-2}b(Q_{0})

by (
5.17
). We sum over the cubes
Q\subset Q_{0}
to obtain

\begin{split}\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}V_{Q}^{k}\lesssim 2^{2k}\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}m(U^{*}_{Q})b(Q_{0})\approx 2^{2k}m(T_{Q_{0}})b(Q_{0})\approx\frac{\rho(Q_{0})\mu(Q_{0})}{\ell(Q_{0})}b(Q_{0})\end{split}

because the
U^{*}_{Q}
are contained in
T_{Q_{0}}
(see (
5.12
) and (
5.11
))
and have bounded overlap.
We now use the definition (
8.31
) of
b(Q_{0})
and the doubling property (
H3
) to get

\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}V_{Q}^{k}\lesssim\int_{2Q_{0}}\int_{4Q_{0}}\frac{\rho(Q_{0})|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}}{\mu(4Q_{0})\ell(Q_{0})}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(z).

Observe that
\mu(B(y,|y-z|))\leq C\mu(4Q_{0})
.
Besides, due to (
H5
),

\frac{\rho(Q_{0})}{\ell(Q_{0})}\lesssim\frac{\rho(y,|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\left(\frac{\ell(Q_{0})}{|x-z|}\right)^{-\epsilon}\lesssim\frac{\rho(y,|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\qquad\text{ for $(y,z)\in 2Q_{0}\times 4Q_{0}$}.

It follows that

\begin{split}\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}V_{Q}^{k}&\lesssim\int_{2Q_{0}}\int_{4Q_{0}}\frac{\rho(y,|y-z|)|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}}{\mu(B(y,|y-z|))|y-z|}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(z)\\
&\quad=\int_{2Q_{0}}\int_{4Q_{0}}\frac{\rho(y,|y-z|)^{2}|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}}{m(B(y,|y-z|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(z)\end{split}

by (
2.6
). Notice that
|y-z|\leq 8\ell(Q_{0})=2^{3-k}
when
y\in 2Q_{0}

and
z\in 4Q_{0}
. Also, for a given pair
(y,z)\in\Gamma\times\Gamma
, with

y\neq z
, the set of cubes
Q_{0}
of generation
k
for which
y\in Q_{0}
and
z\in 2Q_{0}

has less than
C
elements; because of this,

(8.38)

\begin{split}\sum_{j>k}\sum_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{j}}V_{Q}^{k}&=\sum_{Q_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{k}}\sum_{Q\subset Q_{0}}V_{Q}^{k}\\
&\lesssim\iint_{x,y\in\Gamma\,;\,|x-y|\leq 2^{3-k}}\frac{\rho(y,|y-z|)^{2}|g(y)-g(z)|^{2}}{m(B(y,|y-z|)\cap\Omega)}\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(z)=J(k)\end{split}

The combination of (
8.27
), (
8.30
), and (
8.38
) gives that

\sum_{j\geq 0}T_{j}^{k}\lesssim J(k)
, and hence by (
8.17
)

\|g-g_{k}\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{j}T_{j}^{k}\leq CJ(k)
, as needed for (
8.15
).

This completes our proof of the density of smooth functions
in
H
.
The fact that
g_{k}
converges to
g
in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
has been shown in (
8.25
), and up to a subsequence, we can also assume that
g_{k}
also converges to
g

\mu
-a.e. on
\Gamma
. Lemma
8.12
follows.

\Box

9.
Completeness of
W
and density of smooth functions

In all of this section,

(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H6
).

First
we talk about
completeness.
In fact
W
cannot really be a Banach space, because
\|.\|_{W}
is not a norm on
W
,
only a semi-norm. Thus
we need to quotient
W
by the functions
u
such that
\|u\|_{W}=0
, that is,
thanks to Lemma
4.10
, by the constant functions. So we work
with the homogeneous space
\dot{W}
defined as the quotient space
W/\mathbb{R}
- i.e. element of
\dot{W}
are classes
\dot{u}:=\{u+c\}_{c\in\mathbb{R}}
- and outfitted with the quotient norm that we still call
\|.\|_{W}
by notation abuse.

Lemma 9.1
.

Then the quotient space
\dot{W}=W/\mathbb{R}
, equipped with the quotient norm

\|\cdot\|_{W}
, is complete.

Also, ,
if a sequence

\{u_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
in

W
and a function
u_{\infty}\in W
are such that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|u_{k}-u_{\infty}\|_{W}=0
,
then there exists constants
c_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
u_{k}-c_{k}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.

Remark 9.2
.

The measure
\mu
does not
play any role in this lemma, and we might be able to remove the assumption (
H3
). However, it will be convenient to use the dyadic decomposition (and Theorem
5.24
) given in Section
5
.

Proof
.  We follow the arguments of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 5.1]
. Let
\{\dot{u}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\dot{W}
. We need to show that

(i)

for every sequence
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, with
u_{k}\in\dot{u}_{k}
, there exists
u\in W
and
\{c_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
u_{k}-c_{k}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)

and
\nabla u=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\nabla u_{k}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;m)
;

(ii)

if
u_{\infty},u^{\prime}_{\infty}\in W

are such that there exist
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{u^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

u_{k},u^{\prime}_{k}\in\dot{u}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u_{k}-u_{\infty}\|_{W}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u^{\prime}_{k}-u^{\prime}_{\infty}\|_{W}=0,

then
\dot{u}_{\infty}=\dot{u}^{\prime}_{\infty}
.

First we prove (ii).
Let
u_{\infty}
,
u^{\prime}_{\infty}
,
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and

\{u^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be as in (ii).
Notice that
\nabla(u_{k}-u^{\prime}_{k})=0
(in the sense of
W
) because
u_{k}
and
u^{\prime}_{k}

represent the same class; by Lemma
4.10
this also means that
u_{k}-u^{\prime}_{k}
is a constant.
By assumption,
u_{\infty}\in W
and
\nabla u_{\infty}
(in the sense of
W
) is the limit of

\nabla u_{k}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;m)
. Similarly,
\nabla u^{\prime}_{\infty}
is the limit of
\nabla u^{\prime}_{k}
.
Hence
\nabla u_{\infty}=\nabla u^{\prime}_{\infty}
in
L^{2}
, and again this means that

u_{\infty}-u^{\prime}_{\infty}
is constant; (ii) follows.

Let us turn to the proof of (i).
Pick a central point
x_{0}\in\Gamma
and, for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, denote by
Q^{j}

the only cube in
\mathbb{D}_{j}
that contains
x_{0}
.
Observe that when
j
tends to
-\infty
,
the sets
T_{2Q^{j}}
defined by (
5.12
) grow to
\Omega
, i.e., eventually
contain any compact subset of
\Omega
. Thus the convergence in each
L^{1}(T_{2Q^{j}},m)

implies the convergence in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.

We shall restrict our attention to
j\leq 0
.
Observe that because of Theorem
5.24
(Poincaré’s inequality), applied
with
p=2
and
D=T_{2Q^{j}}
,
there exists constants
C_{j}:=C(C_{1},C_{2},C_{4},C_{6},j)
such that for any
f\in W
,

(9.3)

\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|f-f^{0}|\,dm\lesssim C_{j}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla f|^{2}\,dm,

where we can take
f^{0}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}f\,dm
, i.e., take the fixed set
E=U^{*}_{Q^{0}}
,
which is contained in
T_{2Q^{j}}
because
j\geq 0
.

Now let
u_{k}\in\dot{u}_{k}
be as in the statement, and set

(9.4)

c_{k}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}u_{k}\,dm.

By (
9.3
),
(u_{k}-c_{k})_{k}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{1}(T_{Q^{j}})
for each integer
j\leq 0
. Hence, there exists
u^{j}\in L^{1}(T_{Q^{j}})
such that
u_{k}-c_{k}
converges to
u^{j}
in
L^{1}(T_{Q^{j}})
.
By uniqueness of the limit,

u^{j}=u^{i}
almost everywhere on
T_{Q^{j}}\cap T_{Q^{i}}
,
so we can define
u\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
such that
u=u^{j}
a.e. on
T_{Q^{j}}
.

It remains to check that
u\in W

and
u_{k}\to u
in
W
.
Since
u_{k}-c_{k}\in W
, Definition
4.1
gives us a smooth function

\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W

such that

(9.5)

\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}|u_{k}-c_{k}-\varphi_{k}|\,dm\leq\frac{1}{k}

and

(9.6)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{k}-\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}\,dm\leq\frac{1}{k}.

Set
d_{k}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q_{0}}}\varphi_{k}\,dm
.
By (
9.5
) and (
9.4
),

(9.7)

|d_{k}|\leq\frac{1}{k}+\Big|\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}(u_{k}-c_{k})\,dm\Big|=\frac{1}{k}.

We are now ready to prove that
\varphi_{k}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
. Write

\begin{split}\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|u-\varphi_{k}|\,dm&\leq\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|u-(u_{k}-c_{k})|\,dm+\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|(u_{k}-\varphi_{k})-(c_{k}-d_{k})|\,dm+|d_{k}|\\
&:=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}.\end{split}

The term
T_{1}
tends to 0 as
k\to\infty
because by construction

u_{k}-c_{k}\to u=u^{j}
in
L^{1}(T_{Q^{j}})
.
The term
T_{2}
tends also to
0
, thanks to (
9.3
) and (
9.6
). The term
T_{3}
converges to 0 because of (
9.7
). We conclude, since
T_{2Q^{j}}\uparrow\Omega
, that
\varphi_{k}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
; in particular

(9.8)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B}|\varphi_{k}-u|\,dm=0\quad\text{ for any ball $B$ satisfying $2B\subset\Omega$.}

In addition,
\{\dot{u}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

is a Cauchy sequence in
W
, and if we combine this fact with (
9.6
), we get that
\{\nabla\varphi_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
.
We conclude that there exists
v
such that

(9.9)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\varphi_{k}-v|^{2}\,dm=0.

We may now use our smooth functions
\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W

to check that
u\in W
, as in Definition
4.1
, and with the gradient
v
; indeed
(
4.3
) comes from (
9.8
), and (
4.4
) comes from (
9.9
).
Also,
u_{k}\to u
in
W
because
\nabla u_{k}\to v
in
L^{2}(m)
, by
by (
9.6
) and (
9.9
).
Lemma
9.1
follows.

\Box

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence
in
W
, i.e.,
\|u_{i}-u_{j}\|_{W}\to 0
as
i,j\to\infty
. If
u_{i}
converges to
u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, then
u\in W
and
\|u_{i}-u\|_{W}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
.

In connection with this result, we’ll
say that
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
u
in
W
and
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
if
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
W
as above and
u_{i}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.

Proof
.  Keep the same sets
Q^{j}

as in the proof of Lemma
9.1
and let
\{u_{i}\}
be as in the statement.
As before, by Definition of
u_{i}\in W
, we can find
\varphi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W

such that

(9.11)

\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}|u_{i}-\varphi_{i}|\,dm\leq\frac{1}{i}

and

(9.12)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{i}-\nabla\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,dm\leq\frac{1}{i}.

Set
c_{i}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q_{0}}}(u_{i}-\varphi_{i})\,dm
; by (
9.11
),

|c_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{i}
.
For each fixed
j\geq 0
, and as in the proof of Lemma
9.1
,

\begin{split}\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|u-\varphi_{i}|\,dm&\leq\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|u-u_{i}|\,dm+\fint_{T_{2Q^{j}}}|(u_{i}-\varphi_{i})-c_{i}|\,dm+|c_{i}|,\end{split}

which tends to
0
as
i\to\infty
. This
proves that
\varphi_{i}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.
Moreover, by (
9.12
) and the fact that
\{\nabla u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
,
\{\nabla\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
, hence there exists
v\in L^{2}(\Omega,m)
such that
\nabla\varphi_{i}\to v
in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
. These two convergences - the convergence in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
and the convergence of the gradients in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
- entail by definition of
W
that
u\in W
, and by uniqueness of the gradient that
v=\nabla u
. The lemma follows.

\Box

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of functions in
W
, and let
u\in W
. If
u_{i}
converges to
u
in both
W
and
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, then
\Tr u_{i}
converges to
\Tr u
in
H
and
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
.

Proof
.  The convergence of the traces in
H
is a direct consequence of Theorem
6.6

and the convergence of the initial sequence in
W
,
and actually does not need the convergence in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.

The convergence of the traces in

L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega,m)

is the analogue of (5.16) in
[
DFM2
]
.
Let us write
g
for
\Tr u
and
g_{i}
for
\Tr u_{i}
.
Since the operator
\Tr
is linear, without loss of generality, we can assume that
u\equiv 0

and thus
g\equiv 0
. So we want to prove that
\{g_{i}\}
converges to 0 in

L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
.
That is,
if
x_{0}
is a fixed point in
\Gamma
and
Q_{j}
is the only set in

\mathbb{D}_{j}
containing
x_{0}
, we want to show that for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, there exists
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(9.14)

\int_{Q_{j}}|g_{i}|^{2}d\mu\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{ for }i\geq i_{0}.

We introduce
g_{i}^{k}:=\Tr_{k}u_{i}
, where
\Tr_{k}
is defined in the proof of Theorem
6.6
.
Then

\begin{split}\int_{Q_{j}}|g_{i}|^{2}d\mu&\leq 2\int_{Q_{j}}|g_{i}-g_{i}^{k}|^{2}d\mu+2\int_{Q_{j}}|g_{i}^{k}|^{2}d\mu\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{-2j}\mu(Q_{j})}{m(U^{*}_{Q_{j}})}2^{-(k-j)\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dm+\int_{x\in Q_{j}}\left|\fint_{B_{x}^{k}}u_{i}\right|^{2}d\mu(x)\ :=T_{1}+T_{2}.\end{split}

where we invoke (
6.13
) for the second line
and
B_{x}^{k}
is the ball used to define
\Tr_{k}u(x)
.
The values of
\|u_{i}\|_{W}^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}\,dm
are uniformly bounded,
since
\{u_{i}\}
converges in
W
.
So we can fix
k
, so large that
that
T_{1}\leq\epsilon/2
uniformly in
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
As for
T_{2}
, observe that

T_{2}\leq C_{j,k}\int_{E_{j,k}}|u_{i}|

where
E_{j,k}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}:\,Q\subset Q_{j}}U^{*}_{Q}
is relatively compact
in
\Omega
. Since the values of
j,k
are fixed and
\{u_{i}\}
converges to
u\equiv 0
in
L^{1}(E_{j,k})
, we can choose
i_{0}
such that
T_{2}\leq\epsilon/2
for

i\geq i_{0}
.
Lemma
9.13
follows.

\Box

Lemma 9.15
.

The space

(9.16)

W_{0}:=\{u\in W,\,\Tr u=0\}

equipped with the scalar product
\left<u,v\right>_{W}:=\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm
(and the norm
\|.\|_{W}
) is a Hilbert space.

Proof
.  Notice that
\|.\|_{W}
is indeed a norm for
W_{0}
, because the only constant that is allowed in
W_{0}
is
0
. The proof will be
similar to
[
DFM2
, Lemma 5.2]
, and use Lemmas
9.1
and
9.13
.

Let
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence
in
W_{0}
.
By
the proof of
Lemma
9.1
, there exists
\bar{u}\in W
and a sequence of constants

c_{i}=\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}u_{i}\,dm
(see (
9.4
))
such that

(9.17)

u_{i}-c_{i}\to\bar{u}\quad\text{ in }L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m).

Let us prove that
\{c_{i}\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{R}
. For
i,j\geq 0
,

|c_{i}-c_{j}|\leq\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}|u_{i}-u_{j}|\,dm\leq C\Big\{\fint_{U^{*}_{Q^{0}}}|\nabla u_{i}-\nabla u_{j}|^{2}\,dm\Big\}^{1/2}\leq C\|u_{i}-u_{j}\|_{W},

where the second inequality is due to the Poincaré inequality (Theorem
5.24
).
So
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is indeed a Cauchy sequence, and thus converges to a constant

c\in\mathbb{R}
. Define
u\in W
as
\bar{u}-c
;
then (
9.17
) says that

u_{i}\to u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, but the convergence also holds in
W

by definition of
\bar{u}
. Lemma
9.13
implies now that
\Tr u
is the limit in

L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
of
\Tr u_{i}\equiv 0
, that is
\Tr u\equiv 0
and hence
u\in W_{0}
.
The lemma follows.

\Box

Recall from Lemma
6.21
that
u\varphi\in W
when
u\in W
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
and that we have the product rule
\nabla(u\varphi)=\varphi\nabla u+u\nabla\varphi
for
its derivative. Also, the trace of
u\varphi
is
\varphi Tru
. We can use this to prove that

C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
is dense in
W_{0}
, as in the following lemma.

Lemma 9.18
.

The completion of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
for the norm
\|.\|_{W}
is
W_{0}
.

Moreover, if
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open set and
u\in W
is compactly supported in
E\cap\overline{\Omega}
, then
u
can be approximated in the norm
\|.\|_{W}
by functions in
C^{\infty}_{0}(E\cap\Omega)
.

Proof
.  This result is entirely similar to
[
DFM2
, Lemma 5.5]
and we refer to it for a complete proof. The main steps are:

(i)

we use cut-off functions
\varphi_{r}
to approach
u\in W_{0}
by functions that are equal to 0 on
\Gamma_{r}:=\{X\in\Omega,\,\delta(X)\leq r\}
,

(ii)

we use cut-off functions
\phi_{R}
to approach the functions
u\varphi_{r}
obtained in (i) by functions compactly supported in
\Omega
,

(iii)

we use a mollifier to smooth the functions
u\varphi_{r}\phi_{R}
.

And obviously, in order to deal with the functions
u\varphi_{r}
or
u\varphi_{r}\phi_{R}
,
we use in a crucial manner
the aforementioned Lemma
6.21
.

\Box

Lemma 9.19
.

The set
C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W
is dense in
W
.
That is, for any
u\in W
, there exists a sequence
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W

such that
\{u_{i}\}
converges to
u
pointwise a.e. and in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, and

\|u_{i}-u\|_{W}\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{ as }i\to+\infty.

Proof
.  In
[
DFM2
]
, the analogue of this result is given by
[
DFM2
, Lemma 5.3]
, but we cannot follow the same approach here (in
[
DFM2
]
, the functions we considered were in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and thus allowed us to simply use a mollifier).

However, most of the job is already done by Lemmas
8.12
and
9.18
.
We take
u\in W
, and we want to find a smooth approximating sequence
\{u_{i}\}
.
First write
u=v+w
where

w=\Ext\circ\Tr u\quad\text{ and }\quad v=u-w.

Theorems
6.6
and
8.5
imply that

w
- and thus
v
- lies in
W
. Moreover, thanks to (
8.6
),

\Tr w=\Tr u
and hence
\Tr v=0
;
that is,
v\in W_{0}
.

Thanks to Lemma
9.18
, we can find
a sequence
\{v_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}

in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)\subset C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W

such that
\|v_{i}-v\|_{W}
tends to
0
.
And since
W_{0}
continuously injects
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
(
by Theorem
7.1
), the sequence
\{v_{i}\}
converges also in

L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
and, up to a subsequence,
pointwise a.e.
This takes care of
v
.

We use Lemma
8.12
to approximate
\Tr u
by some functions
(g_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap H
. Then we construct
w_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
as
\Ext g_{i}
.
Thanks to Theorem
8.5
,
\|w_{i}-w\|_{W}
tends to
0

as
i
goes to
+\infty
. Besides it is easy to check from the definition of the extension operator
\Ext
that the convergence of
g_{i}
to
\Tr u
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
(also given by Lemma
8.12
) implies that
w_{i}
converges to
w
uniformly on an compact subsets of
\Omega
,
and thus also pointwise a.e.
and in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
.

If we set
u_{i}=v_{i}+w_{i}
, we showed above the right convergences
(in
W
,
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega)
, and a.e. pointwise) of
u_{i}
to
u
.
The only unproved fact is
that
w_{i}
is continuous
up to the boundary, that is
w_{i}\in C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.
We skip this part because it
is very classical (see for instance in Section VI.2.2 of

[
Ste
]
).

\Box

The next result states some basic properties of
the derivative of
f\circ u
when
u\in W
(chain rule), and the fact that

uv
lies in
W\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
as soon as

u
and
v
both
lie in
W\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

Lemma 9.20
.

The following properties hold:

(i)

Let
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
f^{\prime}
is bounded, and let
u\in W
. Then
f\circ u\in W
,

\nabla(f\circ u)=f^{\prime}(u)\nabla u,\quad\text{ and }\quad\Tr(f\circ u)=f\circ(\Tr u),

where the last two equalities hold in the
m
-a.e. and
\mu
-a.e. sense, respectively.

(ii)

Let
u,v\in W
. Then
\max\{u,v\}
and
\min\{u,v\}
lie in

W
,

\nabla\max\{u,v\}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla u(x)&\text{ if }u(x)\geq v(x)\\
\nabla v(x)&\text{ if }v(x)\geq u(x),\\
\end{array}\right.

\nabla\min\{u,v\}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla u(x)&\text{ if }u(x)\leq v(x)\\
\nabla v(x)&\text{ if }v(x)\leq u(x),\\
\end{array}\right.

\Tr(\max\{u,v\})=\max\{\Tr u,\Tr v\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\Tr(\min\{u,v\})=\min\{\Tr u,\Tr v\},

where the first two equalities hold
m
-a.e., and the last two
\mu
-a.e.

(iii)

If
\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{v_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are two sequences of functions in
W
that
converge
to
u
,
v\in W
both in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, pointwise a.e.,
and in
W
(that is,
\|u_{k}-u\|_{W}+\|v_{k}-v\|_{W}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
),
then
\max\{u_{k},v_{k}\}
and
\min\{u_{k},v_{k}\}
lie in
W
and converge to

\max\{u,v\}
and
\min\{u_{k},v_{k}\}
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, pointwise a.e.,
and in
W
. In addition,

\Tr\max\{u_{k},v_{k}\}
tends to
\max\{\Tr u,\Tr v\}
and

\Tr\min\{u_{k},v_{k}\}
tends to
\min\{\Tr u,\Tr v\}
, in both case in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
.

Proof
.  Point (i) and (ii) are the analogues of Lemmas 6.1 in
[
DFM2
]
. The proof is the same as in
[
DFM2
]
(which is itself based on the proof of results 1.18 to 1.23 in
[
HKM
]
), and strongly relies on Lemma
9.19
(the approximation of elements in
W
by smooth functions) and
Lemma
9.13
(the convergence in
W
implies the convergence of traces). The conclusion (iii) is an intermediate result for (ii), proved as (6.16) and (6.17) in
[
DFM2
]
.

\Box

Lemma 9.21
.

Let
u,v\in W\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Then
uv\in W\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, with

\nabla[uv]=v\nabla u+u\nabla v
, and
\Tr(uv)=\Tr u\cdot\Tr v
.

Proof
.  If
u
or
v
is the zero constant, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, we can divide

u
and
v
by their respective
L^{\infty}
norm, and thus, without loss of generality,
we can assume that
\|u\|_{\infty}=\|v\|_{\infty}=1
.

By Lemma
9.19
, we can find two sequences
\{\tilde{u}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\{\tilde{v}_{k}\in\mathbb{N}\}

in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)\cap C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W
such that
\tilde{u}_{k}\to u

and
\tilde{v}_{k}\to v
in
W
,
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, and pointwise a.e.
By
(iii) of Lemma
9.20
, the truncated functions

u_{k}:=\max\{-1,\min\{1,\tilde{u}_{k}\}\}\quad\text{ and }v_{k}:=\max\{-1,\min\{1,\tilde{v}_{k}\}\}

lie in
C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W
, are locally Lipschitz, and converge to respectively
u
and
v
in
W
,
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
, and pointwise a.e.

Since the derivative is a local object,
we can use (
4.5
) and the classical product rule to say that

\nabla[u_{k}v_{k}]=u_{k}\nabla v_{k}+v_{k}\nabla u_{k}.

We conclude by showing, as in the proof of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 6.3]
that
u_{k}v_{k}\to uv
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,m)
,
u_{k}\nabla v_{k}+v_{k}\nabla u_{k}\to u\nabla v+v\nabla u
in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
, and
u_{k}v_{k}=\Tr(u_{k}v_{k})\to\Tr u\cdot\Tr v
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Gamma,\mu)
. The lemma follows then from Lemma
9.10
and Theorem
6.6
.

\Box

10.
The localized versions
W_{r}(E)
of our energy space
W

The aim of this short section is to define local versions of
W
, which will be useful to study
local solutions to our degenerate elliptic equations.
As in the previous section, we assume throughout that
(\Omega,m,\mu)

satisfies (
H1
)–(
H6
).

In general, we want to localize
W
with an open set
E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we set

(10.1)

E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega},

and define the space of functions
W_{r}(E)
by

(10.2)

W_{r}(E):=\{u\in L^{1}_{loc}(E\cap\Omega,m):\,\varphi u\in W\text{ for all }\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E^{\prime})\}.

It is natural to call this space
W_{r}(E)
, as opposed to
W_{r}(E^{\prime})
, because it does not depend
on the part of
E^{\prime}
that leaves away from
\overline{\Omega}
. But there is an important special case,
when
E^{\prime}\subset\Omega
and so
E=E^{\prime}
is an open subset of
\Omega
.
In this case, the information that
f\in W_{r}(E)
does
not give any control on
f
at the boundary
\partial E
(which may intersect
\Gamma
),
and
W_{r}(E)

will be mainly used to give interior estimates for weak solutions (that will be defined soon).
In the general case,
E
may contain pieces of the boundary
\Gamma
, and then the fact that

f\in W_{r}(E)
gives some information on the behavior of
f
near
E\cap\Gamma
, in the same
way as the fact that
f\in W
gives a global information on
f
near
\Gamma
.
For instance, we can can take for
E
(the interior in
\overline{\Omega}
of) the set

T_{Q}\cup Q
, for some dyadic cube
Q\in\mathbb{D}
.
Obviously

W_{r}(E)\subset W_{r}(F)
when

F\subset E
, and in particular
W_{r}(T_{Q}\cup Q)\subset W_{r}(T_{Q})
.
In addition, if
F\subsetneq E
, it is not very hard to find a function

u\in W_{r}(F)\setminus W_{r}(E)
- just make
|\nabla u(X)|
blows up when
X

gets close to
E\setminus F
- and thus the local spaces
W_{r}(E)
are all different.
Thus for instance

W\subsetneq W_{r}(\overline{\Omega})\subsetneq W_{r}(\Omega),

smooth functions on
\Omega
that possibly explode along
\Gamma
lie in the last space,
while they only lie in
W_{r}(\overline{\Omega})
when they are locally controlled
near
\Gamma
, and they only lie in
W
when in addition their gradient lies in
L^{2}(\Omega,m)
.

Functions in
W_{r}(E)
are not necessarily in
L^{1}_{loc}(E)
(see Section
4
where we defined
W
). They still have the a notion of gradient - that may be different from the distributional gradient - inherited from
W
. Indeed, if
E^{\prime}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
, consider
K
any compact subset of
E^{\prime}
and take
\varphi_{K}\in C^{\infty}(E^{\prime})
such that
\varphi_{K}=1
on
K
, then we construct the
W
-gradient of
u\in W_{r}(E)
on
K
as the
W
-gradient of
\varphi_{K}u
. As an easy consequence, for
u\in W_{r}(E)
, we have
\nabla u\in L^{2}_{loc}(E,m)

(where in fact we just integrate on
E\cap\Omega
,
but local means in terms of the open set
E^{\prime}
, or
E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
)
and then
u\in L^{2}_{loc}(E,m)
by Theorem
5.24
.
These observations are summarized in the next lemma.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
, for some open set
E^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then every function
u\in W_{r}(E)
lies in

L^{1}_{loc}(E,m)
, and its gradient lies in
L^{2}_{loc}(E,m)
.

Remark 10.4
.

We don’t have many doubts that the reverse inclusion

(10.5)

W_{r}(E)\supset\{u\in L^{1}_{loc}(E,m):\,\nabla u\in L^{2}_{loc}(E,m)\}.

also holds.
The idea of the proof of (
10.5
) would be to take
u\in L^{1}_{loc}(E)

that satisfies
\nabla u\in L^{2}_{loc}(E,m)
,
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E^{\prime})
. We would set then
K^{\prime}:=\supp\,\varphi
which has a smooth boundary, and we would say that
u|_{K^{\prime}}
can be extended to a function
\bar{u}\in W
such that
\bar{u}=u
a.e. on
K^{\prime}
. Then we would use Lemma
6.21
in order to show that
\varphi u=\varphi\bar{u}\in W
. The problem with this proof is that we don’t know any reference for the extension theorem needed to built
\bar{u}
in weighted Sobolev spaces (an analogue of
[
Maz
, Section 1.1.17]
in the unweighted case), and we do not want to spend time on something that we will not need.

The next lemma allows us to speak about
traces for functions in the local Sobolev spaces

W_{r}(E)
.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
E^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open, and set
E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
as in (
10.1
).
For every function
u\in W_{r}(E)
, we can define the trace of
u
on
\Gamma\cap E
by

(10.7)

\Tr u(x)=\lim_{X\in\gamma(x)\atop\delta(X)\to 0}\fint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}u\qquad\text{ for $\mu$-almost every }x\in\Gamma\cap E,

and
\Tr u\in L^{2}_{loc}(\Gamma\cap E^{\prime},\mu)
.
Moreover, for every choice of
f\in W_{r}(E)
and

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(E^{\prime})
,
\varphi u\in W_{r}(E)
and

(10.8)

\Tr(\varphi u)(x)=\varphi(x)\Tr u(x)\ \text{ for $\mu$-almost every }x\in\Gamma\cap E.

Proof
.  None of this is too surprising; the trace is a local notion, and
W_{r}(E)
is designed to ba a local space.
Let
E^{\prime}
,
E
, and
f
be as in the statement, and let
B
a compact ball in
E^{\prime}
, and choose

\psi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E)
such that
\psi\equiv 1
near
B
.
Then
\psi u\in W
by (
10.2
), and the analogue of (
10.7
)
for
\psi u
comes
with the construction of the trace. This implies the existence
of the same limit for
u
, almost everywhere in
\Gamma\cap B
.

In addition, since
\psi u\in W
, Theorem
6.6
says that

Tr(\psi u)\in H
, and then
Tr(u)=Tr(\psi u)\in L^{2}(B,d\mu)

(see the definition (
6.5
)). Therefore
Tr(u)\in L^{2}_{loc}(E^{\prime},d\mu)
, as announced.

The fact that
\varphi u\in W_{r}(E)
when
u\in W_{r}(E)
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(E^{\prime})
comes
right from the definition (
10.2
) and Lemma
6.21
, and (
10.8
)
is immediate because when
B
and
\psi
are as above and
\mu
-almost everywhere on
B
,

Tr(\varphi u)=Tr(\psi^{2}\varphi u)=\psi\varphi Tr(\psi u)=\varphi Tr(u)

by (
10.7
), the formula for the trace of a product of
\psi u\in W
and
a
\psi\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and the fact that in
B
, the formula (
10.7
) does not
see the cut-off
\psi
.

\Box

11.
Definitions of solutions and their properties

We now have all the functional analysis needed
to deal with the main goal of this section, which is to
define weak solutions to appropriate degenerate elliptic operators,
and give their first properties. We will
follow Section 8 in
[
DFM2
]
(which itself
copies the frame of the first sections of
[
Ken
]
), and we will
refer to
[
DFM2
]
for most
of the proofs. As in the previous section, we systematically assume
that
(\Omega,m,\mu)

satisfies (
H1
)–(
H6
).

Recall that we intend to work with the degenerate elliptic operators
L=-\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A\nabla
,
where the matrix
A:\Omega\to\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the following elliptic and boundedness conditions:

(11.1)

A(X)\xi\cdot\xi\geq C_{A}^{-1}w(x)|\xi|^{2}\quad\text{ for }X\in\Omega\text{ and }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and

(11.2)

A(X)\xi\cdot\zeta\leq C_{A}w(x)|\xi||\zeta|\quad\text{ for }X\in\Omega\text{ and }\xi,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where

w
is the weight associated to the measure
m
given in as part of (
H4
).
We shall also
use the “normalized” matrix
\mathcal{A}:=w^{-1}A
which satisfies the unweighted ellipticity
and boundedness conditions

(11.3)

\mathcal{A}(X)\xi\cdot\xi\geq C_{A}^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\quad\text{ for }X\in\Omega\text{ and }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and

(11.4)

\mathcal{A}(X)\xi\cdot\zeta\leq C_{A}|\xi||\zeta|\quad\text{ for }X\in\Omega\text{ and }\xi,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

We introduce the bilinear form
a
defined by

(11.5)

\begin{split}a(u,v):=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla v&=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm\\
&\qquad\text{ for any $u,v$ that satisfies }\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u||\nabla v|\,dm<+\infty.\end{split}

The conditions (
11.1
)–(
11.2
) entail that
a
is
bounded on
\dot{W}\times\dot{W}
(the homogeneous quotient space) and coercive on
\dot{W}
, i.e.,

(11.6)

a(u,u)\geq C_{A}^{-1}\|u\|_{W}^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad a(u,v)\leq C_{A}\|u\|_{W}\|v\|_{W}\qquad\text{ for }u,v\in W.

It is also coercive on
W_{0}
(no need to take a quotient, because
W_{0}
does not contain nontrivial
constant functions).

Definition 11.7
.

Let
E\subset\Omega
be a open set. We say that
u\in W_{r}(E)
is a (weak)
solution to
Lu=0
in
E
when

(11.8)

a(u,\varphi)=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=0\qquad\text{ for any }\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega).

Similarly

u\in W_{r}(E)
is a subsolution (respectively supersolution) to
Lu=0
in
E
when

(11.9)

a(u,\varphi)\leq 0\ \text{(resp. $\geq 0$)}\qquad\text{ for any }\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)\text{ that satisfies }\varphi\geq 0.

In the rest of the section, we present the analogues of the results in
[
DFM2
, Section 8]
, and we discuss the differences in the proofs when needed.

The first result enlarges the class of possible test functions.

Lemma 11.10
.

Let
E\subset\Omega
be an open set and
let
u\in W_{r}(E)
be a solution to

Lu=0
in
E
. We write
E^{\Gamma}
for
E\cup(\Gamma\cap\partial E)
, that is,
E^{\Gamma}
is
the union of
E
with the part of its boundary that intersects
\Gamma
. The identity (
11.8
) holds:

•

when
\varphi\in W_{0}
is compactly supported in
E
;

•

when
\varphi\in W_{0}
is compactly supported in
E^{\Gamma}

and

u\in W_{r}(E^{\Gamma})
;

•

when
E=\Omega
,
\varphi\in W_{0}
,
and

u\in W
.

In addition,
(
11.9
) holds when
u
is a subsolution (resp. supersolution) in
E
,
\varphi
is a non-negative test function, and the couple
(u,\varphi)
satisfies one of the above conditions.

Proof
.  See the proof of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 8.3]
. This lemma is a consequence of Lemma
9.18
, that gives that the functions in
W_{0}
can be approximated by smooth functions.

\Box

The next result proves the stability of subsolutions/supersolutions under
\max
/
\min
.

Lemma 11.11
.

Let
E\subset\Omega
be an open set.

•

If
u,v\in W_{r}(E)
are subsolutions (to
Lu=0
) in
E
, then
t=\max\{u,v\}
is also a subsolution in
E
.

•

If
u,v\in W_{r}(E)
are supersolutions in
E
, then
t=\min\{u,v\}
is also a supersolution in
E
.

In particular if

k\in\mathbb{R}
, then
(u-k)_{+}:=\max\{u-k,0\}
is a subsolution in
E
whenever
u\in W_{r}(E)
is a subsolution in
E
and
\min\{u,k\}
is a supersolution in
E
whenever
u\in W_{r}(E)
is a supersolution in
E
.

Proof
.  The proof is the same as the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 8.23]
and
[
Sta
, Theorem 3.5]
. Lemma 8.5 in
[
DFM2
]
shows that the result can be localized into a relatively compact open subset
F
of
E
. Theorem 3.5 in
[
Sta
]
relies on the fact the bilinear form
a
is coercive and continuous (on appropriate local spaces) and on convex analysis.

\Box

In the sequel, the notation
\sup
and
\inf
are used for the essential supremum and essential infimum,
since they are the definitions that makes sense for the functions in
W
or in
W_{r}(E)
, for
E=E^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Omega}
and
E^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open.

In addition, the expression “
\Tr u=0
a.e. on
B
”, for a function
u\in W_{r}(B\cap\overline{\Omega})
,
means
that
\Tr u
, which is defined on
\Gamma\cap B
and lies in
L^{1}_{loc}(B\cap\Gamma,\mu)
thanks to Lemma
10.6
, is equal to
0

\mu
-almost everywhere on
\Gamma\cap B
.
The expression “
\Tr u\geq 0
a.e. on
B
” is defined similarly.

We now state some classical regularity results inside the domain and at the boundary.

Lemma 11.12

(interior Caccioppoli inequality)
.

Let
E\subset\Omega
be an open set, and let
u\in W_{r}(E)
be a non-negative subsolution in
E
. Then for any
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(E)
,

(11.13)

\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}u^{2}dm,

where
C
depends only upon the constant
C_{A}
.

In particular, if
B
is a ball of radius
r
such that
2B\subset\Omega
and
u\in W_{r}(2B)
is a non-negative subsolution in
2B
, then

(11.14)

\int_{B}|\nabla u|^{2}dm\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{2B}u^{2}dm.

Lemma 11.15

(Caccioppoli inequality on the boundary)
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball of radius
r
centered on
\Gamma
, and let
u\in W_{r}(2B\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a non-negative subsolution in
2B\cap\Omega
such that

\Tr u=0
a.e. on
2B
. Then for any
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(2B)
,

(11.16)

\int_{2B\cap\Omega}\alpha^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{2B\cap\Omega}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}u^{2}dm,

where
C
depends only on the constant
C_{A}
. In particular, we can take
\alpha\equiv 1
on
B
and
|\nabla\alpha|\leq\frac{2}{r}
, which gives

(11.17)

\int_{B\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dm\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{2B\cap\Omega}u^{2}dm.

Proof
.  The proofs of the two lemmas are similar to Lemma 8.6 and Lemma 8.11
in
[
DFM2
]
. There is not any difficulty here, maybe it is worth saying that we use
\varphi=\alpha^{2}u
, where
\alpha
is an appropriate cut-off function; and
\varphi
is a valid test function due to Lemma
11.10
and, for the boundary version, Lemma
10.6
.

\Box

Let us turn to the statement of the Moser estimates.

Lemma 11.18

(interior Moser estimate)
.

Let
p>0
and
B
be a ball such that
2B\subset\Omega
.
If
u\in W_{r}(2B)
is a non-negative subsolution in
2B
, then

(11.19)

\sup_{B}u\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m(2B)}\int_{2B}u^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
C
depends on
n
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
p
.

Lemma 11.20

(Moser estimates on the boundary)
.

Let
p>0
,
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
, and
u\in W_{r}(2B\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a non-negative
subsolution in
2B\cap\Omega
such that
\Tr u=0
a.e. on
2B
. Then

(11.21)

\sup_{B\cap\Omega}u\leq C_{p}\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B\cap\Omega}|u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
C_{p}
depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
p
.

Proof
.  The proofs for these two results are analogous to the ones of
[
DFM2
, Lemmas 8.7 and 8.12]
, and relies on the so-called Moser iterations.

What we need are Lemma
11.11
, a Cacciopoli inequality (Lemma
11.12
or Lemma
11.15
, according to the version we want to prove), a Sobolev-Poincaré inequality (Theorem
5.24
or Corollary
7.9
, the balls in the right-hand side of (
7.10
) are slightly bigger than the ones in the left-hand side, but the argument can easily be adapted), and the doubling property (
H4
).

\Box

The next step is the Hölder continuity of solutions. We shall give a
few intermediate results, starting by the density lemmas.

Lemma 11.22

(Density lemma inside the domain)
.

Let
B
be a ball such that
4B\subset\Omega
and
u\in W_{r}(4B)
be a non-negative
supersolution in
4B
such that

m(\{X\in 2B,\,u(X)\geq 1\})\geq\epsilon m(2B).

Then

(11.23)

\inf_{B}u\geq C^{-1},

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
\epsilon
.

Lemma 11.24

(Density lemma on the boundary)
.

Let
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(4B\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a
non-negative supersolution in

4B\cap\Omega
such that
\Tr u=1
a.e. on
4B
.
Then

(11.25)

\inf_{B\cap\Omega}u\geq C^{-1},

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
and
C_{A}
.

Proof
.  The proof of Lemma
11.22
can be copied from the one of Density Theorem (Section 4.3, Theorem 4.9) in
[
HL
]
. The proof of Lemma
11.24
is similar to the one Lemma 8.14
in
[
DFM2
]
(which is itself inspired from the Density Theorem in
[
HL
]
).

Formally, the ideas of the proof are to say that
v=-\ln u
is a subsolution that satisfies
\Tr u=0
a.e. on
4B
(if needed), and then to use Moser estimates (Lemma
11.18
or Lemma
11.20
) and a Poincaré inequality (Theorem
5.24
or Corollary
7.9
) in an appropriate way.

Of course, we need to be very careful: for instance when constructing
v
, we want to use Lemma
9.20
in order to verify that
v
is indeed in
W_{r}(2B)
, yet the function
-\ln
is not Lipschitz… But the pitfalls are the same as in the proof of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 8.14]
.

\Box

Next comes oscillation estimates.

Lemma 11.26

(interior Oscillation estimates)
.

Let
B
be a ball such that
4B\subset\Omega
and
u\in W_{r}(4B)
be a solution in
4B
. Then, there exists
\eta\in(0,1)
such that

(11.27)

\osc_{B}u\leq\eta\osc_{4B}u,

where the constant
\eta
depends only on on
n
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.

Lemma 11.28

(Oscillation estimates on the boundary)
.

Let
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(4B\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a solution in
4B\cap\Omega
such that
\Tr u
is uniformly bounded on
4B\cap\Gamma
. Then, there exists
\eta\in(0,1)
such that

(11.29)

\osc_{B\cap\Omega}u\leq\eta\osc_{4B\cap\Omega}u+(1-\eta)\osc_{\Gamma\cap 4B}\Tr u.

The constant
\eta
depends only on on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.

Proof
.  Lemma
11.26
and Lemma
11.28
can be proved respectively as Theorem 2.4 in
[
HL
, Section 4.3]
and as Lemma 8.15 in
[
DFM2
]
. The proofs work as long as Lemma
11.22
or Lemma
11.24
is true.

\Box

We shall now present the Hölder regularity of solutions.

Lemma 11.30

(interior Hölder continuity)
.

Let
x\in\Omega
and
R>0
be such that
B(x,2R)\subset\Omega
, and let
u\in W_{r}(B(x,2R))
be a solution to
Lu=0
in
B(x,2R)
. Set

\osc_{B}u:=\sup_{B}u-\inf_{B}u.

Then there exists
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
C>0
such that for any
0<r<R
,

(11.31)

\osc_{B(x,r)}u\leq C\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{m(B(x,R))}\int_{B(x,R)}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\alpha
and
C
depend only on
n
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.
Hence
u

is (possibly after modifying it on a set of measure
0
)
locally Hölder continuous with exponent
\alpha
.

Lemma 11.32
.

Let
B=B(x,r)
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(B\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a solution in
B\cap\Omega

such that
\Tr u
is continuous and bounded on
B\cap\Gamma
.
There exists
\alpha>0
such that for

0<s<r
,

(11.33)

\osc_{B(x,s)\cap\Omega}u\leq C\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\osc_{B(x,r)\cap\Omega}u+C\osc_{B(x,\sqrt{sr})\cap\Gamma}\Tr u

where the constants
\alpha,C
depend only on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
. In particular,
u
(possibly after modification on a set of measure 0) is continuous on
B\cap\Omega
, can be extended by continuity on
B\cap\Gamma
, and the values of this extension on
B\cap\Gamma
are
\Tr u
.

If, in addition,
\Tr u\equiv 0
on
B
, then for any
0<s<r/2

(11.34)

\osc_{B(x,s)\cap\Omega}u\leq C\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B\cap\Omega}|u|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof
.  The proof of the two last lemmas are the same as the ones of Theorem 2.5 in
[
HL
, Section 4.3]
and Lemma 8.16 in
[
DFM2
]
.

\Box

It remains to treat the Harnack inequality.

Lemma 11.35

(Harnack Inequality)
.

Let
B
be a ball such that
2B\subset\Omega
, and let
u\in W_{r}(2B)
be a non-negative solution to
Lu=0
in
2B
. Then

(11.36)

\sup_{B}u\leq C\inf_{B}u,

where
C
depends only on
n
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.

Proof
.  The proof in
[
DFM2
]
uses, roughtly speaking, the condition (
H6’
) to say that the Harnack inequality can be proved using the classical theory of uniformly elliptic operators in divergence form.

If we were to have (
H6’
) instead of (
H6
), we could proceed in a similar manner.
Fortunately for us, the proof in the classical theory can easily adapted to our setting. This observation was already made in
[
FKS
]
, but since our conditions are slightly weaker than
[
FKS
]
, we sketch the proof to check that we don’t have any extra difficulties.

Step 1:
The John-Nirenberg lemma.

Let
O
be an open subset of
\Omega
. Suppose that
w\in L^{1}(O,m)
lies
in
BMO(O,m)
, in the sense that
that for every ball
B\subset O

(11.37)

\fint_{B}|w-w_{B}|\,dm\leq C_{JN}

for a constant
C_{JN}
independent of
B
, and where
w_{B}
denotes
\fint w\,dm
. Then we claim that for any
B\subset O
,

(11.38)

\fint_{B}\exp\left(\frac{p_{0}}{C_{JN}}|w-w_{B}|\right)\,dm\leq C,

where
p_{0}
and
C
depend
only on
C_{4}
(and
n
).

The claim is the John-Nirenberg lemma, whose proof uses only the Calderón-Zygmund decomposition
(see for instance
[
HL
, Chapter 3, Theorem 1.5]
).

Step 2:
The weak Harnack inequality.

Suppose
2B\subset\Omega
and let

u\in W(2B)
be a non-negative supersolution to
Lu=0
. Then we claim that
there exists
p_{1}>0
such that

(11.39)

\inf_{B}u\geq C^{-1}\left(\fint_{2B}u^{p_{1}}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}},

where
C^{-1}
depends only on
C_{4}
,
C_{6}

C_{A}
, and
n
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we consider
the supersolution
\bar{u}=u+\epsilon>0
and then
v=\bar{u}^{-1}
.
For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(2B)
, the function
v^{2}\varphi
belongs to
W_{0}
thanks to Lemmas
6.21

and
9.20
, and is compactly supported in
\Omega
. So
v^{2}\varphi
can be used as a test function, by
Lemma
11.10
, hence

\int_{2B}\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla[v^{2}\varphi]\,dm\geq 0,

that is,

\int_{2B}\bar{u}^{-2}(\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\varphi)\,dm\geq 2\int_{2B}\bar{u}^{-3}\varphi(\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla u)\,dm

hence, by the positivity of
\mathcal{A}

(11.40)

\int_{2B}(\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla\varphi)\,dm\leq-2\int_{2B}\bar{u}^{-3}\varphi(\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla u)\,dm\leq 0.

We deduce that
v
is a non-negative subsolution in
2B
, so using Moser’s estimate (Lemma
11.18
), we get that for any
p>0

\sup_{B}v\leq C_{p}\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}v^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

where
C_{p}
depends on
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
,
n
, and
p
.
Using the fact that
v=\bar{u}^{-1}
,
we deduce that

(11.41)

\inf_{B}\bar{u}\geq C_{p}\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\bar{u}^{-p}\,dm\right)^{-\frac{1}{p}}.

The claim (
11.39
) will be established as soon as we prove
that for some
p_{1}>0
, one has

(11.42)

\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\bar{u}^{-p_{1}}\,dm\right)\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\bar{u}^{p_{1}}\,dm\right)\leq C,

with a bound
C
independent of

u
and

the
\epsilon
used to define
\bar{u}
, and we shall now
prove (
11.42
)
using the John-Nirenberg inequality.

Take
w=\log\bar{u}
;
we want to check that
w\in BMO(\frac{3}{2}B)
.
We test
\bar{u}
against the test function
\bar{u}^{-1}\varphi^{2}
,
where
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(2B)
to obtain

2\int_{2B}\varphi\bar{u}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\varphi)\,dm-\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\bar{u}^{-2}(\mathcal{A}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u})\,dm\geq 0.

We use the fact that
\nabla w=\bar{u}^{-1}\nabla u
and the ellipticity conditions (
11.3
)–(
11.4
) to obtain

\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dm\leq C\int_{2B}\varphi|\nabla w||\nabla\varphi|\,dm,

which implies, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, that

(11.43)

\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dm\leq C\int_{2B}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\,dm.

For any ball
B^{\prime}\subset\frac{3}{2}B
of radius
r^{\prime}
, we can built a smooth function
\varphi
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
B^{\prime}
,
\varphi\equiv 0
on
\frac{9}{8}B^{\prime}
, and
|\nabla\varphi|\leq 10/r^{\prime}
. Using those test functions in (
11.43
) gives that for any
B^{\prime}\subset\frac{3}{2}B
,

(11.44)

\fint_{B^{\prime}}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dm\leq C(r^{\prime})^{-2},

where
C
depends only on
C_{A}
. The assumption (
H6
), i.e. the Poincaré inequality,
infers now that

\fint_{B^{\prime}}|w-w_{B^{\prime}}|\,dm\leq C,

as in (
11.37
). From step 1, the inequality (
11.38
) thus holds, that is we can find a
p_{1}>0
such that

(11.45)

\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp\left(p_{1}|w-w_{\frac{3}{2}B}|\right)\,dm\leq C.

We are now ready for the proof of (
11.42
). Indeed, just observe that

\begin{split}\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}u^{-p_{1}}\,dm\right)&\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}u^{p_{1}}\,dm\right)=\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp(-p_{1}w)\,dm\right)\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp(p_{1}w)\,dm\right)\\
&=\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp(-p_{1}[w-w_{\frac{3}{2}B}])\,dm\right)\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp(p_{1}[w-w_{\frac{3}{2}B}])\,dm\right)\\
&\leq\left(\fint_{\frac{3}{2}B}\exp(p_{1}|w-w_{\frac{3}{2}B}|)\,dm\right)^{2}\lesssim 1,\end{split}

by (
11.45
).

Step 3:
Conclusion.

We combine (
11.39
) with Lemma
11.18
- the Moser inequality inside the domain,
to get the desired Harnack inequality

\sup_{B}u\leq C\inf_{B}u

We should require
B
to satisfy
4B\subset\Omega
, but we can easily solve
this issue by covering
B
by balls
B^{\prime}
of smaller radius that satisfy

4B^{\prime}\subset 2B\subset\Omega
.

\Box

We shall also need the following version of the Harnack inequality, which will be useful to define the harmonic measure.

Lemma 11.46
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
\Omega
and let
u\in W_{r}(\Omega)
be a non-negative
solution in
\Omega
. Then

(11.47)

\sup_{K}u\leq C_{K}\inf_{K}u,

where
C_{K}
depends only on

n
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
\diam\,K/\,\mathrm{dist}(K,\Gamma)
.

Proof
.  The proof is the same as the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 8.10]
. The topological conditions (
H1
)–(
H2
) allow us to connect any couple of points in
K
by a chain of balls that says away from the boundary (see Proposition
2.18
).
The length of the chain can be bounded by a constant depending only on
\diam K/\,\mathrm{dist}(K,\partial\Omega)
. We then use the Harnack inequality above on those balls.

\Box

12.
Construction of the harmonic measure

We follow Section 9 in
[
DFM2
]
and, as in the previous section, we will refer to
[
DFM2
]
when the proofs do not require any new argument.

The objective of the section is, as the title suggests, to construct a harmonic measure associated to our degenerate
operator
L=-\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A\nabla
that still satisfies (
11.1
)–(
11.2
). By harmonic measure,
we mean
a family of measures
\omega^{X}_{L}
, where
X\in\Omega
is called pole of the harmonic measure, such that for any Borel set
E\subset\Gamma
, the function
u_{E}
defined as
u_{E}(X)=\omega^{X}_{L}
solves
the Dirichlet problem

(12.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Lu_{E}=0&\text{ in }\Omega\\
u_{E}={\mathds{1}}_{E}&\text{ on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

But
(
12.1
) does not make a lot of sense for the moment. The part “
Lu_{E}=0
in
\Omega
” is easy to interpret: we want
u_{E}
to lie in
W_{r}(\Omega)
and to be a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega
.
The part “
u_{E}={\mathds{1}}_{E}
on
\Gamma
” is harder to understand: we could hope that the meaning is

\Tr u_{E}={\mathds{1}}_{E}

\mu
-a.e. on
\Gamma
, but it is unclear that it is possible at this point.

Another issue
is the uniqueness: just take
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
- i.e.
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
- and
E=\emptyset
- i.e.
{\mathds{1}}_{E}\equiv 0
- and we can find at least two solutions (
u_{1}\equiv 0
and
u_{2}=\delta
) to
Lu=0
that satisfy both
\Tr u=0
. Imposing that
u
lies in
W
is not immediately
possible, since characteristic functions of non-trivial sets do not always lie
in
H
.

Our salvation will come
from the maximum principle. And instead of (
12.1
), we shall say that the harmonic measure
\omega_{L}^{X}
is built such that for any
g\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the function defined by

u(X)=\int_{\Gamma}g(y)d\omega^{X}_{L}(y)

lies in
W
, is a solution to
Lu=0
, and satisfies
\Tr u=g
.
Let us now give a full presentation.

We say that
f\in W^{-1}
if
f
is a linear form on
W_{0}
that satisfies

|\left<f,v\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}|\leq C_{f}\|v\|_{W},

where we anticipate slightly and denote by
\left<f,v\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}

the effect of
f
on
v
.
The best constant
C_{f}
in the inequality above is denoted
\|f\|_{W^{-1}}
.

Let us give first the existence and uniqueness of solutions
u\in W
to

Lu=f
and
\Tr u=g
, where
f\in W^{-1}
and
g\in H
are given.

Lemma 12.2
.

For any
f\in W^{-1}
and any
g\in H
, there exists a unique
u\in W
such that

(12.3)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm=\left<f,v\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}\qquad\text{ for all }v\in W_{0},

and

(12.4)

\Tr u=g\text{ a.e. on }\Gamma.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
,
independent of
f
and
g
,
such that

(12.5)

\|u\|_{W}\leq C(\|g\|_{H}+\|f\|_{W^{-1}}).

Proof
.  The lemma follows from the extension theorem (Theorem
8.5
) and the Lax-Milgram theorem. Details are given in the proof of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 9.1]
.

\Box

The next result needed is a maximum principle. In its weak form, the maximum principle is as follows.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
u\in W
be a supersolution in
\Omega
satisfying
\Tr u\geq 0

\mu
-a.e. on
\Gamma
. Then
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
.

Proof
.  Take
v:=\min\{u,0\}\leq 0
;
we want
to prove that
v\equiv 0
.
Lemma
9.20
allows us to say that
v\in W
,
\nabla v=\nabla u{\mathds{1}}_{\{u<0\}}
, and
\Tr v=0
a.e. in
\Gamma
. In particular
v\in W_{0}
, which makes
v
a valid test function to be tested against the supersolution
u
(see Lemma
11.10
). This gives

(12.7)

0\geq\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm=\int_{\{u<0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dm=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v\,dm\geq C_{A}^{-1}\|\nabla v\|_{W}^{2}\geq 0.

that is,
\|\nabla v\|_{W}=0
.
Yet,
\|.\|_{W}
is a norm on
W_{0}\ni v
, hence
v=0
a.e. in
\Omega
.

\Box

Here is a stronger form of the maximum principle.

Lemma 12.8

(Maximum principle)
.

Let
u\in W
be a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega
. Then

(12.9)

\sup_{\Omega}u\leq\sup_{\Gamma}\Tr u

and

(12.10)

\inf_{\Omega}u\geq\inf_{\Gamma}\Tr u,

where we recall that
\sup
and
\inf
actually essential supremum and infimum.
In particular, if
\Tr u
is essentially bounded (for the measure
\mu
), then

(12.11)

\sup_{\Omega}|u|\leq\sup_{\Gamma}|\Tr u|.

Proof
.  Let us prove (
12.9
). Write
M
for the essential supremum of
\Tr u
on
\Gamma
;
we may assume that
M<+\infty
, because otherwise (
12.9
) is trivial.
Then
M-u\in W
and
\Tr(M-u)\geq 0
a.e. on
\Gamma
.
Lemma
12.6
yields
M-u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
, that is

(12.12)

\sup_{\Omega}u\leq\sup_{\Gamma}Tu.

The lower bound (
12.10
) is similar
and (
12.11
) follows.

\Box

The harmonic measure will be defined with the help of the Riesz representation theorem (for measures), so we need a linear form on
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
, the space of compactly supported continuous functions on
\Gamma
.
We also write
C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
for the space of continuous bounded functions on
\overline{\Omega}
.

Lemma 12.13
.

There exists a unique linear operator

(12.14)

U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})

such that, for every every
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
,

(i)

if
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
, then
Ug\in W
, and it is the solution of (
12.3
)–(
12.4
), with
f=0
, provided by Lemma
12.2
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}Ug=\sup_{\Gamma}g
and
\displaystyle\inf_{\Omega}Ug=\inf_{\Gamma}g
;

In addition,
U
enjoys the following properties:

(iii)

the restriction of
Ug
to
\Gamma
is
g
;

(iv)

Ug\in W_{r}(\Omega)
and is a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega
;

(v)

if
B
is a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
g\equiv 0
on
B
, then

Ug
lies in
W_{r}(B\cap\overline{\Omega})
;

Proof
.  The proof of the existence of
U
and its properties is similar to the one of Lemma 9.4 in
[
DFM2
]
.
Still, let us give a sketch of
the proof of
existence for

U
.

First, we use
(i)
to define
U
on
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
. Lemma
12.8
proves that
(ii)
is satisfied for any
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
, and in particular

U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})

is a continuous operator if we equip both
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
and
C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
with the norm
\|.\|_{\infty}
. Then, observe that the space
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
contains all the restrictions to
\Gamma
of functions in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and hence
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
is dense in
C_{0}^{0}(\Gamma)
(equipped with the norm
\|.\|_{\infty}
). We define
U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
as the only bounded extension of
U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
, and in particular
(ii)
is preserved.

The property
(iii)
is true when
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
thanks to Lemma
11.32
, and the property is kept by the extension. The property
(iv)
is true when
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
by Lemma
12.2
, and can be extended for all
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
with the help of Cacciopoli’s inequality (Lemma
11.12
). As for the property
(v)
- which is immediate by construction for any
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
- we prove it by approaching
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
by functions in
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
and we use Lemma
11.15
to control the estimate on the gradient when we take the limit.

The uniqueness of
U
is also immediate, since
(ii)
forces
U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
to be the continuous extension of
U:\,C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H\to C^{0}_{b}(\overline{\Omega})
given by
(i)
.
\Box

Lemma 12.15
.

For any
X\in\Omega
, there exists a unique positive regular Borel measure
\omega^{X}:=\omega_{L}^{X}
on
\Gamma
such that

(12.16)

Ug(X)=\int_{\Gamma}g(y)d\omega^{X}(y)

for any
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
. Besides, for any Borel set
E\subset\Gamma
,

(12.17)

\omega^{X}(E)=\sup\{\omega^{X}(K):\,E\supset K,\,K\text{ compact}\}=\inf\{\omega^{X}(V):\,E\subset V,\,V\text{ open}\}.

In addition, the harmonic measure is a probability measure, that is

(12.18)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma)=1.

Proof
.  The first part, that is the existence of a positive regular Borel measure satisfying (
12.16
), and the property (
12.17
), is immediate by applying the Riesz representation theorem (see for instance
[
Rud
, Theorem 2.14]
) to
U
. The positivity of the harmonic measure comes from
\displaystyle\inf_{\Omega}Ug=\inf_{\Gamma}g
given by Lemma
12.13

(ii)
.

The fact that
\omega^{X}(\Gamma)\leq 1
comes from the fact that
\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}Ug=\sup_{\Gamma}g
. We can prove that

\omega^{X}(\Gamma)\geq 1
by using the Hölder regularity at the boundary (Lemma
11.32
). See the proof of Lemma 9.6 in
[
DFM2
]
for details.

\Box

Lemma 12.19
.

Let
E\subset\Gamma
be a Borel set and define the function
u_{E}
on
\Omega
by
u_{E}(X)=\omega^{X}(E)
.
Then

(i)

if there exists
X\in\Omega
such that
u_{E}(X)=0
, then
u_{E}\equiv 0
;

(ii)

the function
u_{E}
lies
in
W_{r}(\Omega)
and is a solution in
\Omega
;

(iii)

if
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a ball such that

E\cap B=\emptyset
, then
u_{E}\in W_{r}(B\cap\overline{\Omega})
and
\Tr u_{E}=0
a.e. on
B
.

Proof
.  The proof of this result is analogous to the one of Lemma 9.7 in
[
DFM2
]
.
Here are some of the main ideas.
The proof of
(i)
is quite easy.
We approach
{\mathds{1}}_{E}
by
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
, and compare
u_{E}
with
u_{g}=Ug
.
We get that

\left|u_{g}(X)-u_{E}(X)\right|=u_{g}(X)

is as small as we want. Then we use
Lemma
11.46
to say that
0\leq u_{E}(Y)\lesssim u_{g}(Y)\lesssim u_{g}(X)\leq\epsilon
, and we let
\epsilon
tend to
0
.

The proofs of
(ii)
and
(iii)
are longer. They consist in approaching
u_{E}
by functions
u_{g}=Ug
that have all the desired properties by Lemma
12.13
, then controlling
\nabla u_{g}
uniformly
with the help of
Lemma
11.12
and Lemma
11.15
. We eventually use Lemma
11.30
,
Lemma
11.32
, and Lemma
12.8
to ensure that the
u_{g}
’s
are nice functions that satisfy
g\leq h\Rightarrow u_{g}\leq u_{h}
.

\Box

13.
Bounded boundaries

So far, in Section
5
and hence in all the sections following it,
we have been working with a boundary set
\Gamma
which is unbounded,
and hence an unbounded domain
\Omega
too. But it is some times interesting,
and not too difficult, to deal with bounded sets
\Gamma
. In this section we describe how to modify our assumptions, and some times the proofs, to extend the results of this paper to the case of bounded
\Gamma
. So let us assume now that

\Gamma=\partial\Omega
is bounded, and (to normalize things) that

(13.1)

0\in\Gamma\ \text{ and }\ \diam(\Gamma)=R_{0}>0.

There will be two slightly different cases to consider, Case 1 when
\Omega
also is bounded (and connected - due to (
H1
)),
and Case 2 when
\Omega
is the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Gamma
.
When the dimension of
\Gamma
is smaller than
n-1
, we are in Case 2,
but Case 1 is interesting too, especially in the context of mixed co-dimensions,
where we may do it on purpose to add pieces of boundary
that isolate some parts of a domain. That is, even if we start with the unbounded
component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Gamma
, we could for instance add to
\Gamma
a large sphere
like
S=\partial B(0,2R_{0})
to
\Gamma
, and restrict our attention to the bounded component
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus(\Gamma\cup S)
that touches
S
because this is simpler.

Most of the results above
are local, in the sense that they rely on computations that do not go too far.
The only difference that it makes on our assumptions is that - if
\Omega
is bounded - we need
to take the
r
in (
H1
) not too large, for instance not bigger than
\diam\Omega
,
while the unbounded case will require that
r
to be taken in the full range
(0,+\infty)
.

Observe also that in the case where
\Gamma
is bounded, we just need
the analogue of (
H1
)-(
H6
), where
we keep the same statement as before but only ask
(
H1
) and (
H3
) to hold when
B(x,r)\subset B_{0}=B(0,2R_{0})
, and (
H2
) to hold for points
X,Y\in B_{0}

(the other case would follow anyway). When
\Omega
is also bounded,
we can restrict to
B(0,2R_{0})
in the definition of (
H4
)
(the absolute continuity and doubling property for
m
) and (
H6
)
(the density and weak Poicaré inequality for
m
).
One could see such apparent weakening as an improvement, but it is easy to check that the cases that we dropped are automatically true for bounded
\Gamma
and/or
\Omega
.
However the truth is that we are not really interested in studying wild weights
w
far from

\Gamma
, and a simple monomial equivalent at infinity would probably be enough.

With these assumptions, most of our local estimates still hold, with very little changes in the proofs.
Let us be a little more specific.

We keep the definition of
W
as it was. Notice that constant functions still lie in
W
(with a vanishing
norm); depending on the behavior of
m
far from
\Gamma
, the functions
u\in W
may have a more or less rich behavior near infinity, but let us not bother yet. Section
4
goes through without modification
(we kept the same assumptions on
m
alone).

The definition of dyadic pseudocubes has to be changed a little bit: we only use the
partition
\Gamma=\bigcup_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{k}}Q_{j}^{k}
for
k\geq k_{0}
, where

k_{0}
is such that
2^{-k_{0}}\sim 4R_{0}
, and also it is customary to take for
k=k_{0}

the trivial decomposition into a unique cube
Q_{0}=\Gamma
.
Of course, all the subsequent sums in
k
will be restricted to
k\geq k_{0}
.

Then, even in the definition of the access regions
\gamma(x)
(as in (
5.8
))
for unbounded domains
\Omega
, we will only consider cubes of size at most
CR_{0}
, and so our
access regions will be bounded. We are not shocked because for many of our results we already
considered the truncated regions of (
5.11
).
The results of Section
5
, and in particular the improved Poincaré inequality in
Theorem
5.25
, are local and stay the same, but we only consider sets that are contained in
CB_{0}
.
Recall however that the case of balls
B
such that
2B\subset\Omega
, even when
B
is large,
is taken care of in Lemma
4.7
, so we will never be in real trouble anywhere.

Our definition (
6.5
) of the Hilbert space
H
on
\Gamma
stays the same; as before
constants lie in
H
, with a vanishing norm. Theorem
6.6
on the existence of a trace
operator is still valid with the same proof. The proof does not use the values of
u\in W
at distance
more than
CR_{0}
from
\Gamma
, so we may even forget the corresponding part of
||f||_{W}
in the
estimate for
\|\Tr(f)\|_{H}
. That is,

(13.2)

\|\Tr u\|_{H}^{2}\lesssim\int_{\{Z\in\Omega\cap B(0,CR_{0})\}}|\nabla u(Z)|^{2}\,dm(Z).

Another way (softer but just a bit more complicated technically)
to check this is to notice that, when
\Omega
is unbounded, we may always
truncate any
u\in W
in the following way. We select a smooth cut-off function
\varphi
such that

\varphi=1
in
B_{0}
and
\varphi=0
outside of
2B_{0}
, pick a ball
B_{1}
of radius
R_{0}

such that
B_{1}\subset 2B_{0}\setminus B_{0}
and
B_{1}
touches
\partial B_{0}
, let
m_{1}
denote the average
of
u
on
B_{1}
, and consider the “truncated” function
\widetilde{u}=\varphi u+(1-\varphi)m_{1}
.
Obviously
\widetilde{u}
has the same trace, and is would be easy to see, using the extension of Lemma
4.7

to a
(2,2)
-Poincaré inequality, as in Theorem
5.24
with
p=2
, that

\widetilde{f}\in W
, with
||\widetilde{f}||_{W}^{2}\leq C\int_{2B_{0}}|\nabla f|^{2}dm
. Of course all this is much easier if

w
is reasonably smooth on
2B_{0}\setminus B_{0}
.

The product rule for the trace and gradient (Lemma
6.21
), as well as all the local algebraic formulas,
go through. Similarly, the Poincaré inequalities on the boundary
(Theorem
7.1
and Corollary
7.9
) stay the same,
except that we restrict to balls of radius at most
10R_{0}
, say.

Our extension theorem (Theorem
8.5
) is still true; the construction also easily gives that

\Ext(f)
is constant on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus CB_{0}
(when
\Omega
is unbounded), and we can take the constant
equal to the average
m_{0}=\fint_{\Gamma}fd\mu
. The simplest way to see this is to consider
f-m_{0}

and use the formula (
8.3
), but restrict the sum to Whitney cubes of size at most
CR_{0}
. Or said
differently, for the function
f-m_{0}
we can use
y_{I}=0
for all the large Whitney cubes.

There is no difficulty with the density or algebraic results of Section
9
, and the local
spaces of Section
10
are (just a bit) simpler.
The definition of solutions is local, and all the regularity theorems for solutions found in Section
11
stay the same.
This statement may look obvious, we are saying since the beginning of the section that all the results are exactly the same for bounded
\Gamma
and unbounded
\Gamma
, but let us observe the following interesting fact.
The boundary regularity results, such as Lemmas
11.20
and
11.32
, hold for all balls
B
centered at the boundary even when the radius of
B
is way bigger than the diameter of
\Gamma
, and so can be applied for instance to the Green functions - that we shall introduce in a
next paper.

Let us now review the basic features of the harmonic measure. Its construction given in Section
12
still goes through; that is, the existence of solutions as in Lemma
12.2

is still valid, by Lax Milgram, and so is the maximum principle that allows one to solve the
Dirichlet problem for
f\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)=C^{0}(\Gamma)
. Thus
\omega^{X}
is defined
(by the Riesz representation theorem), and is again a probability measure because the best extension
of the function
1
is
1
.

If
\Gamma
is bounded and
\Omega
is the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Gamma
, then Brownian paths leaving from
X\in\Omega

have a nonzero probability of never touching
\Gamma
before going to infinity. It means that the classical harmonic measure - defined from the Laplacian - is not a probability measure.
This simple case is however not included in our theory; indeed the assumption (
H5
) fails for large
r
when we take
\mu
as the surface measure on the bounded set
\Gamma
and
m
as the Lebesgue measure on
\Omega
.
On the contrary, our theory roughly says that a modified Brownian motion, that imposes a drift in the direction of
\Gamma
when we are far from it, is sufficient to guarantee to touch
\Gamma
with probability one.

For the two last sections, where we study the Green functions and the harmonic measure, leading to a comparison principle, we do not assume that
\Gamma
is unbounded anymore. So both
\Gamma
and
\Omega

can be bounded or unbounded, and we believe that the case where
\Gamma
is bounded and
\Omega
