is the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Gamma
is the most tricky one.

14.
Green functions

We associate Green functions to the degenerate elliptic operator
L
. A Green function is, formally, a function
g
defined on
\Omega\times\Omega
and such that for any
y\in\Omega
, the function
g(.,y)
satisfies (
12.3
) and (
12.4
) with
f=\delta_{y}
- the Dirac distribution at the point
y
- and
g\equiv 0
.

The harmonic measure can be seen as a fundamental tool to solve the problem
Lu=0
in
\Omega
with
\Tr u=g
on
\Gamma
, while the Green function is a key ingredient to be able to solve
Lu=f
in
\Omega
with
\Tr u=0
on
\Gamma
. Their properties are actually related, as we shall see in Section
15
.

Let us recall that, as in the previous sections, we assume (
H1
)–(
H6
), and (
11.1
)–(
11.2
).

In order to define the Green function in our context, we will follow closely the proof of Grüter and Widman
[
GW
]
(as in
[
DFM2
]
). In the article
[
GW
]
, the authors proved the existence of the Green functions
g(.,y)
by taking a weak limit of some
g^{\rho}(.,y)
that solves
Lu=f^{\rho}
and
\Tr u\equiv 0
for some
f^{\rho}
that ‘approximates’ the delta distribution
\delta_{y}
.

Some difficulties appears when we try to get ‘local’ estimates, i.e. when the distance between
x
and
y
is small compared to the distance of both points to the boundary. Those estimates are needed to show that our
g^{\rho}(.,y)
are uniformly bounded in some good space. We solve those difficulties by using methods inspired from
[
FJK
]
, where the authors deal with degenerate elliptic operators but they define Green function via another method.

For short, we claim here that Grüter and Widman’s method can be applied - up to few changes - to obtain Green functions in a large varieties of situations, and for instance doesn’t require to have a global Sobolev inequality.

Instead of giving a big theorem for the start, as in
[
DFM2
]
, we choose here to divide the work, and prove plenty of small lemmas, whose proofs are sometimes omitted because they are the same as in
[
DFM2
]
. The important results are gathered at the end of the section, in Theorem
14.60
.

Definition 14.1
.

Let
y\in\Omega
and
\rho>0
satisfy

100\rho<\delta(y)
. The function
g^{\rho}(.,y)
is the function in
W_{0}
that satisfies

(14.2)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla g^{\rho}(.,y)\cdot\nabla v\,dm=\fint_{B(y,\rho)}v\,dm\qquad\text{ for all }v\in W_{0},

as given by Lemma
12.2
.

Notice that the definition makes sense, because
v\to\fint_{B(y,\rho)}v\,dm
is a
bounded linear form on
W_{0}
(and hence an element
f^{\rho}\in W^{-1}

to which we apply the lemma), by the doubling condition (
H4
) and the Poincaré inequality
Corollary
7.9
. The norm of
f^{\rho}
in
W^{-1}
depends on
y
and
\rho
,
but it doesn’t matter.

Since
y
will be fixed for a long part of our section, we write in the sequel

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
for
g^{\rho}(.,y)
and
B_{\rho}
for
B(y,\rho)
.
Then the condition (
14.2
) in the definition becomes

(14.3)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla v\,dm=\fint_{B_{\rho}}v\,dm\qquad\text{ for all }v\in W_{0},

We deduce at once from the definition that

(14.4)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
is a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
.

In particular, by Lemmas
11.30
and
11.32
, the function
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
is continuous on
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
.

Lemma 14.5
.

For all
y\in\Omega
, the function
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=g^{\rho}(.,y)

is nonnegative.

Proof
.  The proof of this fact
is identical to the one given for

[
DFM2
, Lemma 10.1]
,
and relies only on the stability of
W_{0}
provided by Lemma
9.20
.

\Box

We
now prove pointwise estimates on
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
and start with the case
when
x
is far from
y
.

Lemma 14.6
.

If
x,y\in\Omega
are such that
10|x-y|\geq\delta(y)
, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)},

where
C
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  Let
R\geq\delta(y)>100\rho>0
, and write
B_{R}
for
B(y,R)
. Let
p
be
in the range given by Corollary
7.9
; that is, any
p\in[1,2k)
, where
k
is a constant that depends on the geometry, will do.
We want to prove that for all
t>0
,

(14.7)

\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)>t\})}{m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)}\leq C\left(\frac{t\,m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)}{R^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}

with a constant
C
independent of
\rho
,
t
and
R
. The proof of the claim is analogous to the one
in
[
DFM2
]
, but we repeat it because we will use similar computations later on. We use (
14.3
) with the test function

(14.8)

\varphi(z):=\left(\frac{2}{t}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)}\right)^{+}=\max\left\{0,\frac{2}{t}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)}\right\}

(and
\varphi(z)=0
if
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)=0
), which lies in
W_{0}
by Lemma
9.20
.
Set

\Omega_{s}:=\big\{z\in\Omega,\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)>s\big\}

and observe that
\varphi
is supported in
\Omega_{t/2}
. Hence

(14.9)

a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\varphi)=\int_{\Omega_{t/2}}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}}\,dm=\fint_{B_{\rho}}\varphi\,dm\leq\frac{2}{t}

and then,
thanks to the ellipticity condition (
11.3
),

(14.10)

\int_{\Omega_{t/2}}\frac{|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{t}.

Pick a point
y_{0}\in\Gamma
such that

|y-y_{0}|=\delta(y)
. Set
\widetilde{B}_{R}
for
B(y_{0},2R)\supset B_{R}
. Define
v
by

v(z):=(\ln(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z))-\ln t+\ln 2)^{+}
; then
v\in W_{0}
too,
thanks to Lemma
9.20
, and

|\nabla v|^{2}=|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}/(g^{\rho})^{2}
.
Corollary
7.9
(the
Sobolev-Poincaré inequality at the boundary) implies that

(14.11)

\begin{split}\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2}\cap\widetilde{B}_{R}}|v|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}&\leq CR\,m(\widetilde{B}_{R}\cap\Omega)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2}\cap 2\widetilde{B}_{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq CR\,m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

by
(
14.10
) and (
H4
). Yet,
v\geq\ln(2)
on
\Omega_{t}
, and thus the above inequality gives that

(14.12)

m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})\leq m(\Omega_{t}\cap\widetilde{B}_{R})\lesssim R^{p}[m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)]^{1-\frac{p}{2}}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\leq m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)\left(\frac{m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)t}{R^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

The claim (
14.7
) follows.
We are now ready to establish pointwise estimates on
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
when
x
is far from
y
.
We now
aim to prove (
14.7
) with a constant independent of
\rho
.
Set
R=10|x-y|\geq\delta(y)
. By (
14.4
),
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
is a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
, so we can use Moser’s estimates;
we claim that we get that

(14.13)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq\frac{C}{m(B(x,R/2)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(x,R/2)\cap\Omega}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm.

When
\delta(x)\geq R/50
we apply Lemma
11.18
in the ball
B(x,R/100)
), and
when
\delta(x)\leq R/50
we apply Lemma
11.20
in the ball
B(x_{0},R/25)
,
where
x_{0}
is such that
|x-x_{0}|=\delta(x)
. We can use (
H4
) to replace
the measure of the ball by
m(B(x,R/2)\cap\Omega)
.

We can use now the fact that
B(x,R/2)\subset B_{R}
and
Cavalieri’s formula (see for instance
[
Duo
, p. 28, Proposition 2.3]
) to get that

(14.14)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\lesssim\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(\Omega\cap B_{R})}dt.

Take
s>0
, to be chosen later.
We bound the interior of the integral above by
1
when
t<s
, and for
t\geq s
we use
(
14.7
), which we apply with some
p>2
(this is possible); we get that

(14.15)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)&\lesssim\int_{0}^{s}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(\Omega\cap B_{R})}dt+\int_{s}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(\Omega\cap B_{R})}dt\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{s}1\,dt+\left(\frac{m(B_{R}\cap\Omega))}{R^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int_{s}^{+\infty}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}dt\\
&\lesssim s\left[1+\left(\frac{s\,m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)}{R^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right].\end{split}

Now we
minimize the right-hand side in
s
.
We find
s\approx R^{2}/m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)
and then
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\lesssim R^{2}/m(B_{R}\cap\Omega)
. Since
R=10|x-y|
, the lemma follows from (
H4
).

\Box

The next result deals with the case when
x
and
y
are close to each other.

Lemma 14.16
.

If
x,y\in\Omega
are such that
40\rho\leq 2|x-y|\leq\delta(y)
, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\,\frac{dr}{r},

where
C
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  The proof of this result in the classical case, at least the one in
[
GW
]
, uses a global Sobolev equality. In our setting, we don’t have Sobolev embeddings, only a Sobolev-Poincaé inequality
(Theorem
4.7
), and the
L^{q}
norm given in the right-hand side of our
Sobolev-Poincaré inequality may just be
L^{2+\epsilon}
.
In particular, we have no reason to get close to the desired
L^{2n/(n-2)}
.

Fortunately, the slight improvement in the exponent of the
L^{p}
space given by
Theorem
4.7

will be - as for Lemma
14.6
- sufficient.
Even better, the proof will follow the same ideas as Lemma
14.6
.

Let
j_{0}\geq 0

be the biggest integer such that
2^{j_{0}+1}|x-y|\leq\delta(y)
.
To lighten the notation, we write
B^{j}
for
B(y,2^{j}|x-y|)
.
We shall prove that for any
j
between
0
and
j_{0}-1
,

(14.17)

\sup_{B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\sup_{B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq C\,\frac{(2^{j}|x-y|)^{2}}{m(B_{j})}.

We write
\mathfrak{g}_{j}^{\rho}
for
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\sup_{B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
.
We also write
\Omega_{s,j}
for
\{x\in\Omega,\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}>s\}
.
Notice that
B^{j+2}\subset\Omega
, and
\sup_{B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}}=\sup_{\Omega\setminus B^{j+1}}
by the maximum principle.
Hence,
\Omega_{s,j}\subset B^{j+1}
when
s>0
.
Let
t>0
be given; we
use again (
14.3
),
but with the test function

\varphi_{j}(z):=\left(\frac{2}{t}-\frac{1}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}(z))^{+}}\right)^{+},

to get, as we did for (
14.9
),

a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\varphi)=\int_{\Omega_{t/2,j}}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j})^{2}}\,dm\leq\fint_{B_{\rho}}\varphi_{j}\,dm\leq\frac{2}{t},

and by
(
11.3
)

(14.18)

\int_{\Omega_{t/2,j}}\frac{|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}|^{2}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j})^{2}}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{t}.

Set
v_{j}(z):=(\ln(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}(z))-\ln t+\ln 2)^{+}
;
as before
v_{j}\in W
, it is supported in
\Omega_{t/2,j}\subset B^{j+1}
, and

|\nabla v_{j}|=|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}|/\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}
on
\Omega_{t/2}
.
Since
v_{j}=0
on
B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}\subset\Omega
, we can apply
Theorem
5.24
and Remark
5.27
, and we get that

(14.19)

\begin{split}\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2,j}}|v_{j}|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}&\leq C2^{j+1}|x-y|\,m(B^{j+1})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2,j}}|\nabla v_{j}|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C2^{j}|x-y|\,m(B^{j})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

by (
14.18
) and (
H4
),
where
p\in[k,2k]

plays the role of
kp
in Theorem
5.24
and Remark
5.27
,
and we are mostly interested in
p=2
there which yields
p=2k
here. Of course
C
is independent of
j
.
Since
|v_{j}|>\ln 2
on
\Omega_{t,j}

and
\Omega_{t,j}\subset\Omega_{t/2,j}
,
(
14.19
) implies that

(14.20)

\frac{m(\Omega_{t,j})}{m(B^{j+2})}\leq C\left(\frac{t\,m(B^{j})}{(2^{j}|x-y|)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

The rest of the proof of (
14.17
) is
similar to what we did for
Lemma
14.6
.
Since
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}
is a solution in
B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j-1}
, we can use the Moser inequality inside

B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j-1}
to get that for
z\in B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}
,

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}(z)
is smaller - up to a constant - than its average on
B(z,2^{j-10}|x-y|)
.
The measure of this last ball
is equivalent, by (
H4
), to the measure of
B_{j+2}
and thus for any
z\in B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}

(14.21)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}(z)\lesssim\fint_{B^{j+2}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}\,dm=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t,j})}{m(B^{j+2})}dt

(compare with (
14.14
)).
Again we split the last integral
into two pieces, and for the second one
we use (
14.20
); we
obtain that for all
z\in B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}

(14.22)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{j}(z)&\lesssim\int_{0}^{s}1\,dt+\left(\frac{m(B^{j}))}{(2^{j}|x-y|)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int_{s}^{+\infty}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}dt\\
&\lesssim s\left[1+\left(\frac{s\,m(B^{j})}{(2^{j}|x-y|)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\right].\end{split}

where we can choose
p
, which comes from Theorem
5.24
(for instance applied with
the exponent
2
),
strictly bigger than 2. We optimize in
s
and take the supremum in
z
to get the desired estimate (
14.17
).

We can use (
H4
) to rewrite
(
14.17
) as

(14.23)

\sup_{B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\sup_{B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq C\int_{2^{j}|x-y|}^{2^{j+1}|x-y|}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}.

This was for
j<j_{0}
, but for
j=j_{0}
we will be able to apply Lemma
14.6
.
Recall that
2^{j_{0}+2}|x-y|>\delta(y)
by definition of
j_{0}
; hence for

z\in B^{j_{0}+1}\setminus B^{j_{0}}
,

|z-y|\geq 2^{j_{0}}|x-y|>\delta(y)/10
and by Lemma
14.6
and the same trick
as for (
14.23
),

\sup_{B^{j_{0}+1}\setminus B^{j_{0}}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq C\int_{2^{j_{0}}|x-y|}^{2^{j_{0}+1}|x-y|}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}\leq C\int_{2^{j_{0}}|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}.

Now,
since
\mathfrak{g}_{\rho}
is continuous around
x
(by the interior Hölder estimates and (
14.4
)),

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)&\leq\sup_{B^{1}\setminus B^{0}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq\sup_{B^{j_{0}+1}\setminus B^{j_{0}}}\mathfrak{g}+\sum_{j=0}^{j_{0}-1}\left(\sup_{B^{j+1}\setminus B^{j}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\sup_{B^{j+2}\setminus B^{j+1}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\right)\\
&\lesssim\int_{2^{j_{0}}|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}+\sum_{j=0}^{j_{0}-1}\int_{2^{j}|x-y|}^{2^{j+1}|x-y|}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}\frac{dr}{r}\,;\end{split}

Lemma
14.16
follows.
\Box

Before we continue to prove estimate about Green functions, we take a little time to talk about cut-off functions. Pick

\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
0\leq\phi\leq 1
,
\phi\equiv 0
on
(2,+\infty)
,
\phi\equiv 1
on
[0,1)
, and
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq 2
. If we want a cut-off function adapted to the ball
B(x,r)
, the first choice will be

(14.24)

\alpha^{1}(y):=\phi\left(\frac{|x-y|}{r}\right).

If the above cut-off function fails to work, we might try to use a cut-off function
that involves logarithms,
in the spirit of the one used by Sobolev. For instance, if we work on the
Green function for the unit disk (with the classical Lebesgue measure) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the good cut-off
may be

(14.25)

\alpha^{2}(y):=\phi\left(\frac{\ln(\delta(y)/r)}{\ln(\delta(y)/|x-y|)}\right).

In the classical theory, where the domains are equipped
with the usual Lebesgue measure, we would use
\alpha^{1}
when
n\geq 3
and
\alpha^{2}
when
n=2
.
In our article,
\alpha^{1}
or
\alpha^{2}
may be needed, or something different. The cut-off functions that we shall need depend on the measure
m
and the purpose of the next lines is to define them.

We define the function
\gamma=\gamma_{y}

on
(0,\delta(y))
by

(14.26)

\gamma(s):=\int_{s}^{\delta(y)}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}.

The function
\gamma
is well defined (because
m(B(y,t))>0
since
m
is doubling on
\Omega
),
and decreasing. In addition,
t\mapsto m(B(y,t))
is continuous, because
m
is absolutely
continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure
(and not because
m
is doubling),
and so
\gamma
is of class
C^{1}
, with a derivative equal to

(14.27)

\gamma^{\prime}(s):=-\frac{s}{m(B(y,s))}.

Next we use our function
\gamma
and set

(14.28)

\alpha_{r}(s):=\phi\left(\frac{\gamma(r)}{\gamma(s)}\right)\hbox{ for }0<r,s<\gamma(y).

By construction,

(14.29)

\alpha_{r}\equiv 1\text{ on }[0,r),

(14.30)

\alpha_{r}(s)=0
when
\gamma(s)<\frac{1}{2}\gamma(r)
,

and
\alpha_{r}
is of class
C^{1}
on
(0,\delta(y))
, with a derivative equal to

-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}(s)\gamma(r)}{\gamma(s)^{2}}\,\phi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\gamma(r)}{\gamma(s)}\right)
.
Thus

(14.31)

\alpha^{\prime}_{r}\text{ is supported on the interval where }\ \frac{1}{2}\gamma(r)\leq\gamma(s)\leq\gamma(r)

(recall that
\gamma
is decreasing), and

(14.32)

|\alpha^{\prime}_{r}(s)|\leq 8\,\frac{\gamma^{\prime}(s)}{\gamma(r)}.

We shall also need a variation of the maximum principle (Lemma
12.6
).

Lemma 14.33
.

Let
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed set and
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
an open set such that

F\subset E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\,\mathrm{dist}(F,\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus E)>0
.
Let
u\in W_{r}(E\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a supersolution
for
L
in
\Omega\cap E
such that

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{E\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dm<+\infty
,

(ii)

\Tr u\geq 0
a.e. on
\Gamma\cap E
,

(iii)

u\geq 0
a.e. in
(E\setminus F)\cap\Omega
.

Then
u\geq 0
a.e. in
E\cap\Omega
.

Proof
.  The proof of this result is the same as the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.3]
.
\Box

We are now ready to establish a lower bound on
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)
when
x
and
y
are close. Those lower bounds are not necessary in our article to prove the existence of the Green function, and a reader
who is only interested in existence can skip the next lemma.

Lemma 14.34
.

If
x,y\in\Omega
are such that
40\rho\leq 2|x-y|\leq\delta(y)
, then

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\geq C^{-1}\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t},

where
C>0
depends on
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  The first point that we need to verify is that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)
is increasing when
x\to y
, at least in a weak sense, and when
|x-y|
is way bigger than
\rho
. Pick
r>10\rho
. The function
v_{r}:=(\sup_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
- when
B_{s}
denotes as usual
B(y,s)
- is a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{r/2}}
. Moreover we can easily observe that
\Tr v_{r}\geq 0
and
v_{r}\geq 0
on
B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}
.
We can apply
Lemma
14.33
with
E=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\overline{B_{r/2}}
and
F=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}
,
and in particular (i) holds because
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
;
we deduce that

v_{r}\geq 0
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{r/2}}
, that is,

(14.35)

\sup_{\Omega\setminus B_{r/2}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq\sup_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}.

For the rest of the proof, we write
r
for
|x-y|
, and for
i\geq 0
, we set
r_{i}
as the only value such that

(14.36)

\gamma(r_{i})=2^{-i}\gamma(r).

Such a point exists, because
\gamma
is a (strictly) decreasing continuous function with

\gamma(\delta(y))=0
. Notice that
r_{0}=r
, and
\{r_{i}\}
is an increasing sequence whoes limit
is
\delta(y)
(but we won’t go that far).
First, we use the test function on
\eta_{1}(x):=\alpha_{r_{1}}(|x-y|)
in (
14.3
). Thanks to (
14.31
),
\nabla\eta_{1}
is supported in
B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}
and one obtains

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\eta_{1}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r_{1})}\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}||\gamma^{\prime}(|x-y|)|\,dm

(14.37)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r_{1})}\left(\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

We now want to prove that

(14.38)

\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}\,dm\leq C\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}\leq C\gamma(r_{1}).

The second part is just the definition of
\gamma(r_{1})
(we integrate further);
the first inequality will be a little longer to prove, because we want to avoid
the unpleasant situation where
B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}
is a very thin annulus.

Let
C_{4}
denote the doubling constant for
m
, as in (
2.3
), then set

C^{\prime}_{4}=2C_{4}+4
, and let
\tau>0
be small, to be chosen soon, depending on
C_{4}
.

First assume that
(1+\tau)r_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq 2r_{1}
.
Then the integrand on the left is comparable to
r_{1}^{2}m(B(y,r_{2})^{-2}
and

m(B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}})
is comparable to
m(B(y,r_{2})
; the desired inequality follows,
with a constant that depends on
\tau
, because
\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\frac{ds}{s}\geq C\tau^{-1}
.
When
r_{2}>2r_{1}
, this is also easy: cut
B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}
into annuli
of modulus comparable to
1
, and prove the inequality separately on each one as we just did.

We may now assume that
r_{2}\leq(1+\tau)r_{1}
; our defense will be that this does not happen
in the present circumstances. We claim that if
\tau
is chosen so small that
C^{\prime}_{4}\tau<1
,

r_{2}\leq(1+\tau)r_{1}
implies that
(1+C^{\prime}_{4}\tau)r_{1}\geq\delta(y)
.

Indeed, suppose instead that
(1+C^{\prime}_{4}\tau)r_{1}<\delta(y)
.
By definition,
\gamma(r_{1})=2\gamma(r_{1})
, hence by (
14.26
)

(14.39)

\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}=\int_{r_{2}}^{\delta(y)}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}\geq\int_{(1+\tau)r_{1}}^{(1+C^{\prime}_{4}\tau)r_{1}}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}.

The left-hand side is at most
(r_{2}-r_{1})r_{2}m(B_{r_{1}})^{-1}\leq 2\tau r_{1}^{2}m(B_{r_{1}})^{-1}

because
\tau<1
, and the left-hand side is at least

[(C^{\prime}_{4}-1)\tau r_{1}]\,[(1+C^{\prime}_{4}\tau)r_{1}]\,m(B_{2r_{1}})^{-1}

because
C^{\prime}_{4}\tau<1
; since
m(B_{2r_{1}})\leq C_{4}m(B_{r_{1}})
because
m
is doubling,
this is at least
C_{4}^{-1}(C^{\prime}_{4}-1)\tau r_{1}^{2}m(B_{r_{1}})^{-1}
. We chose
C^{\prime}_{4}=2C+4
,
and the ensuing contradiction proves the claim.

For the purposes of this lemma, we can take
\tau=(100C^{\prime}_{4})^{-1}
, and then we just proved that
(
14.38
) holds as soon as
(1+10^{-2})r_{1}\leq\delta(y)
. Similarly,

(14.40)

\int_{B_{r_{i+1}}\setminus B_{r_{i}}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}\,dm\leq C\int_{r_{i}}^{r_{i+1}}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}\leq C\gamma(r_{i})

as long as
(1+10^{-2})r_{i}\leq\delta(y)
. We want to show that this does not
happen for
i\leq 2
, so we need a control on the variations of
\delta(y)-r_{i}

along our sequence. Let us check that

(14.41)

\delta(y)-r_{i}\leq 3(\delta(y)-r_{i+1})\,\text{ for $i\geq 0$ such that }r_{i+1}\geq\frac{5}{6}\delta(y).

Suppose not, set
R=\delta(y)-3(\delta(y)-r_{i+1})>r_{i}
, and observe that (as in (
14.39
))

(14.42)

\int_{R}^{r_{i+1}}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}<\int_{r_{i}}^{r_{i+1}}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}=\int_{r_{i+1}}^{\delta(y)}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B(y,t))}\,\frac{dt}{t}.

When we replace
m(B(y,t))
by the larger number
m(B(y,r_{i+1}))
on the left-hand side,
we get a smaller integral; similarly, when we replace
m(B(y,t))
by the smaller number
m(B(y,r_{i+1}))

on the right-hand side, we get a larger integral. Hence

\int_{R}^{r_{i+1}}tdt<\int_{r_{i+1}}^{\delta(y)}tdt
.
Notice that
R\geq\delta(y)/2
because
r_{i+1}\geq\frac{5}{6}\delta(y)

and the interval on the left is twice as long as on the right; this gives a contradiction, and
(
14.41
) follows.

We may now prove that in the present circumstances, (
14.38
) holds,
and even (
14.40
) for
0\leq i\leq 2
.
Indeed, we start from
r_{0}=r=|x-y|\leq\delta(y)/2
, so
\delta(y)-r_{0}\geq\delta(y)/2
,
and it follows from a short iteration of (
14.41
) that
\delta(y)-r_{2}\geq\delta(y)/24
,
and so
(1+10^{-2})r_{2}\leq\delta(y)
.

We may now return to (
14
). Since
\gamma(r_{1})\approx\gamma(r_{0})=\gamma(r)

by (
14.36
),
(
14
) and (
14.38
) imply that

(14.43)

1\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)}\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm.

Next,
since
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
is a solution to
Lu=0
in
B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r}
, we can use the Cacciopoli inequality (Lemma
11.12
) with the test function
\eta_{2}(x):=\alpha_{r_{2}}(|x-y|)[1-\alpha_{r_{0}}(|x-y|)]
,
which lies in
C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r})
and satisfies
\eta_{2}\equiv 1
on
B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}
.
This yields

\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}|^{2}\,dm\leq C\int_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}|\nabla\eta_{2}|^{2}\,dm\leq C\sup_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}\int_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}|\nabla\eta_{2}|^{2}\,dm.

We bound the gradient of
\eta_{2}
with the help of (
14.32
), (
14.27
), and (
14.36
).
We find
that
|\nabla\eta_{2}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)}\frac{|x-y|}{m(B_{|x-y|})}
.
So by (
14.40
)

\int_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}|\nabla\eta_{2}|^{2}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)^{2}}\int_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)}.

As a consequence,

\int_{B_{r_{2}}\setminus B_{r_{1}}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)}\sup_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}

and,
together with (
14.43
),

(14.44)

1\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)^{2}}\sup_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}.

To conclude, we invoke (
14.35
) and then the interior Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
),
to get (since
r_{0}=r
)

\sup_{B_{r_{3}}\setminus B_{r_{0}}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq\sup_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\leq C\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x).

We use the above estimate in (
14.44
) and notice
\gamma(r)
is exactly the bound required
for Lemma
14.34
(see (
14.26
)); the lemma follows.

\Box

Lemma 14.45
.

Suppose that
\rho\leq 10^{-2}\delta(y)
and
r<\delta(y)/2
, and set
B_{r}=B(y,r)
as usual. Then

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\leq C\int_{r}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(y,s))}\,\frac{ds}{s},

where
C>0
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  By (
14.3
)
for the test function
v:=\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
(and the elliptic condition (
11.3
)),

(14.46)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\lesssim\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm=\fint_{B_{\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm.

Then cut the last integral in two; this yields

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm&\lesssim\fint_{B_{\rho}}\left(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\fint_{B_{50\rho}\setminus B_{25\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm\right)\,dm+\fint_{B_{50\rho}\setminus B_{25\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm\\
&\lesssim\fint_{B_{50\rho}}\left|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}-\fint_{B_{50\rho}\setminus B_{25\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm\right|\,dm+\fint_{B_{50\rho}\setminus B_{25\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm.\end{split}

We use the Poincaré inequality (Theorem
5.24
) to bound the
first integral, and Lemma
14.16
to bound the last one.
Notice in particular that
40\rho\leq 2|x-y|<\delta(y)
for
x\in B_{50\rho}\setminus B_{25\rho}
,
so the lemma applies. This gives

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm&\lesssim\rho\left(\fint_{B_{50\rho}}\left|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\right|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\int_{25\rho}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}\\
&\lesssim\frac{25\rho}{m(B_{25\rho})^{1/2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\right|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\int_{25\rho}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}.\end{split}

Since
m
is doubling,
m(B_{t})\leq C_{4}m(B_{25\rho})
for
25\rho\leq t\leq 50\rho
, so

\frac{(25\rho)^{2}}{m(B_{25\rho})}\leq C_{4}\int_{25\rho}^{50\rho}\frac{t^{2}}{m(B_{t})}\frac{dt}{t}\leq C_{4}\gamma(25\rho)

(because
50\rho<\delta(y)
and by the definition (
14.26
)).
So the above estimate can be written

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\lesssim\gamma(25\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\right|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\gamma(25\rho),

or

(14.47)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\lesssim\gamma(25\rho):=\int_{25\rho}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}.

The estimate (
14.47
) is
already good, and it proves
Lemma
14.45
for any
r>0
such that

\gamma(r)\geq\gamma(25\rho)/2
. Assume now that
\gamma(r)\leq\gamma(25\rho)/2
.
Since
\gamma
is decreasing, there is a unique
R\in[25\rho,r)
such that

\gamma(R)=2\gamma(r)
.
Then
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
is a solution to
Lu=0
in
\Omega\setminus B_{R}
.
By Lemma
14.16
and the proof of (
14.35
),

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\gamma(R)=2C\gamma(|x-y|)

for any
x\in\Omega\setminus B_{R}
.
We claim that if
\eta_{3}
is a nonnegative smooth function that satisfies

\eta_{3}\equiv 0
on
B_{R}
,
we have the following Cacciopoli-type inequality:

(14.48)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}|\eta_{3}|^{2}\,dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}|\nabla\eta_{3}|^{2}dm.

The above bound is not an application of the Cacciopoli inequalities stated in Section
11
,
because the test function
\eta_{3}
is not
contained in a ball
2B
such that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
is a solution in
2B
.
However, the proof of (
14.48
) is
very similar to the proof of the usual Cacciopoli inequality, and we leave it to the reader.
It relies on
the fact that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\eta_{3}\in W_{0}
even though
\eta_{3}
is not compactly supported.

We use (
14.48
) with
\eta_{3}(x):=1-\alpha_{R}(|x|)
,
where
\alpha_{R}

is the function in (
14.28
).
Notice that
\eta_{3}(x)=0
on
B_{R}
and
\eta_{3}(x)=1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}

(because
\gamma(|x|)<\gamma(r)=\gamma(R)/2
).
So we obtain

(14.49)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{\gamma(r)^{2}}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{R}}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)|^{2}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}dm(x).

by
(
14.32
) and
(
14.27
). But for
x\in B_{r}\setminus B_{R}
, Lemma
14.16
says that

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\gamma(R)=2C\gamma(r)
; hence by the proof of (
14.38
),

(14.50)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\lesssim\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{R}}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))^{2}}dm(x)\lesssim\gamma(R)\lesssim\gamma(r).

Lemma
14.45

follows.

\Box

Lemma 14.51
.

There exists

q>1
,
that depends only on
C_{4}
,
such that for any
y\in\Omega

and any
\rho\leq\delta(y)/100
,

\int_{B(y_{0},2\delta(y))}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}\,dm\leq Cm(B_{\delta(y)})\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}\right)^{q},

where
y_{0}\in\Gamma
is such that
|y-y_{0}|=\delta(y)
,
and where
C>0
depends only on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Remark 14.52
.

If

\gamma(r):=\int_{r}^{\delta}(y)\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}
is uniformly bounded,
or in other words
if
\int_{0}^{\delta}(y)\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}
is finite, then
by (
14.47
) we can take
q=2
in Lemma
14.51
.

Proof
.  We first start by proving general results, which are only consequences of
the doubling property
(
H4
).
There exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

(14.53)

m(B)\leq\alpha m(2B)

for every ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
2B\subset\Omega
.
Indeed, if
r
denotes
the radius of
B
, then we can find a ball
B_{0}
of radius
r/2
in
2B\setminus B
.
Then
B\subset 3B_{0}
, hence
m(B)\leq C_{4}^{2}m(B_{0})
by (
H4
),
and now
m(2B)-m(B)\geq m(B_{0})\geq C_{4}^{-2}m(B)
, and (
14.53
)
holds with
\alpha=(1+C_{4}^{-2})^{-1}
.

Similarly to (
2.5
), the estimate (
14.53
) can be improved into

(14.54)

m(B(y,r))\leq C\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2\epsilon}m(B(y,s))\qquad\text{ for }r\leq s\leq\delta(y),

where
C,\epsilon>0
depends only on
C_{4}
,
and we use
2\epsilon
instead of
\epsilon
to simplify the later computations. Indeed, let
k
be the integer such that
2^{-k-1}<r/s\leq 2^{-k}
.
Then by (
14.53
)

\begin{split}m(B(y,r))&\leq m(B(y,2^{-k}s))\leq\alpha^{k}m(B(y,s))\leq(2^{-k})^{\ln_{2}(1/\alpha)}m(B(y,s))\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{\ln_{2}(1/\alpha)}m(B(y,s)).\end{split}.

The claim (
14.54
) follows by taking
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\ln_{2}(1/\alpha)>0
.

Let us use again
B_{s}
for
B(y,s)
. The inequality (
14.54
) implies in particular that

\frac{r^{\epsilon}}{m(B_{r})}\geq C\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{-\epsilon}\frac{s^{\epsilon}}{m(B_{s})}\qquad\text{ for }r\leq s\leq\delta(y),

which proves that the function
r\to\delta(y)^{1-\epsilon}r^{\epsilon}/m(B_{r})
reaches all the values between
\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})
and
+\infty
. Moreover if
t
is in the given range, the values of
t

that satisfy
t=\delta(y)^{1-\epsilon}r^{\epsilon}/m(B_{r})
are all the same up to a harmless constant.

For the next step we
prove weak
L^{q}
estimates on the gradient of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
.
Set
\widehat{\Omega}_{t}:=\{x\in\Omega,\,|\nabla g^{\rho}(x)|>t\}
.
Thanks to Lemma
14.45
, for all
r\in(0,\delta(y)/2)
, we have

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq m(B_{r})+\frac{C}{t^{2}}\int_{r}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B_{s})}\frac{ds}{s}.

Then by (
14.54
)

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq m(B_{r})+\frac{C}{t^{2}}\frac{1}{m(B_{r})}\int_{r}^{\delta(y)}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2\epsilon}s\,ds.

Or,
since we can always chose
\epsilon<1
,

(14.55)

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq m(B_{r})+\frac{C}{t^{2}}\frac{\delta(y)^{2(1-\epsilon)}r^{2\epsilon}}{m(B_{r})}.

We aim to optimize the above expression in
r
. But we shall only care about big values of
t
,
so let us only consider
t\geq\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})
for the moment.
First assume that
2^{-\epsilon}\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)/2})\geq t\geq\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})
.
Then we choose
r=\delta(y)/2
in (
14.55
), and it is easy to see, using (
H4
)
and (
2.5
) in particular, that

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Cm(B_{\delta(y)})^{-\frac{\epsilon}{d-\epsilon}}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{t}\right)^{\frac{d}{d-\epsilon}},

where

d=d_{m}>0
is the (possibly large) exponent of (
2.5
).
Notice that we may always replace
d_{m}
with a larger exponent in (
2.5
), so
we may assume that
d\geq 2\epsilon
, and this way the exponent

-\frac{\epsilon}{d-\epsilon}
is rather small and negative.
We strive for the same bound when
t\geq 2^{-\epsilon}\delta(y)m(B_{\delta(y)/2})
. We then take

r
such that

(14.56)

t=\delta(y)^{1-\epsilon}r^{\epsilon}/m(B_{r}),

and we have seen in the previous paragraph that, even if we may have different choices for
r
, they are all the same up to a constant. Using
r
as in (
14.56
) in (
14.55
), we obtain that

(14.57)

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\lesssim m(B_{r})=\frac{\delta(y)^{1-\epsilon}r^{\epsilon}}{t}.

Yet, by (
14.56
) and (
2.5
),

\begin{split}m(B_{r})&=\frac{\delta(y)}{t}\left(\frac{r}{\delta(y)}\right)^{\epsilon}\leq C\frac{\delta(y)}{t}\left(\frac{m(B_{r})}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}\right)^{\frac{\epsilon}{d}},\end{split}

or equivalently

m(B_{r})^{\frac{d-\epsilon}{d}}\leq C\,\frac{\delta(y)}{t}m(B_{\delta(y)})^{-\frac{\epsilon}{d}}.

Using this bound on
m(B_{r})
in (
14.57
), we obtain that

(14.58)

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Cm(B_{r})\leq C^{\prime}m(B_{\delta(y)})^{-\frac{\epsilon}{d-\epsilon}}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{t}\right)^{\frac{d}{d-\epsilon}}

as desired.

We are ready to conclude.
We write
q_{0}
for
\frac{d}{d-\epsilon}>1
. The bound (
14.58
) becomes

(14.59)

m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Cm(B_{\delta(y)})^{1-q_{0}}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{t}\right)^{q_{0}}.

We take
q=(1+q_{0})/2>1
. Then

\begin{split}\frac{1}{q}\int_{B(y_{0},2\delta(y))}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}\,dm&=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t}\cap B(y_{0},2\delta(y)))dt\\
&\leq m(B(y_{0},2\delta(y)))\int_{0}^{\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})}t^{q-1}dt+\int_{\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})}^{\infty}t^{q-1}m(\widehat{\Omega}_{t})dt.\end{split}

Then by (
H4
) and (
14.59
),

\begin{split}\int_{B(y_{0},2\delta(y))}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}\,dm&\lesssim m(B_{\delta(y)})\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}\right)^{q}+m(B_{\delta(y)})\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}\right)^{q_{0}}\int_{\delta(y)/m(B_{\delta(y)})}^{\infty}t^{q-q_{0}-1}dt\\
&\lesssim m(B_{\delta(y)})\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}\right)^{q}\end{split}

since
q<q_{0}
. Lemma
14.51

follows.
\Box

We are now ready for the big theorem.

Theorem 14.60
.

There exists a non-negative function
g:\ \Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

with the following properties.

(i)

For any
y\in\Omega
and
any function
\alpha\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
\alpha\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of
y

(14.61)

(1-\alpha)g(.,y)\in W_{0}.

In particular,
g(.,y)\in W_{r}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\})\subset L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\},dm)
and
\Tr[g(.,y)]=0
on
\Gamma
.

(ii)

There exists
q>1
that depends only on
C_{4}
such that for every choice of
y\in\Omega
,

(14.62)

\nabla g(.,y)\in L^{q}(B(y,\delta(y)),dm).

(iii)

For

y\in\Omega
and

\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,

(14.63)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla_{x}g(x,y)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)dx=\varphi(y).

In particular,
g(.,y)
is a solution
of
Lu=0

in
\Omega\setminus\{y\}
.

In addition, the following bounds hold.

(iv)

For
x,y\in\Omega
such that
|x-y|\geq\delta(y)/10
,

(14.64)

0\leq g(x,y)\leq C\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)},

where
C>0
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

(v)

For
x,y\in\Omega
such that
|x-y|\leq\delta(y)/2
,

(14.65)

c\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(y,s))}\frac{ds}{s}\leq g(x,y)\leq C\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(y,s))}\frac{ds}{s},

where
C>0
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
; and where
c>0
depends on
C_{4}
,
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

(vi)

For
r\in(0,\delta(y)/2)
and
y\in\Omega
,

(14.66)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B(y,r)}|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|^{2}dm(x)\leq C\int_{r}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(y,s))}\frac{ds}{s},

where
C>0
depends again on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

(vii)

If
q>1
is the exponent in (
14.62
)

(14.67)

\left(\fint_{B(y,\delta(y))}|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|^{q}dm(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B(y,\delta(y))},

where
C>0
depends as usual on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  As we shall see, we already have all the desired estimates on the
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}:=g^{\rho}(x,y)
; the proof will mainly
consist in choosing a right limit to those
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
.

We start with a standard exercise on compactness.
For every compact set
K
in
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}
,
Lemmas
14.5
,
14.6
, and
14.16
prove that
the set
F_{K}:=\big\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x),\,;\,x\in K\text{ and }0<\rho<\,\mathrm{dist}(y,K)/100\big\}
is bounded;
then by Lemma
11.32
the functions
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
,
\rho<\,\mathrm{dist}(y,K)/100
are Hölder continuous
on
K
(on a slightly smaller compact set), with uniform bounds.
In particular, for every compact set
K\subset\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}

the set
A_{K}:=\big\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\,;\,0<\rho\leq\,\mathrm{dist}(y,K)/200\big\}
- seen as a subset of the continuous functions on
K
- is equicontinuous.
Ascoli’s theorem entails that
A_{K}
is relatively compact in
C^{0}(K)
, that is we can find a sequence
of radii
\rho
, that tends to
0
, such that the corresponding
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
converge, uniformly on
K
,
to a (continuous) function written
\mathfrak{g}_{K}
.
We take a sequence of compacts sets
K_{i}
such that
K_{i}\subset K_{i+1}
and

\bigcup_{i}K_{i}=\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}
, and by a diagonal process, we can find a sequence
(\rho_{\eta})_{\eta\in\mathbb{N}}
and a continuous function
\mathfrak{g}
on
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}

such that
\rho_{\eta}\to 0
and

(14.68)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
converges to
\mathfrak{g}
uniformly on every
compact set of
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}
.

We shall use again the cut-off functions
\alpha_{r}
defined in (
14.28
) and their properties. Set
\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}(x)=\alpha_{r}(|x-y|)
;
we want to prove that the
\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})\}_{\eta\in\mathbb{N}}

form a Cauchy sequence in
W_{0}
.
For any
r<\delta(y)/2
, define
r_{1}\in(r,\delta(y))

as the only value such that
\gamma(r_{1})=\gamma(r)/2
; then
for
\eta,\nu\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla[(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\nu}})

\displaystyle(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})]|^{2}\,dm

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\Omega}|\nabla[\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\nu}}]|^{2}|1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}|^{2}\,dm+2\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\nu}}|^{2}|\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}|^{2}\,dm

(14.69)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\nu}}|^{2}|\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}|^{2}\,dm

where, for the last line, we take
\eta,\nu
big enough so that
\rho_{\eta},\rho_{\nu}

are way smaller than
r
and we use the Cacciopoli-type inequality (
14.48
).
Since
\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}

is supported in
B_{r_{1}}\setminus B_{r}
, and the later is a compact set in

\Omega\setminus\{y\}
, the convergence (
14.68
) forces the right-hand side of (
14
) to tend to
0
.
In addition, all the
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}_{\eta}
have a vanishing trace,
and so do the
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})

(see Lemma
6.21
).
We deduce that
\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})\}_{\eta\in\mathbb{N}}

is indeed a Cauchy sequence in
W_{0}
, so it converges
strongly
in
W_{0}
to a function
g^{(r)}
. By uniqueness of the limit,

\mathfrak{g}^{(r)}=\mathfrak{g}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})
.
In short, we proved that for
0<r<\delta(y)/2

(14.70)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})

converges strongly to
\mathfrak{g}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})

in
W_{0}
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{g}
has a gradient in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega\setminus\{y\},dm)
defined as

(14.71)

\nabla\mathfrak{g}(x)=\nabla[\mathfrak{g}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})]
if

\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}(x)=0
.

We still need a last convergence, one that goes across the pole
\{y\}
. Lemma
14.51
provides us with the uniform bound

\left(\fint_{B_{\delta(y)}}|\nabla g^{\rho_{\eta}}|^{q}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\frac{\delta(y)}{m(B_{\delta(y)})}.

So,
up to a subsequence, the quantities
\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
converges
weakly
to a function
h\in L^{q}(B_{\delta(y)},dm)
. But since
\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
already converges to
\nabla\mathfrak{g}
in
L^{2}_{loc}(B_{\delta(y)}\setminus\{y\},dm)
, it forces
\nabla\mathfrak{g}=h
except maybe at the point
y
, but it has no importance because
m(\{y\})=0
. To sum up,

(14.72)

\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
converges weakly to
\nabla\mathfrak{g}
in
L^{q}(B_{\delta(y)},dm)
.

Now
let us show
(i)
–
(vii)
of the theorem.
For the first statement
(i)
, let us start with the more likely situation where

\lim_{r\to 0}\gamma(r)=+\infty
. Since
\alpha=1
near
y
,
we can we can find
s>0
such that
|y-x|>s
when
\alpha(x)\neq 1
.
Choose
r
so small that
\gamma(r)>2\gamma(s)
; then for
x
such that
\alpha(x)\neq 1
,

\gamma(|x-y|)\leq\gamma(s)<\frac{1}{2}\gamma(r)
, so

\widetilde{\alpha}_{r}(x)=\alpha_{r}(|x-y|)=0
by (
14.30
).
Because of this,
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}=(1-\alpha)(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r})\mathfrak{g}
.
This function lies in
W_{0}
, as needed, by (
14.70
) and Lemma
6.21
.

In the other case when
\lim_{r\to 0}\gamma(r)<+\infty
, we are in the happy situation where (
14.47
)
says that
\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq C
, with a constant that depends on
y
,
but not on
\rho
; then the almost everywhere pointwise limit
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
satisfies

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}|^{2}dm\leq C
too, and its trace is still
0
on
\Gamma
.
Finally
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}
does the same; see for instance the proof of Lemma
9.15
for the limit, and Lemma
10.6
for the product. This takes care of
(i)
.

The statement
(ii)
is part of (
14.72
).

For the identity
(iii)
, we take
r_{0}
so that

\gamma(r_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\delta(y)/2)
.
We then write
\varphi=\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{2}
where

\varphi_{1}=\varphi\widetilde{\alpha}_{r_{0}}

and
\varphi_{2}=\varphi(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{r_{0}})
.
The function
\varphi_{1}
is continuous and smooth enough
for
\nabla\varphi_{1}
to lie in
L^{q^{\prime}}(B_{\delta(y)},dm)
,
and so by (
14.72
)
and then definition (
14.3
),

(14.73)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla\mathfrak{g}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{1}\,dx=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{1}\,dm=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\fint_{B_{\rho_{\eta}}}\varphi_{1}\,dm=\varphi_{1}(x)=\varphi(x).

When
x\in B_{r_{0}}
,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{r_{0}}(x)=\alpha_{r_{0}}(|x-y|)=1
by (
14.29
),
and hence
\varphi_{2}(x)=0
. But otherwise
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\delta(y)/2}(x)=\alpha_{\delta(y)/2}(|x-y|)=0
because
\gamma(|x-y|)\leq\gamma(r_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\delta(y)/2)
, and by (
14.30
). Hence

\mathfrak{g}(1-\alpha_{\delta(y)/2})=\mathfrak{g}
on the support of
\nabla\varphi_{2}
, and so

(14.74)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}A\nabla\mathfrak{g}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}\,dx&=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla[\mathfrak{g}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{\delta(y)/2})]\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}\,dx=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla[\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}(1-\widetilde{\alpha}_{\delta(y)/2})]\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}\,dm\\
&=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}\,dm=\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\fint_{B_{\rho_{\eta}}}\varphi_{2}\,dm=\varphi_{2}(x)=0\end{split}

where we used (
14.70
) for the second equality
and then returned by the same path.
The combination of (
14.73
) and (
14.74
) infers
(iii)
.

The estimates given in
(iv)
and
(v)
are direct consequences of Lemmas
14.5
,
14.6
,
14.16
,
14.34
, and the convergence (
14.68
). The bound found in
(vi)
is due to Lemma
14.45
and (
14.70
), while
(vii)
comes from Lemma
14.51
and (
14.72
).
Theorem
14.60

follows.
\Box

Remark 14.75
.

Before stating the next result, let us comment a bit on
Theorem
14.60
.
One can easily see that
g(.,y)
lies
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\},dm)
, since the latter is bigger than
the space of
continuous functions on
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}
(and
g(.,y)
is continuous on
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}
thanks to (
14.68
)). However, we said nothing about the integration of
g(.,y)
on a neighborhood of
\{y\}
.
The fact that
g(.,y)
can be integrated over a bounded region that covers
\{y\}
is a simple consequence of (
14.65
). Indeed, if
y\in\Omega
and
r\leq\delta(y)/2
,
first observe that

\int_{B(y,r)}g(x,y)\,dm(x)\leq C\int_{B(y,r)}\int_{|x-y|}^{\delta(y)}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(y,s))}\frac{ds}{s}\,dm(x)\leq C\int_{t=0}^{r}\int_{s=t}^{\delta(y)}\frac{m(B(y,t))}{m(B(y,s))}\,s\,ds\frac{dt}{t}.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be the constant in (
14.54
); then

\int_{B(y,r)}g(x,y)\,dm(x)\leq C\int_{0}^{r}\int_{t}^{\delta(y)}\,s^{1-2\epsilon}ds\,t^{2\epsilon-1}dt\leq C\delta(y)^{2-2\epsilon}r^{2\epsilon}.

In addition, by (
14.64
)

\int_{B(y,\delta(y))\setminus B(y,\delta(y)/2)}g(x,y)\,dm\leq Cm(B(y,\delta(y))\sup_{x\in B(y,\delta(y))\setminus B(y,\delta(y)/2)}g(x,y)\leq C\delta(y)^{2}.

The combination of the last two
estimates implies
that

(14.76)

\int_{B(y,\delta(y))}g(x,y)\,dm\leq C\delta(y)^{2}.

Due to (
14.68
), the functions
g^{\rho_{\eta}}(.,y)
converges pointwise a.e. to

g(.,y)
on
B(y,\delta(y))
. So by
the Lebesgue domination theorem
(and the fact that bounds above are also valid for the
g^{\rho_{\eta}}
),
we even have

\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\int_{B(y,\delta(y))}|g^{\rho_{\eta}}(x,y)-g(x,y)|\,dm(x)=0.

Together with (
14.68
), we proved that

(14.77)

the functions
g^{\rho_{\eta}}(.,y)
converge
to
g(.,y)
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega})
.

For the next lemma, we need some additional notation. We write
A^{T}
for the transpose
matrix of
A
, i.e.
(A^{T})_{ij}(x)=A_{ji}(x)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
x\in\Omega
. Obviously,
A^{T}
satisfies the ellipticity and boundedness conditions (
11.1
)–(
11.2
) with the same constant as
A
. The elliptic operator
L_{T}:=-\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A^{T}\nabla
enjoys the very same properties as
L
, in particular, Theorem
14.60

yields the existence of
g^{T}:\,\Omega\cap\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

with the same properties as
g
(except for (
14.63
), where
A
is replaced by
A^{T}
).

Lemma 14.78
.

With the notation above,

(14.79)

g(x,y)=g_{T}(x,y)\quad\text{ for }x,y\in\Omega.

In particular, the functions

x\to g(y,x)
satisfy
the estimates in Theorem
14.60
.

Proof
.  The result is the same as the one of
[
GW
, Theorem 1.3]
(or Lemma 10.6 in
[
DFM2
]
). Yet, the limits we took in the proof of Theorem
14.60
is a bit different to the one in
[
GW
]
and
[
DFM2
]
. So our result deserves a proof.

Actually, the convergence property (
14.72
) will make the proof very easy for us. Let
x,y\in\Omega
be such that
x\neq y
.
By our
construction of the Green functions,
there exist two sequences
(\rho_{\eta})_{\eta\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\sigma_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}

such that
\rho_{\eta},\sigma_{\nu}\to 0
and

(14.80)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
converges to
\mathfrak{g}
uniformly on any compact set of
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{y\}

and

(14.81)

\mathfrak{g}_{T}^{\sigma_{\nu}}
converges to
\mathfrak{g}_{T}
uniformly on any compact set of
\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{x\}
.

Using (
14.2
) and (
14.4
) for both
g(.,y)
and
g_{T}(.,x)
, we see
that for any
\eta,\nu\in\mathbb{N}

(14.82)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla g^{\rho_{\eta}}(z,y)\cdot\nabla g_{T}^{\sigma_{\nu}}(z,x)\,dm(z)=\fint_{B(y,\rho_{\eta})}g_{T}^{\sigma_{\nu}}(z,x)\,dm(z)=\fint_{B(x,\sigma_{\nu})}g^{\rho_{\eta}}(z,y)\,dm(z)

We use the uniform convergence of
\mathfrak{g}_{T}^{\sigma_{\nu}}
on
\overline{B(y,|x-y|/2)}\subset\Omega\setminus\{x\}
and the uniform convergence of
g(.,y)^{\rho_{\eta}}
on
\overline{B(x,|x-y|/2)}\subset\Omega\setminus\{y\}
given by (
14.80
)–(
14.81
) in the last equality of (
14.82
). We get that

g_{T}(y,x)=g(x,y)
as desired.
\Box

Lemma 14.83
.

The Green function satisfies

(14.84)

g(x,y)\leq C\delta(x)^{\alpha}\frac{|x-y|^{2-\alpha}}{m(B(x,|x-y|)\cap\Omega)}\ \ \text{ for
$x,y\in\Omega$ such that }|x-y|\geq 4\delta(x),

where
C>0
and
\alpha>0
depend only on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  See the proof of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 10.9]
. The arguments are based on the pointwise bounds (
14.64
) and the Hölder regularity at the boundary (Lemma
11.32
). Actually, the coefficient
\alpha
is the one of Lemma
11.32
.

\Box

The next result that we wanted is the representation of solutions by Green functions. More precisely, we wanted to take a smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
and construct
u(x)
for
x\in\Omega
as

(14.85)

u(x)=\int_{\Omega}g(x,y)f(y)dm(y).

We have seen in Remark
14.75
that
g(.,y)

lies in
L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega},dm)
. Moreover, due to
Lemma
14.78
,
we also have that
g(x,.)
is in
L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega},dm)
.
Yet,
in the case of a general weights
w
,
we do not know if
g(x,.)
lies
in the unweighted space
L^{1}_{loc}(\overline{\Omega})
. That is why, in the definition (
14.85
), the function
u
has to be defined as an integral over the measure
m
.

In doing so, the formal identity satisfied by
u
is not
Lu=f
but
Lu=fw
, where
w
is the weight used to define the measure
m
. Another way to see it, maybe more relevant, is to say that
w^{-1}Lu=f
. That is, we are solving the Dirichlet problem
\mathcal{L}u=f
for an elliptic operator
\mathcal{L}=-w^{-1}\mathop{\operatorname{div}}[\mathcal{A}w\nabla]
where
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the classical elliptic conditions (
11.3
)–(
11.4
).

At last, by using
L
instead of
\mathcal{L}:=w^{-1}L
, we are somehow linking the measure
m
to the plain Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. So some readers may want to use
\mathcal{L}
all the time. The theory is identical to what we have done until now, since we only worked with solutions to
Lu=0
before the Green functions, and
Lu=0
is obviously equivalent to
\mathcal{L}u=0
.

We expect from the Green representation of solutions that the function
u=u_{f}
constructed in (
14.85
) lies in
W_{0}
and is a weak solution to
Lu=fw
in the sense that

(14.86)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}f\varphi w=\int_{\Omega}f\varphi\,dm\ \ \text{ for every }\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Our assumptions (
H1
)–(
H6
) are enough to have (
14.86
) and the fact that
\Tr u\equiv 0
(for the former, we still need to be careful about our weird definition of the gradient, and for the later, just use Lemma
14.83
). However, we did not succeed to prove that
u\in W
. That is why our next results will be restricted
to the case where the weight
w
is nice enough, that is when (
H6’
) is satisfied instead of (
H6
)

Lemma 14.87
.

Assume that
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H5
) and (
H6’
). Let
g:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

be the non-negative Green function constructed in Lemma
14.60
. Take
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
and construct
u(x)
for
x\in\Omega
as

(14.88)

u(x)=\int_{\Omega}g(x,y)f(y)dm(y).

Then
u
belongs to
W_{0}
and is the solution to
Lu=fw
(given by Lemma
12.2
) in the sense that

(14.89)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}f\varphi w=\int_{\Omega}f\varphi\,dm\ \ \text{ for every }\varphi\in W_{0}.

Remark 14.90
.

In (
14.88
) and (
14.89
), we can replace
dm
by the classical
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
dx
.

Proof
.  The proof is the same as the one of Lemma 10.7 in
[
DFM2
]
. It relies on the fact that the solutions to
Lu=fw
are continuous inside
\Omega
, because as long as we consider inside estimates, (
H6’
) implies that the classical unweighted elliptic theory can be applied. See Theorem 8.22 in
[
GT
]
for the theorem in the classical case.

We also need the fact that the approximations
g^{\rho}(.,y)
converges in
L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega)
to
g(.,y)
. Under (
H6’
), this result is a consequence of the weak
L^{q}
convergence of the gradients and the
L^{1}
-Poincaré inequality for inside balls, the latter is true because inside estimates works here exactly like the classical unweighted case. With only (
H6
), we can only use
L^{2}
- or
L^{2-\epsilon}
- Poincaré inequalities.

\Box

Lemma 14.91
.

Assume that
(\Omega,m,\mu)
satisfies (
H1
)–(
H5
) and (
H6’
).
There exists a unique function
g:\Omega\times\Omega\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
such that

g(x,.)
is continuous on
\Omega\setminus\{x\}

and locally integrable in
\Omega
for every

x\in\Omega
, and such that for every

f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)

the function
u
given by

(14.92)

u(x):=\int_{\Omega}g(x,y)f(y)dm(y)

belongs to
W_{0}
and is a solution of
Lu=f
in the sense that

(14.93)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=\int_{\Omega}f\varphi dm\qquad\text{for every }\varphi\in W_{0}.

Proof
.  See the proof of Lemma 10.8 in
[
DFM2
]
. In short,
the existence is due to Theorem
14.60
,
and Lemmas
14.78
and
14.87
,
while the uniqueness of
g
comes from the uniqueness of
u\in W_{0}
satisfying
Lu=fw
, and the latter is due to Lemma
12.2
.

\Box

15.
Comparison principle

First, let us state the non-degeneracy of the harmonic measure.

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
\alpha>1
,
B:=B(x_{0},r)
be a ball. Take
X_{0}\in\Omega
be any corkscrew point associated to
x_{0}
and
r
given by the assumption (
H1
).
Then

(15.2)

\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\geq C_{\alpha}^{-1}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\frac{1}{\alpha}B

and

(15.3)

\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\geq C^{-1}_{\alpha}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/\alpha),

(15.4)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)+\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C_{\alpha}^{-1}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus\alpha B,

and

(15.5)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)+\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C^{-1}_{\alpha}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/\alpha),

where in the four estimates,
C_{\alpha}
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
,
n
, and
\alpha
.

Remark 15.6
.

The estimates (
15.2
)–(
15.3
) are classical results about the non-degeneracy of the harmonic measure. However, the reader can be at first surprised by the appearance of the Green functions in (
15.4
)–(
15.5
).
The terms that involves the Green functions are yet necessary. Indeed, none of our assumptions stops the boundary
\Gamma
to be a bounded and
\Omega
to be still infinite. Simply take for instance
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
and
\Gamma=\{0\}
with appropriate measure
\mu
and
m
.
Under those conditions, we can actually have
\Gamma\setminus B=\emptyset
, which leads to
\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)=0
for all
X\in\Omega
.

We claim - without proof but we pretend that there are real difficulties to it - that the estimates

(15.7)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)\geq C_{\alpha}^{-1}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus\alpha B

holds whenever
\Omega
is bounded (since the
\Omega\setminus\alpha B
would be empty when
\Gamma\setminus B=\emptyset
) or when we can find a point in
\Gamma
close to
\alpha B
yet outside of
\alpha B
, i.e. whenever
[100B\setminus\alpha B]\cap\Gamma\neq\emptyset
.

At last, the estimate (
15.5
) is given to make it similar to (
15.4
). The harmonic measure is actually unnecessary in (
15.5
).

Proof
.  The proof of (
15.2
)–(
15.3
) is the same as the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.10]
, and relies on the Hölder continuity at the boundary (Lemma
11.32
), the existence of Harnack chains (Proposition
2.18
), and the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
).

Rapidly, the Hölder inequality at the boundary will imply that
\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)
is bigger than
1/2
for any points “close” to
\Gamma\setminus\frac{1}{\alpha}B
. Then we use Harnack chains of balls to link any point in
\frac{1}{\alpha}B
to one of the previous points, and the Harnack inequality repeatedly on the balls of the Harnack chain.

Let us make the proof of (
15.4
)–(
15.5
).
First, let us prove (
15.5
). Thanks to (
14.65
), we have

(15.8)

g(X,X_{0})\geq C^{-1}\int_{\delta(X_{0})/2}^{\delta(X_{0})}\frac{s^{2}}{m(B(X_{0},s))}\frac{ds}{s}\qquad\text{ for all }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2).

Using the doubling property, since
\delta(X_{0})\approx r
, we have
m(B(X_{0},s))/s^{2}\approx m(B\cap\Omega)/r^{2}
for all
s\in(\delta(X_{0})/2,\delta(X_{0}))
. The estimate (
15.8
) becomes

(15.9)

\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C^{-1}\qquad\text{ for all }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2).

We let the reader check that
\Omega\setminus\{X_{0}\}
, obtained from
\Omega
by removing a single point, will still satisfy (
H1
)–(
H2
), maybe with some constant
C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}
smaller than
C_{1},C_{2}
.
Indeed, if
X_{0}
is close to a Corkscrew point for
\Omega
associated to
(x,r)
, then the Corkscrew point for
\Omega
associated to
(x,C_{1}^{-1}r)
will be far from
X_{0}
and so Corkscrew point for
\Omega\setminus\{X_{0}\}
with a constant
C^{\prime}_{1}=(C_{1})^{2}
.
The Harnack chains in
\Omega\setminus\{X_{0}\}
are the same as in
\Omega
, except if they got close to
X_{0}
. In this case, we consider smaller balls, and we avoid
X_{0}
by taking balls in
B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4)\subset\Omega
.
As a consequence, we can link any point from

[\{X\in\Omega,\,\,\mathrm{dist}(X,\Gamma)>\eta r\}\cap 4B]\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4)

to a point in
B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4)
by a Harnack chain of ball with uniformly finite length (the length of the chain is bounded by a constant that depends only on
\eta
and
n
). For the sequel, we write
\Omega_{\eta}
for
\{X\in\Omega,\,\,\mathrm{dist}(X,\Gamma)>\eta r\}
. We use the fact that
g(.,X_{0})
is a solution to
Lu=0
on
\Omega\setminus\{X_{0}\}
and the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
) on each balls of those Harnack chain to improve (
15.9
) into

(15.10)

\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C_{\eta}^{-1}\qquad\text{ for all }X\in\Omega_{\eta}\cap 4B.

In particular, if
\eta=(\alpha-1)/C_{1}
, we get (
15.5
) without the harmonic measure, so we get (
15.5
) since
\omega^{X}
is non-negative.

The proof of (
15.4
) needs additional computations.
We write
h
for the smooth function in
C^{\infty}_{0}((\alpha+1)B/2))
satisfying
0\leq h\leq 1
and
h\equiv 1
on
B
. We set
u_{h}\in W
for the solution to
Lu_{h}=0
with
\Tr u_{h}=1-\Tr h
. By positivity of the harmonic measure,

(15.11)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)\geq u_{h}(X)\geq\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus(\alpha+1)B/2)\geq 0\qquad\text{ for }X\in\Omega.

We prefer
u_{h}
to
\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus B)
because
u_{h}
is in
W
, which makes him suitable for the use of Lemma
14.33
(our maximum principle). The first estimate that we state comes from (
15.10
) without difficulty:

(15.12)

u_{h}(X)+\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C_{\eta}^{-1}\qquad\text{ for all }X\in\Omega_{\eta}\cap[4B\setminus\alpha B].

We want the estimate on the larger set
\Omega\cap[4B\setminus\alpha B]
, so we need to prove that (
15.12
) is also true when
X
is close to
\Gamma
. Let
\eta>0
be small and to be fixed. Let
X\in[\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\eta}]\cap[4B\setminus\alpha B]
. Take
x\in\Gamma
so that
|X-x|\leq\eta r
, which is possible since
X\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\eta}
. Due to the fact that
X
is also in
2B\setminus\alpha B
, it forces
x
to be in
\Gamma\setminus(\alpha-\eta)B
. We chose then
\eta=(\alpha-1)/8
, which makes
x\in\Gamma\setminus\frac{7\alpha+1}{8}B
. Consequently, for
X\in[\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\eta}]\cap[4B\setminus\alpha B]
,

u_{h}(X)\geq\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus(\alpha+1)B/2)\geq\omega^{X}(B(x,3\eta r)\geq C^{-1}

by (
15.11
), the construction of
\eta
and
x
, and (
15.2
). The combination of the last estimate with (
15.12
) entails

(15.13)

u_{h}(X)+\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq C_{\alpha}^{-1}\qquad\text{ for all }X\in\Omega\cap[4B\setminus\alpha B]

since Green functions are non-negative. We finish the proof with the maximum principle given by Lemma
14.33
, which will become our favorite tool for the section. Indeed, we define
v
as

v:=u_{h}(X)+\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})-C_{\alpha}^{-1}

where
C_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the constant in the right-hand side of (
15.13
), and we aim to apply Lemma (
14.33
) for the solution
v
with the sets
E=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\alpha B
and
F=4B\setminus\alpha B
. Recall that the term
u_{h}(X)
lies in
W
. Together with (
14.67
), we deduce that assumption (i) of Lemma
14.33
is true. The other assumptions required by the lemma are given by (
15.13
) and the fact that
\Tr v=1-C_{\alpha}^{-1}>0
on
\Gamma\setminus\alpha B
. We deduce that
v\geq 0
on
E
, which is exactly the desired estimate (
15.4
). The lemma follows.

\Box

If
B
is a ball centered on the boundary
\Gamma
, we bound the values in
B\cap\Omega
of a solution
u
(to
Lu=0
in
KB\cap\Omega
) by the value of
u
at a Corkscrew point associated to the ball
B
.

Lemma 15.14
.

There exists
K:=K(C_{1},C_{2},n)
such that the following holds.

Let
B=B(x_{0},r)
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and let
X_{0}
be a Corkscrew point associated to
x_{0}
and
r
given by (
H1
). Let
u\in W_{r}(KB\cap\overline{\Omega})
be a non-negative,
non identically zero,
solution of
Lu=0
in
KB\cap\Omega
,
such that

\Tr u\equiv 0
on
KB\cap\Gamma
. Then

(15.15)

u(X)\leq Cu(X_{0})\ \ \ \ \text{ for }X\in B\cap\Omega,

where
C>0
depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
.

Proof
.  We get inspiration from the proof of
[
KJ
, Lemma 4.4]
(see also
[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.8]
). Lemma 11.8 in
[
DFM2
]
deals with balls centered at the boundary, and
K=2
. However, in
[
DFM2
]
, the connectedness is not a issue, while we need to be careful here. Indeed, taking the universal constant
K=2
in Lemma
15.14
is not possible, since nothing garantees that we can link 2 points in
B\cap\Omega
by a path that stays in
2B
.

We solve this problem by taking the tent sets constructed in Section
5
, which can be seen as connected substitute of the sets
B\cap\Omega
. Then we use the property (
5.15
) to conclude.

First, let us recall the following fact. Let
x\in\Gamma
and
s>0
such that
\Tr u\equiv 0
on
B(x,s)\cap\Gamma
. Then the Hölder continuity of solutions given by Lemma
11.31
proves the existence of
\epsilon>0
(that depends on
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
n
) such that

(15.16)

\sup_{B(x,\epsilon s)}u\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{B(x,s)}u.

Without loss of generality, we can choose
\epsilon<1/1000C_{1}<\frac{1}{2}
.

A rough idea of the proof of (
15.15
) is that
u(X)
should not be near the maximum of
u
when
X
lies close to
B\cap\Gamma
, because of (
15.16
). Then we are left with
points
x
that lie far from the boundary, and we can use the Harnack inequality to control
u(x)
.
The difficulty is that when
X\in B\cap\Gamma
lies close to
\Gamma
,
u(x)
can be bounded by values
of
u

inside the domain
, and not by values of
u
near
\Gamma
but from the exterior
of
B
.
We will prove this latter fact by contradiction: we show that if
\sup_{B}u
exceeds a certain bound,
then we can construct a sequence of points
X_{k}\in\frac{K}{2}B
, where
K
is large enough, such that
\delta(X_{k})\to 0
and

u(X_{k})\to+\infty
, and hence we
contradict the Hölder continuity of solutions at the boundary.

As said in the beginning of the proof, the quantities
\lambda B\cap\Omega
lack connectedness, and it will be more convenient to work with a tent set
T_{2Q^{*}}
, which has all the desired connectedness by Lemma
5.23
. The cube
Q^{*}
and the constant
K
are defined as follow. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
2^{-k-2}\leq r\leq 2^{-k-1}
, and we write
Q
for the unique cube in
\mathbb{D}_{k}
containing
x
. Notice that
2Q\supset B\cap\Gamma
, but
T_{2Q}
is not necessarily bigger than
B
, and so we take
Q^{*}
the first ancestor of
Q
such that
\,\mathrm{dist}(T_{2Q},T_{2Q^{*}}^{c})>\ell(Q)
. Check that the difference of generations between
Q
and
Q^{*}
is uniformly finite, and so combined with (
5.15
), we obtain that we can find
K
that depends only on
n
,
c_{1}
, and
C_{2}
such that

(15.17)

2B\cap\Omega\subset T_{2Q^{*}}\subset\frac{1}{2}B^{*}:=\frac{K}{2}B.

We can link any couple of points in
T_{2Q^{*}}
by a chain of balls
B_{i}
that satisfies
2B_{i}\subset 2B^{*}\cap\Omega
. The proof of this fact is similar to the proof of fact that
T_{2Q^{*}}
satisfies the chain condition
C(\kappa,M)
for some
\kappa,M
(see Lemma
5.23
) and thus will be omitted.
Therefore, the fact that
u(X)>0
somewhere, that
T_{2Q^{*}}
is connected, and the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
), maybe applied a few times, yield
u(X_{0})>0
. We can rescale
u
and assume that
u(X_{0})=1
.

We claim
that there exists

M>0
such that for any integer
N\geq 1
and

Y\in T_{2Q^{*}}
,

(15.18)

\delta(Y)\geq\epsilon^{N}r\Longrightarrow u(Y)\leq M^{N},

where
\epsilon

comes from
(
15.16
) and the constant
M
depends only upon
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.
We will prove the claim by induction.
The base case is given by the following. We want to show the existence of
M_{1}\geq 1
such that

(15.19)

u(Y)\leq M_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \text{ for every }Y\in T_{2Q^{*}}\text{ such that }\delta(Y)\geq\epsilon^{2}r.

Indeed, if
Y\in T_{2Q^{*}}
satisfies
\delta(Y)\geq\epsilon^{2}r
, Proposition
2.18
implies that we can link
Y
to
X_{0}
by a chain of balls that stay away from the boundary and with length uniformly bounded by
C(\epsilon)
. We can construct the chain such that it stays also far from the boundary of
B^{*}
1
1

1

This fact is true because by construction of
T_{2Q^{*}}
, one can see that the center of the balls constituting the chain can be taken in
T_{2Q^{*}}
, and (
15.17
) let us a bit of freedom, but if may be easier for the reader to think that this statement would be also true by taking a larger
K
.
Together with the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
), we obtain (
15.19
), and hence (
15.18
) for
N=1,2
as long as
M
is chosen bigger than
M_{1}
.

Now, let
Y\in T_{2Q^{*}}
such that
\delta(Y)\leq\epsilon^{2}r
. By construction of
T_{2Q^{*}}
,
Y
belongs to some
\gamma^{*}_{Q^{*}}(z)
for some
z\in 2Q^{*}
. We take
Z
a Corkscrew point associated to
z
and
C_{1}\delta(Y)/\epsilon
. Since
\epsilon<C_{1}^{-1}
,
Z\in B(z,r)
, and so
Z
stays in
T_{2Q^{*}}
. In addition, by construction,
\delta(Z)\geq\delta(Y)/\epsilon
and
|Z-Y|\leq\delta(Y)/\epsilon^{2}
; these two estimates can be combined to Proposition
2.18
(existence of Harnack chains, as before the chain can stay far from
\partial B^{*}
) and Lemma
11.35
(Harnack inequality) to get we the existence of
M_{2}\geq 1
such that
u(Y)\leq M_{2}u(Z)
.
So we just proved that

(15.20)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{for any $Y\in T_{Q^{*}}$ such that
$\delta(Y)\leq\epsilon^{-2}r$, }\\
\qquad\text{there exists $Z\in T_{2Q^{*}}$ such that $\delta(Z)\geq\delta(Y)/\epsilon$ and $u(Y)\leq M_{2}u(Z)$.}\end{array}

We turn
to the main
induction step. Set
M=\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}\geq 1
and let
N\geq 2
be given.
Assume, by induction hypothesis, that for any
Z\in T_{2Q^{*}}
satisfying
\delta(Z)\geq\epsilon^{N}\ell(Q)
, we have
u(Z)\leq M^{N}
.
Let

Y\in T_{2Q^{*}}
be such that

\delta(Y)\geq\epsilon^{N+1}\ell(Q)
.
The assertion (
15.20
) yields the existence of
Z\in T_{2Q^{*}}
such that
\delta(Z)\geq\delta(Y)/\epsilon\geq\epsilon^{N}\ell(Q)
and
u(Y)\leq M_{2}u(Z)\leq Mu(Z)
.
By the induction hypothesis,
u(Y)\leq M^{N+1}
.
This completes our induction step, and the proof of (
15.18
) for every
N\geq 1
.

Choose an integer
i
such that
2^{i}\geq M
, where
M
is the
constant of (
15.18
) that we just found, and then set
M^{\prime}=M^{i+3}
.
We want to prove by contradiction that

(15.21)

u(X)\leq M^{\prime}u(X_{0})=M^{\prime}\ \ \ \text{ for every }X\in B(x_{0},r).

So we assume that

(15.22)

there exists
X_{1}\in B(x_{0},r)
such that
u(X_{1})>M^{\prime}

and we want to prove by induction that for every
integer
k\geq 1
,

(15.23)

there exists
X_{k}\in T_{2Q^{*}}
such that
u(X_{k})>M^{i+2+k}
and
\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{k},B)\leq(1-2^{1-k})r
.

The base step of the induction is given by (
15.22
) and we want to do
the induction step. Let
k\geq 1
be given and assume that
(
15.23
) holds.
From the contraposition of (
15.18
), we deduce that
\delta(X_{k})<\epsilon^{i+2+k}r
.
Choose

x_{k}\in\Gamma
such that
|X_{k}-x_{k}|=\delta(X_{k})<\epsilon^{i+2+k}r
.
By the induction hypothesis,

(15.24)

\,\mathrm{dist}(x_{k},B)\leq|x_{k}-X_{k}|+\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{k},B)\leq(1-2^{1-k})r+\epsilon^{i+2+k}r

and,
since
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
,

(15.25)

|x_{k}-x_{0}|\leq(1-2^{1-k}-2^{-2-k})r.

Now, due to (
15.16
), we can find
X_{k+1}\in B(x_{k},\epsilon^{2+k}r)
such that

(15.26)

u(X_{k+1})\geq 2^{i}\sup_{X\in B(x_{k},\epsilon^{i+2+k}r)}u(X)\geq 2^{i}u(X_{k})\geq M^{i+2+(k+1)}.

The induction step will be complete if we can prove that
\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{k+1},T_{2Q})\leq(1-2^{-k})r
. Indeed,

(15.27)

\begin{split}\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{k+1},B)&\leq|X_{k+1}-x_{k}|+\,\mathrm{dist}(x_{k},B)\leq\epsilon^{2+k}r+(1-2^{1-k}-2^{-2-k})r\\
&\leq(1-2^{-k})r\end{split}

by (
15.25
) and because
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

Let us sum up. We assumed the existence of
X_{1}\in B
such that
u(X_{1})>M^{\prime}
and we end up
with (
15.23
), that is a sequence
X_{k}
of values in
2B
such that
u(X_{k})

increases to
+\infty
.
Up to a subsequence, we can thus find a point in
\overline{2B}\subset B^{*}
where
u
is not continuous,
which contradicts Lemma
11.32
. Hence

u(X)\leq M^{\prime}=M^{\prime}u(X_{0})
for

X\in B
. Lemma
15.14
follows.

\Box

We can now compare the harmonic measure with the Green function, that can be seen as a weak version of the comparison principle.

Lemma 15.28
.

Let
B:=B(x_{0},r)
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
. Let
X_{0}
is a corkscrew point associated to
x_{0}
and
r
. Then one has

(15.29)

C^{-1}\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\leq\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\leq C\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B,

and

(15.30)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\setminus\frac{5}{4}B)\leq C\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in[B\cap\Omega]\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4),

where
C>0
depends only upon
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.

Remark 15.31
.

The bound (
15.30
) may look a bit artificial.
There is nothing deep about the constant
\frac{5}{4}
in the left-hand side.
We could have used
2
instead, and obtain a statement which looks a little weaker
but is actually equivalent (we leave the proof of this to the reader);
we simply reproduced
\frac{5}{4}
in the form given by our
general comparison principle (Theorem
15.64
).

Observe also that we do not necessarily have
the lower bound in (
15.30
).
See Remark
15.6
.

Proof
.  This lemma is the analogue of
[
DFM2
, Lemmas 11.9 and 11.11]
.

First, we quickly prove the first inequality in (
15.29
). The
upper bound (
14.64
) for the Green function,
together with (
H4
), implies that

(15.32)

0\leq\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\leq C\qquad\text{ for }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4).

As in
the proof of Lemma
15.1
, we take

h\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
such that

0\leq h\leq 1
and
h\equiv 1
on
\frac{1}{2}B
. We set
u_{h}\in W
for the solution to
Lu_{h}=0
with
\Tr u_{h}=\Tr h
. By the
positivity of the harmonic measure,

(15.33)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma\cap\frac{1}{2}B)\leq u_{h}(X)\leq\omega^{X}(\Gamma\cap B)\qquad\text{ for }X\in\Omega.

We combine (
15.33
) with the non-degeneracy of the harmonic measure (
15.3
) to get that

(15.34)

u_{h}\geq C^{-1}\qquad\text{ for }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2).

The estimates (
15.32
) and (
15.34
) easily infer the existence of a constant
\kappa
such that

v(X):=\kappa u_{h}(X)-\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\geq 0\qquad\text{ for }X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4).

The assumptions of Lemma
14.33
for the function
v
and the sets

E=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4)
and

F=B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4)
are satisfied, which implies that

v\geq 0
on
E
, i.e.,

(15.35)

\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})\leq\kappa u_{h}\leq\omega^{X}(\Gamma\cap B)\qquad\text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/4).

This is stronger than
the first inequality in (
15.29
).

We shall also use the following result on Green functions:
for
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap W
and
X\notin\supp\,\phi
,

(15.36)

u_{\phi}(X)=-\int_{\Omega}A\nabla\phi(Y)\cdot\nabla_{y}g(X,Y)dY,

where
u_{\phi}\in W
is the solution ro
Lu=0
, with the Dirichlet condition
\Tr u_{\phi}=\Tr\phi
on
\Gamma
, given by Lemma
12.2
.
The identity (
15.36
) is the same as (11.70) in
[
DFM2
]
,
and its proof - which only relies on the properties on the Green functions given in Section
14
- is the same as in
[
DFM2
]
.

We turn to the proof of the upper bound in (
15.29
), that is,

(15.37)

\omega^{X}(B(x_{0},r)\cap\Gamma)\leq C\,\frac{m(B\cap\Omega)}{r^{2}}\,g(X,X_{0})\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B.

For the rest of the proof,
K
is the constant in Lemma
15.14
. Let
X\in\Omega\setminus 2B
be given, and choose
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
0\leq\phi\leq 1
,
\phi\equiv 1
on
B\cap\Gamma
,

\supp\,\phi\subset E_{B}:=\{Y\in\Omega,\,\,\mathrm{dist}(Y,B\cap\Gamma)\geq(100K)^{-1}r\}
, and
|\nabla\phi|\leq 200K/r
.
We get that

(15.38)

u_{\phi}(X)\leq\frac{C}{r}\int_{E_{B}}|\nabla_{y}g(X,Y)|dm(Y)

by (
15.36
) and (
11.2
), and since
\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\leq u_{\phi}(X)

by the positivity of the harmonic measure,

(15.39)

\begin{split}\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)&\leq\frac{C}{r}\int_{E_{B}}|\nabla_{y}g(X,Y)|dm(Y).\end{split}

We cover
E_{B}

by a finitely overlapping collection of balls
(B_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
centered on
B\cap\Gamma
and of radius
(10K)^{-1}r
. Then

(15.40)

\begin{split}\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)&\leq\frac{C}{r}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\int_{B_{i}\cap\Omega}|\nabla_{y}g(X,Y)|dm(Y)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{m(B_{i}\cap\Omega)^{1/2}}{r}\left(\int_{B_{i}\cap\Omega}|\nabla_{y}g(X,Y)|^{2}dm(Y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{m(B_{i}\cap\Omega)^{1/2}}{r^{2}}\left(\int_{2B_{i}\cap\Omega}|g(X,Y)|^{2}dm(Y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

where we use successively the Cauchy-Schwarz
inequality and Cacciopoli’s inequality at the boundary (Lemma
11.15
);
the use of Cacciopoli’s inequality is indeed allowed
because
Y\to g(X,Y)
is a solution of
L_{T}u:=-\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A^{T}\nabla u
in
2B_{i}\cap\Omega
by Lemmas
14.78
and
14.60
(iii).
Observe that
Y\to g(X,Y)
is more generally a solution of
L_{T}u:=-\mathop{\operatorname{div}}A^{T}\nabla u

in each set

2KB_{i}\cap\Omega
, because the radius of
2KB_{i}
is less than
r/2
and hence
2KB_{i}\subset 2B\not\ni X
. As a consequence, Lemma
15.14
yields that

(15.41)

\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\lesssim\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{m(B_{i})}{r^{2}}\,g(X,X_{i}),

where
X_{i}
is a corkscrew point associated to the ball
B_{i}
. Hence

(15.42)

\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\lesssim\frac{m(B)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{0})

because of the finite overlapping of the
(B_{i})_{i}
, the Harnack inequality, and the fact that we can easily find Harnack chains of balls that link
X_{i}
to
X_{0}
and that avoids
X
. The bounds (
15.37
) and then (
15.29
) follow.

The proof of (
15.30
) can be treated in a similar manner, and we refer to

[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.11]

for additional ideas on the proof.

\Box

Lemma 15.43

(Doubling volume property for the harmonic measure)
.

Let
\alpha>1
, and take a ball
B:=B(x_{0},r)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered on
\Gamma
. One has

(15.44)

\omega^{X}(2B\cap\Gamma)\leq C_{\alpha}\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma)\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2\alpha B,

where
C_{\alpha}>0
depends only upon
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
,
C_{A}
, and
\alpha
.

Proof
.  The proof is the same as the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.102]
. Here are some ideas.

When
\alpha=2
, we use Lemma
15.28
, the doubling property (
H4
), the Harnack inequality, and the existence of Harnack chains of balls to write

(15.45)

\omega^{X}(2B\cap\Gamma)\lesssim\frac{m(2B)}{(2r)^{2}}g(X,X_{2})\lesssim\frac{m(B)}{r^{2}}g(X,X_{1})\lesssim\omega^{X}(B\cap\Gamma),

where
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are corkscrew points associated to respectively
(x_{0},r)
and
(x_{0},2r)
.

When
1<\alpha<2
, we cover
2B\cap\Gamma
by a collection of finitely overlapping balls
2B_{i}
of radius
2r_{\alpha}:=(\alpha-1)r
and centered on
B(x_{0},2r-\frac{3}{2}r_{\alpha})\cap\Gamma
. In this case, for any
X\in\Omega\setminus 2\alpha B

(15.46)

\omega^{X}(2B\cap\Gamma)\leq\sum_{i}\omega^{X}(2B_{i}\cap\Gamma)\lesssim\sum_{i}\omega^{X}(B_{i}\cap\Gamma)\lesssim\omega^{X}(B(x_{0},2r-\frac{1}{2}r_{\alpha})\cap\Gamma),

where the second estimate is due to (
15.45
). We repeat the argument a finite number of time (depending in
\alpha-1>0
) to get (
15.44
). The lemma follows.

\Box

Lemma 15.47

(Comparison principle for global solutions)
.

Let
B:=B(x_{0},r)
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
, and let

X_{0}\in\Omega
be a corkscrew point associated to
(x_{0},r)
.
Let
u,v\in W
be two non-negative, non identically zero,
solutions to
Lu=Lv=0
in
\Omega
such that

\Tr u=\Tr v=0
on
\Gamma\setminus B(x_{0},r)
.
Then

(15.48)

C^{-1}\frac{u(X_{0})}{v(X_{0})}\leq\frac{u(X)}{v(X)}\leq C\frac{u(X_{0})}{v(X_{0})}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B,

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
C_{1}
to
C_{6}
, and
C_{A}
.

Remark 15.49
.

We also have (
15.48
) for any
X\in\Omega\setminus B(x_{0},\alpha r)
, where
\alpha>1
. In this case, the constant
C
depends also on
\alpha
. We let the reader check that the proof below can be easily adapted to prove this too.

Proof
.  The proof is very similar to the one of
[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.14]
.
Let us recall the main steps and show the differences.

By symmetry of the roles of
u
and
v
, we only need to show the upper bound

(15.50)

\frac{u(X)}{v(X)}\leq C\frac{u(X_{0})}{v(X_{0})}\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B.

Notice also that thanks to the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
),
v(X)>0
on the whole
\Omega
, so we we don’t need to be careful when we divide by
v(X)
.

We introduce some notation for two boundary balls: set
\Gamma_{1}:=\Gamma\cap B
and
\Gamma_{2}:=\Gamma\cap\frac{15}{8}B
.
The proof of the lemma is composed of three steps :

(a)

we prove
the lower bound

(15.51)

v(X)\geq C^{-1}\omega^{X}(\Gamma_{1})v(X_{0})\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B;

(b)

we prove
the upper bound

(15.52)

u(X)\leq Cu(X_{0})\omega^{X}(\Gamma_{2})\ \ \ \text{ for }X\in\Omega\setminus 2B;

(c)

we
conclude by using the fact that the harmonic measure is doubling (Lemma
15.43
).

The proof of (
15.51
) is can be done exactly as in

[
DFM2
, Lemma 11.14]
. Let us quickly sketch it.
By the Harnack inequality (Lemma
11.35
), for all
X\in B(X_{0},\delta(X_{0})/2)
, we have
v(X)\gtrsim v(X_{0})
. Together with the upper bound (
14.64
), we get the existence of a constant
K_{1}
such that the function
